Title: What Counts as Attack Success? Evaluating Harmfulness in Red Teaming LLMs

  • Authors: Ruyuan Wan, Adriana Alvarado Garcia, Md Toufikuzzaman, Dongwon Lee, Ting-Hao `Kenneth' Huang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY, cs.HC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28038
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28038
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28038]] What Counts as Attack Success? Evaluating Harmfulness in Red Teaming LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28038)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Judging whether a model response is harmful requires contextual and normative choices, yet red-teaming studies often delegate this judgment to automated evaluators. We examine how harmfulness criteria are specified in red-teaming research and how they carry through to reported results. Reviewing 76 papers, we find that many adopt their criteria from prior datasets, classifiers, or judge prompts rather than defining harmfulness themselves. Applying evaluators from these papers to identical outputs, we find that they can reach different judgments on the same response, which can change conclusions when comparing models. These differences arise partly because evaluators draw the boundary of harm in different places: some count any response that does not refuse, while others count only output that could plausibly cause harm. We argue that red-teaming studies should state their harmfulness criteria and report how they chose and validated their evaluators to support more reliable measurement and interpretation.

Title: Distributionally-Verifiable Rewards for Human-Behavior Simulation

  • Authors: Ruoyun Ma, Shuang Liu, Peiye Liu, Ao Qu, Baichuan Mo
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28048
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28048
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28048]] Distributionally-Verifiable Rewards for Human-Behavior Simulation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28048)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to simulate survey respondents in social-science research. A useful simulator should predict individual responses, reproduce population response distributions, and capture demographic differences. Common training approaches struggle to balance these goals: exact-match rewards encourage the model to concentrate on the most frequent human response, which is contradictory to the goal of distributional alignment. Meanwhile, aggregate distribution matching can look good even when demographic information is ignored. To this end, we introduce Distributionally-Verifiable Rewards (DVR), a reinforcement-learning reward that uses demographic group distribution gaps to guide individual responses, designed by aligning policy generated frequencies with human response frequencies within each question-group margins. We evaluate the method using the OpinionQA dataset and find that comparing with accuracy-only RLVR, DVR preserves per-response accuracy while reducing JSD by 34.5% in-distribution and 14.6% on held-out questions, with higher DPC point estimates on both splits. Comparing KL-SFT with the demographics-blind marginal further shows that low JSD is insufficient to establish fidelity to population heterogeneity, highlighting that accuracy, distributional fit, and demographic structure should be evaluated together.

Title: Benchmarking and Adapting LLMs for English-Swedish Medical Question Answering

  • Authors: Seyed Alireza Molavi, Zhan Su, Stefan Byttner, Yan Hu, Peyman Sheikholharam Mashhadi, Prayag Tiwari
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28050
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28050
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28050]] Benchmarking and Adapting LLMs for English-Swedish Medical Question Answering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28050)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models do well on English medical exams, but less is known about Swedish. This matters most for small open models that hospitals can run locally. To reduce this gap, we first build an item-aligned English-Swedish benchmark from ten medical multiple-choice datasets; each Swedish item keeps the options and answer of its English source. A conservative refinement loop repairs the translations: open models from three families find errors, ground them in a Swedish medical term base, and keep a model-written edit only if blind judges vote for it. On this benchmark, small models lose much more accuracy in Swedish than large ones, especially when they answer without reasoning. Second, we ask whether English data and open teacher models can close this gap. We translate the training data, let teachers write reasoning traces, and fine-tune two small students on gold answers or traces in English, Swedish, or both. Training on reasoning traces that include Swedish closes the gap and lifts the 1.2B student from 23.5\% to 52.0\% in Swedish, slightly above a 20B open model in its fast mode, with a sixth of its memory. We release the benchmark, its edit history, and the code.

Title: Following the Error Through a Compressed Network:An Angular Measure for FFN Weight Compression in ViT-L/16

  • Authors: Paolo D'Alberto
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.IT, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28067
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28067
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28067]] Following the Error Through a Compressed Network:An Angular Measure for FFN Weight Compression in ViT-L/16(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28067)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We study the compression of the 48 feed-forward network (FFN) matrices of the Vision Transformer ViT-L/16 to 2--8 bits with four schemes: a per-token grid, a fixed Gaussian Lloyd--Max table, a table fitted to the matrix, and a random-sign Hadamard rotation followed by the fixed table. We evaluate on 50,000 images. At 4 bits the top-1 accuracy of the four schemes lies within 0.8 points of the float32 model, while the agreement with its predictions ranges from 98.4\% to 94.4\%. We use the angular error between the compressed and the float32 residual stream, followed through the blocks, as a portable measure that ranks the schemes as the final classification does and shows where the ranking is built: with rotation the angle grows close to the square root of the number of blocks, the signature of independently accumulating errors, while the other schemes are amplified in the later blocks. The same measure shows that a scheme that is better on one block in isolation can be worse in the whole network. To address this inconsistency we introduce an angular measure of the spectrum of the output of a block, which in isolation orders the schemes as the whole network does where they diverge most. We support these observations with real samples and with statistically generated ones. The scope is one model, the FFN weights only, and float evaluation of compressed weights; no speed-up is claimed. The error measures and the sample generation, however, can be applied to other models and precisions.

Title: Fairness Evaluation for Diffusion and Flow Models Should Account for the Generation Path: Implications for Clinical Imaging

  • Authors: Dharini Raghavan, Amritpal Singh, Anant Madabhushi
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28077
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28077
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28077]] Fairness Evaluation for Diffusion and Flow Models Should Account for the Generation Path: Implications for Clinical Imaging(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28077)
  • Keywords: fair, diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Medical image generation models are often evaluated using images produced by a single sampling procedure. However, diffusion and flow models can use different numbers of sampling steps, sampling schedules, or numerical solvers without changing their trained parameters. These changes may affect the generated images and the clinical measurements derived from them. We argue that fairness results obtained under one sampling procedure should not be assumed to hold under another, particularly when changes in sampling affect clinical task errors differently across patient groups. Building on previous work on medical image quality, accelerated sampling, generation dynamics, and generative model fairness, we examine why the sampling procedure should be considered part of the evaluated system. We define subgroup differences in clinical task error across sampling procedures and provide a mathematical example showing that two groups can have identical outputs under the exact dynamics and a reference sampler but different errors under an alternative sampler. We also extend this result to stochastic generation under shared noise. An exploratory experiment with an age-conditioned image generation model illustrates how changing the number and timing of sampling steps can alter image-derived measurements, although these differences do not establish clinical unfairness. We therefore argue that fairness evaluation should assess the sampling procedure used in practice and examine intermediate generation states when they help explain differences in clinically relevant errors across patient groups. Analysis code is available here this https URL

Title: Adversarial Attacks Expose Path-Dependent Failures in Discrete Medical Image Tokenizers

  • Authors: Ahmed Loughzali, Akash Yallamati, Aadiv Reki, Aslan Shaukat Vaida, Jeyashree Krishnan
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28078
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28078
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28078]] Adversarial Attacks Expose Path-Dependent Failures in Discrete Medical Image Tokenizers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28078)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust, generative
  • Abstract: Discrete image tokenizers convert medical images into token sequences that downstream models either decode into an image for a classifier (the reconstruction path), classify directly (the token path), or feed to a generative multimodal model. We ask whether imperceptible input perturbations can break these systems through the tokenizer alone. We evaluate four tokenizers on three imaging modalities and extend the strongest attack to a deployed generative model. Our central finding is path-dependence: the same tokenizer fails in opposite ways depending on how its output is used. A supervised attack collapses diagnostic accuracy on the reconstruction path, with ROC AUC falling to $\leq 0.013$ at $\epsilon=2/255$ on all four tokenizers, while sparing the token path. An unsupervised, label-free attack does the reverse; its damage concentrates in the multi-codebook tokenizers, worst in MedITok, whose token-path AUC falls from 0.919 to 0.239. The same label-free attack transfers to LLaVA-Med, a frozen medical vision-language model built on MedITok, making it report proliferative diabetic retinopathy in a healthy retina or call a malignant lesion benign, without access to the question, answer, or language-model gradients. Harm is driven by how far the attack moves the tokenizer's internal representation, not by how many tokens change: on the learned-codebook tokenizers, a control attack that changes nearly all tokens but moves the representation less leaves every path and answer intact. Domain-specific pretraining does not provide robustness: MedITok has the best clean accuracy yet is the most fragile, more so than UniTok, its general-domain counterpart. None of six candidate defenses repairs every failure mode, and several fail once the attacker optimizes against the defense directly. Medical tokenizer robustness must therefore be evaluated per downstream path, against an adaptive adversary.

Title: Multimodal Violence Understanding and Temporal Retrieval in Movies with Adaptive Audio-Visual Fusion

  • Authors: Jiaqian Li, Rohitash Chandra
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28080
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28080
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28080]] Multimodal Violence Understanding and Temporal Retrieval in Movies with Adaptive Audio-Visual Fusion(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28080)
  • Keywords: protect, transformer
  • Abstract: Violence in films and streaming media remains a concern for audience protection and creates substantial workload for manual content classification. Existing violence-detection methods often rely on limited modalities or short video clips, making it difficult to capture complementary evidence and localise violent events in long, untrimmed videos. In this study, we develop a framework for multimodal video understanding that classifies violence severity, predicts multiple violence types, and locates violent events in time. We combine temporally aligned visual and audio features extracted using pretrained encoders through attention-based adaptive fusion, followed by Transformer-based temporal modelling. Our framework supports three complementary tasks: video-level violence-severity classification, multi-label violence-type prediction, and temporal localisation of violent events. We evaluate the framework on the XD-Violence benchmark and examine its cross-domain performance using AIRTLab. On XD-Violence, our multimodal model achieves 93.27\% severity-classification accuracy and a micro-F1 score of 88.60\% for multi-label violence-type prediction, outperforming the corresponding unimodal configurations. We achieve a mean temporal intersection-over-union (tIoU) of 0.5075 for temporal localisation. Our cross-domain experiments reveal performance degradation under different recording conditions, while our movie case studies demonstrate the framework's potential to retrieve potentially violent scenes for further review. Our results highlight the practical value of multimodal intelligent systems in supporting human reviewers with the identification, classification, and temporal retrieval of violent content in films and streaming media.

Title: BehaviorICL: Task-Conditioned Decoder States for Visual In-Context Learning

  • Authors: Chenyang Wang, Jiamo Hu, Chengrui Wu, Lingyue Chen, Jianguo Chen, Xun Xu, Xulei Yang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28081
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28081
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28081]] BehaviorICL: Task-Conditioned Decoder States for Visual In-Context Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28081)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: The usefulness of an in-context demonstration for a vision-language model (VLM) depends on both the query image and the task posed about it. We study whether the frozen target VLM's own decoder states, read before answer generation, can select such demonstrations. BehaviorICL represents each image and task by a task-conditioned processing signature, the hidden states at the final input position across all decoder layers. Its training-free variant, BehaviorICL-Raw, averages cosine similarities between corresponding layer states and passes the retrieved examples with their reference answers to the same VLM. When the same NICO++ images are asked about their object or their context, Raw retrieves largely different demonstrations for the two questions and outperforms an image retriever given task-matched demonstrations on both VLMs. Across two VLMs and eight classification and visual question answering datasets, Raw gives the highest average accuracy among training-free retrievers on both VLMs and exceeds RICES with a CLIP ViT-L/14 encoder by 3.83 points on Qwen and 1.85 on InternVL. A lightweight shared projection, BehaviorICL-Proj, brings further gains on classification. Analyses of extraction text, decoder depth and bank answers help explain these gains, and attention analysis describes how the task text shifts attention at the extraction position toward the classified object. These results support the target VLM's internal states as a practical basis for visual in-context learning.

Title: ReSUL: Learning Regional Sensor Utility for Robust Multisensor Semantic Segmentation

  • Authors: Yiwen Wang, Jiahao Qin
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28084
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28084
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28084]] ReSUL: Learning Regional Sensor Utility for Robust Multisensor Semantic Segmentation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28084)
  • Keywords: robust, segmentation
  • Abstract: A corrupted sensor observation is not necessarily useless: misalignment can place informative scene structure at the wrong location. Robust multisensor segmentation must determine where to retain this evidence, where to exclude it, and where repair can recover its value. We introduce Regional Sensor Utility Learning (ReSUL), which learns these choices through their effect on segmentation risk. ReSUL compares full-sensor predictions, sensor-exclusion paths, and a re-registration path for displaced thermal inputs. A router predicts each path's signed reduction in category-balanced Brier risk relative to a fixed reference, then uses these estimates to allocate spatial mixture weights. This supervision connects spatial allocation to measurable task benefit without requiring fault labels at inference. MFNet experiments compare project-trained models under eleven clean and disturbed input conditions. ReSUL ranks first in 10 of 11 conditions in both ImageNet-1k and ImageNet-22k comparisons. Under severe thermal displacement, it reaches 54.58% and 55.63% mIoU, exceeding the strongest competing method in each group by 7.19 and 5.63 percentage points. Fixed-bank MFNet ablations show that utility supervision improves all eleven condition means over task-only routing. These results support learning how to use disturbed observations through their regional prediction benefit. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: UbiEnlight: User-Perception-Aware Real-Time Low-Light Video Enhancement on Mobile Devices and Smartglasses

  • Authors: Minfan Wang, Sicong Liu, Teng Li, Zimu Zhou, Sixun He, Bin Guo, Junzhao Du, Zhiwen Yu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28085
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28085
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28085]] UbiEnlight: User-Perception-Aware Real-Time Low-Light Video Enhancement on Mobile Devices and Smartglasses(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28085)
  • Keywords: robust, diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Low-light video capture is now common on smartphones and wearables, yet dynamic illumination coupled with mobile/werable user motion causes noise, blur, and flicker that make videos hard to perceive and use, especially for users with night blindness and near-eye displays. Prior work enhances frames with post-hoc temporal constraints or optimizes videos directly; however,low-light and motion degradations are spatiotemporally coupled, so post-hoc consistency after per-frame restoration often leaves flicker and motion artifacts. We present UbiEnlight, the first diffusion-transformer-based on-device system for real-time, temporally stable low-light video enhancement. UbiEnlight builds on two insights: (1) illumination and structure separate more robustly in the frequency domain, enabling a spectrum-guided diffusion transformer that injects Fourier amplitude/phase priors to correct illumination while anchoring structure without optical flow; and (2) user perceptual tolerance varies by context, motivating a perception-aware controller that adapts sampling depth, attention reuse, and frame caching at runtime. We implement UbiEnlight on smartphones and smartglasses, and evaluate it against state-of-the-art baselines. UbiEnlight improves temporal stability by up to 54.76% under dynamic/extreme conditions while sustaining real-time on-device performance.

Title: Efficient Memory-Constrained Video Object Segmentation via Subspace Optimization

  • Authors: Hai-Anh Le, Le-Trung Nguyen, Enzo Tartaglione

  • Abstract: Memory-based architectures for video object segmentation (VOS) have achieved remarkable performance by maintaining dense feature representations from past frames in a streaming Memory Bank. However, storing these features over time incurs substantial memory overhead and computational cost. In this work, we identify significant temporal redundancy in memory representations, where consecutive frames exhibit highly overlapping low-rank subspaces that remain stable over time. Building on this observation, we propose Memory Subspace Iteration (MSI), a training-free, model-agnostic plugin that exploits temporal subspace stability to efficiently compress streaming memory features. Title: ORDO: Fine-Grained Motion Understanding in Video LLMs via Order-Dependent Supervision

  • Authors: Jiaye Luan, Chaoning Zhang, Pengcheng Zheng, Dongshen Han, Sheng Zheng, Ping Guo, Caiyan Qin, Yang Du, Yang Yang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28097
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28097
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28097]] ORDO: Fine-Grained Motion Understanding in Video LLMs via Order-Dependent Supervision(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28097)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Video large language models (video LLMs) answer a wide range of questions about real videos, yet they remain weak at fine-grained motion such as whether an object speeds up or slows down. Existing methods train these models on synthetic motion clips, on order pretext tasks such as reordering shuffled clips, or with rewards that compare ordered and perturbed frames. None of them requires that the label be unrecoverable from the frames once their order is discarded, so a model can fit such data from static cues without learning to use frame order. We propose ORDO, a framework that builds this requirement into the training data. We define order-necessary supervision as data in which the unordered set of frames has the same distribution under competing labels, and we realise it for motion attributes with three constructions, time-reversal pairing, matched spatial extent and uniform interval coverage. A procedural curriculum of six motion primitives instantiates these constructions, and low-rank adapters of the language model are trained on it with an answer-level objective and self-distillation replay on real videos. With 4,000 synthetic clips, ORDO raises the mean accuracy over four motion benchmarks by +7.6, +4.9 and +5.1 points on Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3-VL-8B and Molmo2-8B, while the same training with shuffled frames changes it by less than one point. Code and generator will be made publicly available.

Title: Scale-Aware Reference Attention for Mannequin-to-Model Gown Generation

  • Authors: Guang Yang, Fengchen Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28101
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28101
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28101]] Scale-Aware Reference Attention for Mannequin-to-Model Gown Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28101)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Formal gowns are sold online with photos of models wearing them, and buyers zoom in to inspect sequins, lace and beading. Image editors can turn a photo of a gown on a mannequin into a convincing photo of a person wearing it, but they receive every reference photo without any notion of its magnification, and the fine detail of the gown is lost when the output is viewed up close. We propose scale-aware reference attention (SARA), which tells an open diffusion transformer the zoom level of the output and of every reference photo, biases the attention of the output toward references of a similar zoom level, uses macro photos of the fabric as references, and is trained on a mix of whole-garment and zoomed targets. To train and evaluate it, we introduce Kiss3000, a dataset of 915 high-resolution mannequin photos and 2274 on-model photos of formal gowns with cross-domain pairs and splits by style, and zoom-crop evaluation, which compares garment crops at full, half and local scale without pixel alignment and judges local fidelity with two vision-language models gated on control crops. With this protocol, Nano Banana Pro at 4K, the highest-resolution commercial editor we test, loses or redraws local detail on 14 of 20 test gowns. At 1536x2048 pixels, both judges prefer SARA over a LoRA fine-tuning baseline on 18 test items and the baseline on 6 (p=0.023), and over the base model on 26 against 4 (p=5.9e-5), while SARA keeps the gown design as often as the baseline.

Title: Cyclic Discrete Diffusion: A Novel Multi-Class Segmentation Refinement Technique

  • Authors: Libo Chen, Souvik Ghosh, Vinay P. Namboodiri
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28107
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28107
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28107]] Cyclic Discrete Diffusion: A Novel Multi-Class Segmentation Refinement Technique(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28107)
  • Keywords: diffusion, segmentation
  • Abstract: We cast image segmentation refinement as a discrete diffusion process. To solve this task we introduce Cyclic Discrete Diffusion (CDD), a novel forward-reverse formulation with a cyclic label topology that maps a discrete label set to the uniform distribution and back. Instead of injecting Gaussian noise as is done for continuous diffusion, CDD evolves segmentation masks through label jumps: at each step, every pixel either remains in its current class or jumps to the next label with a fixed rate. This jump-or-stay mechanism defines a finite-state Markov process with bounded forward and reverse steps, featuring analytically consistent and tractable reverse dynamics. CDD operates natively in a multi-class label space, enabling joint refinement of all classes in a single pass without requiring class-wise decomposition, as commonly needed in prior binary refinement approaches. The proposed formulation admits a finite and controllable number of refinement steps, for which we analyze the convergence properties through spectral gap analysis. We evaluate CDD across diverse segmentation tasks, including multi-class semantic, instance-level, and binary segmentation refinement. Our method consistently improves both region accuracy and boundary quality across multiple backbones and datasets.

Title: ROSE-SAM3: Response-Oriented Prompt Selection for Open-Vocabulary Remote Sensing Segmentation

  • Authors: Yanghui Song, Chengfu Yang, Yongpeng Wen, Zhanxuan Hu, Wei Lu, Qi Ming, Nanqing Liu, Heng-Chao Li
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28112
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28112
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28112]] ROSE-SAM3: Response-Oriented Prompt Selection for Open-Vocabulary Remote Sensing Segmentation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28112)
  • Keywords: protect, robust, segmentation
  • Abstract: In open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) of remote-sensing imagery, class-name prompts can miss objects with varied appearances, while richer descriptions can activate competing classes. We propose Response-Oriented Prompt Selection for SAM 3 (ROSE-SAM3), a training-free framework that selects descriptions using frozen SAM 3 responses on unlabeled target-domain images. We screen candidate descriptions for coverage beyond the native prompts while protecting reliable native class responses, retaining candidates that pass these checks on at least four of five image folds. An offline directed acyclic graph (DAG) search then evaluates candidate combinations and builds a compact PromptBank for each class. Each bank retains the native prompts and adds selected descriptions, which we call residual prompts. At inference, the bank is fixed, and Semantic-Instance Cooperative Head (SICH) uses instance evidence to refine semantic responses before assigning pixel labels. We also introduce RS-OVSS-C to evaluate robustness under six image corruption families across eight remote-sensing datasets, preserving their class vocabularies and reference labels. ROSE-SAM3 achieves 57.3% mean mIoU across eight clean benchmarks and 54.7% on RS-OVSS-C, achieving state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods. The code is publicly available at this https URL.

Title: Benchmarking Foundation Models for Bone Scaffold Pore Detection: A Comparative Analysis of Few-Shot Performance

  • Authors: Tri Tin Vo, Vu Thien An Ngo, Nhi Kieu, Cuong Hung Luu, Giang V. H. Phan, Kien Nguyen, Clinton Fookes
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28113
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28113
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28113]] Benchmarking Foundation Models for Bone Scaffold Pore Detection: A Comparative Analysis of Few-Shot Performance(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28113)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Evaluating the mechanical properties of bone scaffolds conventionally requires destructive testing or costly Finite Element Analysis, while deep learning alternatives are constrained by the scarcity of labeled biomaterial imaging data. This study introduces the first expert-annotated, pixel-dense dataset of biopolymer bone-scaffold SEM images, spanning five magnification series, and uses it to benchmark two state-of-the-art pre-trained foundation models under a 12-shot fine-tuning regime. SAM3, an instance segmenter, and YOLOv26, a bounding-box detector, feed the predicted pore geometry to regression heads that estimate Young's modulus and compressive yield strength. Our central finding is that both models fail by pore density rather than magnification: YOLOv26's predicted pore counts saturate and porosity estimation collapses on the most crowded frames, whereas SAM3 over-fragments sparse pore fields and merges adjacent pores in dense ones, producing eight catastrophic failures against three for YOLOv26. We conclude that model selection should be driven by pore density rather than SEM magnification alone.

Title: NOVA: Normalization-Aware Output-Guided Attention Acceleration for Video Diffusion Transformers

  • Authors: Wenxuan Miao, Haosong Liu, Aiyue Chen, Yiwu Yao, Zihan Liu, Anbang Wu, Yiming Gan, Jingwen Leng, Minyi Guo, Yu Feng
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28115
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28115
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28115]] NOVA: Normalization-Aware Output-Guided Attention Acceleration for Video Diffusion Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28115)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Video diffusion transformers (vDiTs) have become the main backbone for video generation. The quadratic cost of self-attention limits their efficiency at high resolutions and long durations. Existing sparse attention methods often rely on preserving high attention weights. However, these criteria do not fully capture the fidelity of the final value-weighted output, whose error depends jointly on changes to the attention numerator and the softmax denominator. We propose NOVA, a coarse-to-fine sparse attention framework that first approximates the full normalized output using size-weighted key/value centroids. Building on this estimate, we construct a residual proxy that accounts for coupled numerator-denominator errors by centering value contributions around the estimated output. This proxy guides the selection of query-key blocks for refinement under a predefined block budget for each query cluster. Selected blocks are evaluated through exact token-level computation, while the remaining blocks contribute through their centroids under a shared softmax normalization. On five vDiTs, NOVA achieves the best PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS among sparse attention methods, improving PSNR by up to 1.55\,dB over the strongest baseline, while delivering up to 1.82$\times$ end-to-end speedup on an H100 GPU.

Title: RALC: Risk-Controlled Local Adoption of Segmentation Updates with Few Calibration Images

  • Authors: Shize Yin
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28122
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28122
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28122]] RALC: Risk-Controlled Local Adoption of Segmentation Updates with Few Calibration Images(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28122)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Fine-tuning a road-scene segmenter for rain and nighttime driving can introduce errors on normal daytime images. With few labeled normal images for calibration, a whole-image risk check may reject changes that would pass if adopted locally. Risk-Controlled Adoption of Label Changes (RALC) selects local changes from a fixed model update, using confidence differences and an image score to form nested candidates. On each normal calibration image, RALC measures how many more errors each candidate makes than the original model, assigning zero loss when errors stay the same or fall. At test time, each replacement can add at most one error, so the replacement count bounds the net increase without test labels. RALC combines this candidate-dependent bound with recorded calibration losses to select the largest set that passes the risk check. If the models and adoption rules are fixed before calibration and normal calibration and test images are exchangeable, the expected per-image loss stays within budget. On held-out BDD100K, we evaluated three weight-interpolated updates with 10 additional normal images per calibration draw. At a common fixed budget, adverse-scene mIoU was 1.89--3.19 percentage points higher with RALC than with whole-image adoption, while measured mean normal loss was also higher. After matching this loss post hoc at 1,000 pixels per image, RALC still led by 1.135--2.428 points. Selecting changes in $8\times8$-pixel cells recovered 93--98% of this advantage. With 250 calibration images at the original budget, both rules adopted all three updates in full.

  • Abstract: In-context segmentation (ICS) segments a target image given a few annotated reference images. We study the multiclass, multi-reference setting and ask how far a simple joint decision rule can go. We introduce DINO-KNN, which stores every labeled reference patch of a frozen DINOv3 encoder in a single memory and labels each target patch by a distance-weighted vote of its k nearest neighbors.   • Abstract: Anticipating the future behavior of surrounding objects is key for the safe and efficient navigation of autonomous vehicles. Title: Beyond Output Suppression: Mitigating Privacy Risks in LVLMs via Visual Intrinsic Privacy Erasure

  • Authors: Xiangkui Cao, Jie Zhang, Meina Kan, Shiguang Shan, Xilin Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28135
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28135
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28135]] Beyond Output Suppression: Mitigating Privacy Risks in LVLMs via Visual Intrinsic Privacy Erasure(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28135)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect, extraction
  • Abstract: Large vision-language models can extract personally identifiable information from images, making privacy protection essential for their deployment in perception-oriented tasks. Existing targeted fine-tuning and model-editing methods usually suppress sensitive answers by aligning the model toward safe responses. In this paper, we show that such protection can be illusory: although the fine-tuned model refuses to disclose private information under normal decoding, privacy leakage still occurs in sentence completion scenarios. This indicates that existing algorithms only change the model's output distribution, but the model still retains the ability to perceive private information. Private information therefore still remains inside the model. To address this problem, we propose Visual Intrinsic Privacy Erasure (VIPE), which targets privacy-related visual representations to weaken the model's ability to perceive sensitive information. VIPE constructs paired privacy-sensitive and privacy-masked images, uses their internal feature residuals to identify privacy-related visual components, and suppresses these components before they are perceived by the model. To preserve benign visual representations, the erasure objective projects feature differences onto a relaxed benign subspace and adaptively controls the erasure strength. VIPE compels the model to ignore the extraction of sensitive information in the visual representation space, thereby blocking the source of privacy leakage more fundamentally rather than merely altering the model's output distribution. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL and LLaVA-OneVision show that VIPE substantially alleviates prefix-triggered privacy leakage while maintaining competitive utility on general visual-language benchmarks.

Title: Feedback Shortcut: Speculative Decoding for Latent Feedback Transformers

  • Authors: Benhao Huang, Xuezhe Ma, Hector Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28227
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28227
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28227]] Feedback Shortcut: Speculative Decoding for Latent Feedback Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28227)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Feeding each position's top-layer hidden state back into the next position's input improves Transformer performance. However, this feedback breaks speculative decoding, since verifying a drafted token requires the top-layer state from its preceding position, which forces the draft to rely on an approximate stand-in. Training the model to adapt to this stand-in can lower the exact-decoding performance ceiling, while using it at inference time further introduces approximation error. We propose Feedback Shortcut to address both bottlenecks. First, state alignment trains a lightweight projection from multi-token prediction head states to stand-ins, leaving base model training untouched. Second, pipelined correction re-evaluates each draft using its predecessor's newly computed state, a much closer proxy to true feedback. Running this correction inside the verification pass of subsequent drafts improves accuracy with zero extra calls. Across 135M and 780M scales, Feedback Shortcut stays within $10^{-4}$ bits per byte of the exact-decoding ceiling on Paloma and accelerates decoding by 2.4-2.8x with about 2% more training compute than the latent feedback Transformer. At large batch sizes, skipping correction still preserves Transformer speed and over 80% of latent feedback's gains.

  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28234
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28234

  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: We introduce a framework for learning distributions supported on manifolds based on estimating the nearest-point projection onto the data support. We also introduce a direct memorization metric to verify that the learned projection generates new samples rather than reproducing the training set.

However, their applicability to viscoplastic systems and their efficacy in recovering realistic flows remain poorly understood. In this work, we present the first systematic investigation of RheOFormer applied to steady Bingham channel flow. By isolating the problem to a simple geometry and constitutive model, we examine how dataset structure, numerical value range, and input representation govern the model's ability to interpolate and extrapolate within the parameter space. The data regimes in which RheOFormer remains accurate and those in which errors emerge are identified, particularly for plug-dominated flows and for conditions outside the velocity scales or flow structures represented during training. Upon identifying these failure modes, targeted enhancements to the input representation and loss formulation are introduced to better predict low-gradient plug regions. Logarithmic velocity representations and gradient-aware loss formulations substantially improve accuracy in challenging regimes where the baseline formulation fails. In addition, extrapolation errors are reduced by augmenting the training set with only a few strategically selected cases spanning the missing physical regimes. Finally, a single partially developed snapshot suffices for an accurate steady-state prediction, and the model outperforms simply copying its input throughout flow development. These results highlight the importance of appropriate field representation and parameter-space coverage for data-efficient neural-operator prediction of viscoplastic flows.

Title: SONATI: Spatio-temporal ocean neighbourhood asymmetric transformer for imputation of sparse, tabular data with uncertainty

  • Authors: Yvonne Jenniges, Boris Peter Koch, Maike Sonnewald, Sebastian Maneth
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28270
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28270

  • Abstract: Missing value imputation is often a prerequisite for downstream analyses. We present the Spatio-temporal Ocean Neighbourhood Asymmetric Transformer for Imputation (SONATI), a masked, factorised time-space transformer operating on pre-computed, local time and space neighbourhoods, which reduces computational complexity compared to global attention. The results showed that SONATI outperformed classical and transformer-based methods by at least 12.5% in terms of root mean squared error (RMSE) on a large observational oceanographic dataset with key biogeochemical and physical parameters in the North Atlantic (potential temperature, salinity, oxygen, nitrate, silicate and phosphate). Attention type and masking strategy strongly affected imputation accuracy and smoothness of the produced oceanographic fields. Best results were achieved by factorised time-space attention and combined geographic and per-sample masking. The combined masking strategy reduced the imputation error by 14.3% compared to per-sample masking only. Uncertainties were moderately well calibrated (miscalibration area at observed locations $0.074\pm0.012$) and moderately correlated with the imputation error.

  • Abstract: Non-contact measurement of vital signs such as heart rate (HR) is valuable for patient monitoring and rescue operations.   • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28286]] Scalable Beckmann Maps Improve One-step Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28286)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Beckmann Transport Models (BTM) learn one-step generative maps end-to-end from data, without a teacher, a distillation stage, or pretrained features.   • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.NE

  • Keywords: attack, membership infer
  • Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) signals contain sensitive, subject-specific information, making models trained on them potential targets for membership inference attacks (MIAs). Although MIAs have been studied extensively in other domains, their applications to EEG models are relatively scarce, and the factors driving the vulnerability of EEG models to MIAs are not adequately understood. In this paper, we present a systematic empirical analysis of MIAs on EEG models using three datasets, seven model architectures, four attack methods, and two metrics. Results show that poor model generalization (large train-test loss and accuracy gap) is the strongest indicator of MIA vulnerability. Longer training and larger models also increase vulnerability. Moreover, no particular scalp region, frequency band, inter-channel correlation pattern, or temporal versus spectral representation of EEG data consistently explains MIA vulnerability across datasets. Finally, subject identification and leave-one-subject-out experiments indicate that models relying more heavily on subject-specific representations are more vulnerable to MIAs. Overall, our findings identify the key drivers of membership leakage in EEG models and can provide guidance for developing MIA-resilient EEG systems.

Title: Cross-Site Transformers Are Robust Heart Rate Estimators

  • Authors: Mason Kadem, Rong Zheng
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28366

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Single-site heart-rate estimation from photoplethysmography (PPG) degrades sharply under real-world motion. We propose the Cross-Site Transformer, which introduces a hemodynamically informed inductive bias by restricting multi-sensor fusion to cross-attention between PPG sites. Ablating the chest-to-wrist pathway doubles error, whereas the reverse costs far less. Encoding hemodynamic structure into attention routing thus improves real-world sensing robustness and enables rigorous auditing of multi-sensor fusion.

Title: Language models compress human preference geometry while political structure remains prompt-sensitive

  • Authors: Adolfo Ignacio Fuentes-Jofre, Cristian Candia
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28383
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28383

  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to construct synthetic publics. We tested this using matched pairwise policy choices from 1,991 Chilean, 843 French, and 434 Brazilian adults replayed through three open-weight model families. These results separate political legibility from population fidelity: language models can recover salient political divisions while failing to preserve the breadth of human preference variation. Synthetic-public fidelity is therefore a property of the checkpoint, prompt, and setting, not of a checkpoint alone, and must be evaluated separately at individual, population, and political-structure levels.

Title: Targeted In-Context Poisoning of Tabular Foundation Models: An Empirical Study

  • Authors: Adem Alparslan
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28396
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28396
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28396]] Targeted In-Context Poisoning of Tabular Foundation Models: An Empirical Study(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28396)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) predict the label of a query row conditioned on a context of labelled rows, without training on them. Drawn from operational data, the context becomes an attack surface. We study targeted in-context poisoning in its simplest form: an attacker with write access to the context's source places exact copies of a target row there with the wrong label. On 30 OpenML datasets for binary classification, one such poison row flips on average 53% (TabICL) and 65% (TabPFN) of the predictions correct without attack, and three poison rows flip almost all. The same copy with the true label flips almost none, and performance on the remaining rows hardly changes. Predictions flip by two routes: in TabICL, the target row's embedding rotates towards the mean of the wrong class; in TabPFN, the poison row pulls genuine rows of the wrong class along. The TFMs thus infer soundly, but from forged evidence. Predictions of trained models flip as well: almost always for a random forest, for CatBoost as often as for the TFMs, hardly for a logistic regression. New with TFMs is not the kind of vulnerability but the timing: the data take effect at the moment of prediction, not through training, and a poison row in a live context can be deleted afterwards. Three established requirements must therefore cover the context: access control for its source, traceability per prediction, which can also deter, and validation at prediction time. A detection mechanism shows how such validation can work.

Title: Plan Big, Paint Small: Semantic-Aware Computation Allocation for Efficient Autoregressive Video Generation

  • Authors: Yixuan Luo, Yanjing Li

  • Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is commonly adopted as one of the components in post-training of large language models (LLMs), but in multi-turn agentic tasks we find that the student model increases their reasoning lengths after training. Title: When a Permitted Action Completes a Forbidden Workflow: Designing Consequence-Oriented Approval for LLM Agents

  • Authors: S M Asif Hossain, Ruksat Khan Shayoni, Md Kishor Morol, M F Mridha
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28454
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28454
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28454]] When a Permitted Action Completes a Forbidden Workflow: Designing Consequence-Oriented Approval for LLM Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28454)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Tool-using language-model agents increasingly ask people to approve consequential actions, yet an approval prompt shows only the next step. A request to send a file to an approved recipient can look harmless even when that file was assembled, several steps earlier, from data the user said must never leave. We call this a cumulative authorization failure and argue that it is an interaction design problem as much as a security problem. The evidence a reviewer needs is spread across the trajectory, and listing it in time order leaves the reviewer to reconstruct the causal chain. We present the Cumulative Consent Ledger (CCL), an approval interface built on four design principles that restate the user's intent, bind the review to the exact action, compose the cumulative consequence, and keep every claim checkable. A trusted monitor that tracks artifact lineage supplies the evidence. In a between-subjects study with 80 participants and 960 approval decisions, CCL was associated with fewer unsafe approvals than an evidence-matched chronological log (24.2% vs. 32.5%), higher comprehension, and faster decisions, although the adjusted difference in unsafe approvals remained uncertain (-7.9 points, 95% CrI [-19.7, 3.8]). In a system evaluation, the monitor detected 89.2% of 120 violating checkpoints and, used as an execution gate, reduced executed unsafe effects from 43.3% to 5.0% for one agent at a small cost to benign completion. We derive implications for designing consent around consequences rather than individual calls.

  • Abstract: We study stochastic convex optimization with heavy-tailed stochastic gradients under $(\varepsilon, \delta)$-user-level differential privacy, which protects each user's entire collection of samples. This allows for unbounded, heavy-tailed stochastic gradient distributions and can lead to sharper excess risk bounds. (2022) and Zhao et al. (2024). Finally, for mean estimation under the directional-moment assumption of Agarwal et al. Title: PAMS: Preconditioned Activation-Metric Subspace for Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-tuning

  • Authors: Pradeep Kumar Ragu Chanthar, Saksham Sahai Srivastava, Jaewoo Lee
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28476
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28476
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28476]] PAMS: Preconditioned Activation-Metric Subspace for Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-tuning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28476)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization provides a computationally efficient approach to fine-tuning large language models (LLMs), but conventional methods typically rely on isotropic perturbations that ignore the anisotropic structure of neural-network parameter spaces. We introduce Preconditioned Activation-Metric Subspace (PAMS), a ZO optimization framework that uses forward-pass activation statistics to construct structured, layer-wise perturbation geometries without computing gradients or forming dense curvature matrices. PAMS tracks a low-rank approximation of the dominant activation subspace and its associated spectrum online, and uses the spectrum to softly reweight perturbation variance while preserving exploration in directions outside the retained subspace. A trace normalization separates the perturbation geometry from its overall scale, enabling consistent perturbation magnitudes across layers. We show theoretically that the resulting perturbation covariance induces right-side preconditioning of the gradient of the corresponding Gaussian-smoothed objective. We further characterize the estimator's smoothing bias and second moment and derive a nonconvex convergence guarantee that captures the effects of the perturbation geometry and smoothing radius. The proposed low-rank tracking procedure requires $\mathcal{O}(n_l r)$ persistent memory for a layer with input dimension nl and retained rank r, avoiding the $\mathcal{O}(n_l^2)$ memory required by a dense activation covariance. Experiments on transformer-based language-model fine-tuning tasks across GLUE and SuperGLUE demonstrate that PAMS improves the effectiveness and stability of zeroth-order optimization while retaining its gradient-free and objective-evaluation-based advantages.

  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28491
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28491
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28491]] SoK: Splitting the Trigger or Adding Attackers? Disentangling Distributed Backdoor Attacks in Federated Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28491)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, steal, federate
  • Abstract: Distributed backdoor attacks split a trigger among several federated learning clients, so that the backdoor appears only in the aggregated model. Since DBA, a line of work has reported that such attacks are stronger, more persistent, and stealthier than a single attacker with the full trigger. We systematize this literature and test this belief. We code nineteen primary studies and fifteen papers that evaluate against DBA. The reported advantage is rarely measured against a complete trigger delivered by the same attackers, and none of the fifteen evaluations tests whether individual pieces remain effective. We then run 875 paired federated runs that separate adding attackers from splitting the trigger. In our image experiments, adding attackers raised attack success in every seed of all 30 cells where we measured it, by 7.5 to 83.4 percentage points on average. Splitting the trigger among the same attackers had a smaller effect, and its sign depended on the piece layout, the allocation, and the attackers' local training. With DBA's compact layout, splitting lowered attack success on CIFAR-10. A larger penalty on small CNNs vanished once the attackers trained longer, and an FCBA-style allocation gained a few points. Widely separated pieces helped on CIFAR-10, its grayscale version, and GTSRB. No model required all pieces. Neural Cleanse suppressed single pieces together with the full trigger, but split backdoors with separated pieces survived it better than complete ones. We derive reporting requirements for attack and defense evaluations.

Title: Analyzing Atypical Local Features in Museum Artifacts using Generative Image Inpainting

  • Authors: Maho Kinoshita, Wakana Kuwata, Ryota Mibayashi, Hiroaki Ohshima

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28549
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28549

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: This study proposes a method for analyzing local visual features in museum artifacts that may appear atypical or unexpected. We use the appearance distribution learned by an image generation model as a reference for what is generally expected within an artifact category. For each target region, we exclude images from that region and fine-tune a generative model using only images from the remaining regions. For each category, we select several target regions, build region-excluded LoRA models, and perform inpainting on predefined local parts. The results show that typical shapes and simple decorations are often reconstructed consistently, whereas some region-specific colors, patterns, cord arrangements, and painted details are replaced with more common appearances. These results suggest that region-excluded inpainting can serve as an exploratory tool for finding region-specific local visual features in museum artifact images.

Title: Praxis: Practical Witness Encryption for NP

  • Authors: David Tse (1 and 2), Nikhil Vanjani (1), Psi Vesely (1) ((1) Byzantine Research, (2) Stanford University)

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28552
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28552
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28552]] Praxis: Practical Witness Encryption for NP(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28552)
  • Keywords: security, extraction
  • Abstract: Witness encryption encrypts a message to an NP statement so that whoever holds a witness can decrypt. Over a decade after its introduction, known constructions for general NP statements are far from practical or rest on heuristic or broken assumptions. We propose Praxis, a practical scheme for NP, and prove it extractable in the bilinear generic group and random oracle models under Module-SIS. Our instantiation encrypts to a Groth16 verifier on BN254, set up honestly, which serves every NP relation at the same cost: at $100$-bit security, its ciphertext is $7.88$ GiB, encryption takes $2^{34.2}$ base-field multiplications, and decryption with a Groth16 proof $2^{33.0}$ on average. We give Lean 4 proofs of correctness and extraction at this instantiation's parameters. Praxis starts from the rank-gap template of the recent feasibility result [Hair and Sahai, arXiv 2026]. Where they raise the gap to keep decryption polynomial, we fix it, so decryption ends with a search that is exponential but concretely fast: $O(2^{\lambda/4} N^{1/4})$ expected operations for a statement in $N$ Boolean unknowns at $\lambda$-bit security. A quadratic arithmetic program makes our hidden matrix low rank, so where their ciphertext encodes a basis of a matrix space, ours holds factors of width two, which a pairing multiplies. Weights and identities from an extension field tie copies of this matrix, so an adversary telling encryptions apart yields a witness or a Module-SIS solution. For the same Groth16 statement and $100$-bit security level, Praxis reduces the ciphertext size and the cost of encryption of either of their constructions by over ten orders of magnitude, and that of decryption by over six.

Title: From Reward Engineering to Data-Centric Reinforcement Learning for Effective Agentic Visual Tool Use

  • Authors: Jin Cui, Xinyue Long, Jiepeng Zhou, Boran Zhao, Pengju Ren, Hao Dong
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28558
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28558
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28558]] From Reward Engineering to Data-Centric Reinforcement Learning for Effective Agentic Visual Tool Use(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28558)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly use external visual tools to acquire evidence during reasoning, yet learning when to invoke these tools remains challenging. Existing tool-use reinforcement learning (RL) methods often rely on call-contingent rewards to encourage beneficial tool use, but such reward engineering can distort the task objective and lead to degenerate calling behaviors. We argue that effective tool-use learning requires identifying not merely which questions are difficult, but which failures are attributable to missing visual evidence and can therefore be corrected through tool observations. We propose AOVO, a data-centric framework that enables MLLMs to learn effective tool use through perception attribution and policy-adaptive data sampling (PADS), without introducing tool-specific rewards. It counterfactually probes the model's reasoning states to identify observation-sensitive questions, distinguishing them from reasoning-dominated ones. PADS then periodically estimates category-level observation effects from recent policy rollouts and dynamically reallocates the training distribution toward questions where visual observations provide informative learning signals, while optimizing solely for final-answer correctness. Experiments across diverse visual perception and multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over competitive tool-use MLLMs, with causal analysis further validating the data-centric perspective for learning effective visual tool use.

Title: LoopSight: Looped Vision Transformers with Gated Prediction Memory

  • Authors: Zhaozhi Wang, Mingrui Wu, Jiaming Zhao, An-Syu Li, Jun Luo
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28575
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28575
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28575]] LoopSight: Looped Vision Transformers with Gated Prediction Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28575)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped vision transformers extend computation for visual reasoning by repeatedly applying a shared Transformer core. A challenge is to preserve useful prediction information across loop iterations while allowing the computation state to evolve. In this paper we introduce LoopSight, a looped vision transformer that uses Gated Prediction Memory (GPM) to maintain a persistent prediction state for readout, separate from the core's evolving computation state. At each iteration, a cross-attention gate uses the core's candidate features, the previous prediction state, and the current task state to control writes at the patch and channel level. The model decodes its prediction from the updated memory. We condition the shared core on the current iteration using asymptotic loop encoding and train the model by supervising its prediction at loop counts sampled from a truncated Gaussian distribution. LoopSight outperforms LoopViT across three model scales on ARC-AGI-1 and ARC-AGI-2. On ARC-AGI-1, LoopSight-Small achieves 67.4% pass@2, a gain of 6.6 percentage points over LoopViT-Small with comparable parameter counts and FLOPs. LoopSight also outperforms LoopViT on Visual Sudoku and ImageNet classification. Further analyses on ARC-AGI-1 show that LoopSight preserves more already-correct predictions and maintains more stable readout features as its computation state continues to evolve. The code will be released at this https URL.

Title: Understanding and Mitigating the Fragility of Self-Rewarding Optimization

  • Authors: Rongyi Zhu, Tanqiu Jiang, Fenglong Ma, Ting Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28582
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28582
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28582]] Understanding and Mitigating the Fragility of Self-Rewarding Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28582)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Self-rewarding optimization (SRO) promises to improve language models by enabling them to generate their own reward signals. Yet when and how SRO succeeds or fails remains poorly understood. We reveal that SRO is fundamentally fragile: even seemingly benign perturbations to training prompts can corrupt the self-generated preference signal and trigger iterative collapse. We demonstrate this through two prompt-level attacks that serve as controlled stress tests: a Re-Ranking attack that concentrates training on prompts where self-reward diverges from an oracle, and a Preamble attack that learns short prefixes inflating self-reward without any gain in oracle quality. To explain why these attacks succeed, we introduce self-reward fidelity (SRF), a metric quantifying on-policy agreement between self-evaluated and oracle rewards. We show that SRF can serve as a diagnostic signal of self-supervision reliability, and that controlled SRF perturbations induce a phase-transition region: SRO improves when SRF remains sufficiently high, but collapses once SRF drops below a model- and task-dependent threshold. This analysis motivates Projection Decoupling Defense, which suppresses SRO update components along directions to which the reward generator is locally sensitive, improving SRO robustness under adversarial conditions. This work sheds light on the fragility of SRO and charts promising directions toward making it more robust.

Title: CAR3T: Generalizable Calibration-Guided Reference-View Attention Reuse for Multi-View 3D Transformers

  • Authors: Lan Wei, Haosong Peng, Sicheng Tian, Tianyu Qi, Wenchao Xu, Lei Xue, Yufeng Zhan, Yuanqing Xia
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28591
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28591
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28591]] CAR3T: Generalizable Calibration-Guided Reference-View Attention Reuse for Multi-View 3D Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28591)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Feed-forward multi-view 3D reconstruction relies on global attention for cross-view aggregation, but the attention cost grows quadratically with the number of views, making inference increasingly expensive for larger inputs. Existing acceleration methods compress tokens or sparsify attention, potentially losing geometric detail or restricting cross-view aggregation while leaving redundancy among attention outputs underexploited. We observe similar cross-view attention distributions in certain layers, revealing an opportunity to reduce repeated global aggregation through output reuse. However, the layers suitable for reuse vary with the input, and reusing one layer alters the features received by later layers. Similarity at individual layers therefore cannot characterize the combined effect of multiple reuse decisions on output drift. To address these challenges, we propose CAR3T, a training-free framework combining reference-view attention output reuse with offline risk calibration and online dynamic layer selection. Calibration models cumulative reuse risk, while online selection adapts reuse to each input under a calibrated budget. CAR3T retains all input tokens and attends to the keys and values of all views, reducing the core attention computation in selected layers from quadratic to linear. Experiments on VGGT, DA3, and $\pi^3$ show forward speedups up to $1.41\times$ with at most 100 views and $1.75\times$ on longer sequences, with accuracy trade-offs across backbones and datasets.

Title: BitShare: Sensitivity-Aware Binning and Bit Sharing for Compacting LLM Weight Storage

  • Authors: Qiuyu Ren, Sudipta Paria, Aritra Dasgupta, Swarup Bhunia
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.ET
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28607
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28607
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28607]] BitShare: Sensitivity-Aware Binning and Bit Sharing for Compacting LLM Weight Storage(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28607)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Decoding with large language models at small batch sizes is limited by memory bandwidth, so the bits stored per weight largely set its cost. Group-wise integer quantization stores every weight of a group at the same precision, although the input channels of a group differ in sensitivity. We present BITSHARE, a codebook-free weight format that keeps the integer grid and the dequantizer of group-wise quantization but varies precision within each group. Sensitivity-aware binning places the channels of each group into three bins at fixed positions, and static bit sharing with optimal bit-value allocation lets the less sensitive weights share their lower bits, each stored once per group or once per pair of weights. A sharing-aware version of GPTQ chooses every stored value, with no fine-tuning and no codebook. BITSHARE has two modes. BITSHARE-U gives every group the same layout, so its decoder routes bits by fixed wiring, while BITSHARE-N chooses a layout per group within the same bit budget. With calibration on the Pile and at the same storage, both modes have lower perplexity than GPTQ at 3 and 2 bits on WikiText-2 and C4. At 2 bits across Llama-2 and Llama-3 models, BITSHARE-N lowers WikiText-2 perplexity by up to 70% against SliM-LLM and up to 35% against GPTAQ. At the 2-bit perplexity of SliM-LLM, BITSHARE-U stores 13.6-19.4% fewer bits per weight, cutting DRAM weight traffic by the same fraction. A verified hardware decoder implementation synthesized in SkyWater 130 nm reconstructs BITSHARE weights at the throughput of INT-n and SliM-LLM decoders.

The discrepancy in AUROC did not reach statistical significance. Title: Nearly Minimax-Optimal Sample Complexity of Natural Policy Gradient with a Matching Information-Theoretic Lower Bound

  • Authors: Asha Barua, Sajad Khodadadian
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.OC, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28628
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28628
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28628]] Nearly Minimax-Optimal Sample Complexity of Natural Policy Gradient with a Matching Information-Theoretic Lower Bound(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28628)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: A fundamental question in reinforcement learning is how many sampled transitions are needed to learn a near-optimal policy, and whether direct policy optimization can attain this statistical limit. To answer this question, we consider natural policy gradient (NPG), a widely used policy optimization method. We study tabular finite-horizon Markov decision processes (MDPs) with known rewards and unknown horizon-dependent transition kernels, accessed through a generative model. Our sample-based NPG algorithms update the policy by backward sweeps and do not estimate transition probabilities. Under three different batch-size and step size schedules, we establish last-iterate guarantees with sample complexity $\widetilde O(SAH^4/\varepsilon^2)$, where $S$, $A$, and $H$ denote the number of states, the number of actions, and the horizon length, respectively. In this setting, horizon-dependent transition kernels contribute a factor of $H$ to our transition-query bound. Our information-theoretic lower bound of $\Omega(SAH^4/\varepsilon^2)$ shows that the resulting $H^4$ dependence is unavoidable in the worst case, even for agents that select their queries adaptively. To the best of our knowledge, these are the first last-iterate guarantees for NPG whose sample complexity matches an information-theoretic lower bound up to logarithmic factors. Consequently, our results demonstrate that direct policy-space optimization through NPG can achieve the fundamental statistical limits of reinforcement learning while guaranteeing the performance of the final policy. Furthermore, for known kernels, we prove that exact NPG with a constant step size $\eta=1/H$ requires $\Omega(H^2/\varepsilon)$ iterations in the worst case, matching the known upper bound for that step size.

  • Abstract: Long video question answering requires finding relevant evidence before reasoning over it. We introduce LoopSeek, a framework that decouples evidence search from answer generation through a division of labor between a non-thinking System I and a generative System II. Our key insight is that search can be expressed as a sequence of small, typed decisions, allowing System I to find the evidence that System II needs. We introduce Context-Marginalized Retrieval Evaluation (CMRE), which repeatedly evaluates each target query in sampled contexts using the corresponding rows of a fixed query-gallery similarity matrix. CMRE distinguishes structural exploitability, quantified by gains over independent retrieval, from context robustness, measured by same-query Top-1 context consistency. Under leave-one-subject-out evaluation on THINGS-EEG2 across six frozen representations and six structure-aware inference rules, structure-aware inference improves full-context Top-1 accuracy by up to 20.40 percentage points over independent retrieval. However, the rule selected under full-context evaluation is no longer optimal in 17 of 24 representation-context-size conditions (70.8%). Selecting the full-context winner incurs an average loss of 2.70 percentage points and a maximum loss of 8.29 percentage points under reduced contexts. Representation rankings are likewise inference- and context-dependent: the ordering under independent retrieval is not consistently preserved under structure-aware inference. Moreover, larger retrieval gains do not necessarily imply greater context robustness, showing that retrieval effectiveness and prediction stability provide complementary views of structure-aware inference. Full-context accuracy captures one operating point of context-dependent inference. CMRE complements it by characterizing how structural gains, context consistency, and comparisons of rules and representations change with query context.

Title: S-Mind: Internalizing Multi-View Geometry for Spatial Intelligence

  • Authors: Muxin Fu, Peike Yu, Guibin Zhang, Wenrui Zhou, Runze Liu, Lang Feng, Fuchao Yang, Feng Chen, Yixuan Li, Mengao Zhang, Shiying Zhang, Wen Shen, Ling Pan, Shuicheng Yan, Bo An

  • Abstract: Tor end-to-end correlation attacks can link a client to its destination by matching traffic observed at both ends of a Tor circuit.   • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Reward-guided diffusion requires effective steering and correction of the bias introduced by approximate value functions. Title: Rethinking Visual Token Retention in Multimodal Reasoning

  • Authors: Fawaz Sammani, Nikos Deligiannis
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28684
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28684
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28684]] Rethinking Visual Token Retention in Multimodal Reasoning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28684)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Autoregressive Large Vision-Language Models solve multimodal reasoning tasks by retaining hundreds or thousands of visual tokens, allowing every generated token to attend to the visual tokens at any time during the generation process. However, whether continuous access to these visual tokens is necessary remains poorly understood. In this work, we investigate how visual token influence evolves along two dimensions: generation time and transformer depth. Using long-form visual reasoning benchmarks and multiple model scales, we first analyze the attention dynamics across generation time and transformer depth. We identify a temporal and depth-wise structure in the use of visual information. Visual attention peaks during the initial generation steps and then rapidly declines as attention shifts toward previously generated reasoning tokens. Across depth, visual influence is concentrated in intermediate layers and becomes very weak in the final layers. We further verify these observations with causal intervention that removes all visual tokens across generation time and model depth. These interventions confirm that visual-token dependence in multimodal reasoning is neither temporally persistent nor uniformly distributed across transformer depth. Thus, retaining the complete visual context at every layer and decoding step may be unnecessary. Our experiments demonstrate that we can remove up to 59\% of tokens from the full input prompt across generation time and 11\% across all transformer layers, while incurring only a very small performance drop. On video benchmarks, up to 65\% of the input prompt tokens can be removed during generation without affecting performance.

  • Abstract: QR-UOV is a Quotient Ring Unbalanced Oil and Vinegar (UOV) multivariate quadratic (MQ) signature scheme that has progressed to round-3 of the post-quantum cryptography (PQC) additional signatures call by the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). Until now, for architectural efficiency and performance trade-offs, QR-UOV has been evaluated only on software platforms and has never been considered on hardware, such as field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs). Title: Peel, Don't Whack: Partial Coverage, Imperfect Validators, and Intervention Based Shortcut Mitigation

  • Authors: Ali Alavi
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28701
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28701
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28701]] Peel, Don't Whack: Partial Coverage, Imperfect Validators, and Intervention Based Shortcut Mitigation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28701)
  • Keywords: robust, watermark
  • Abstract: Without group labels, shortcut mitigation increasingly relies on interventions : label-preserving edits, such as background or watermark replacement, that are proposed automatically and accepted by a validator. Invariance to a complete family of valid edits yields robustness, but in practice coverage is partial and validators err. We analyze both in a stylized linear model. For the population logistic solution and the max-margin limit of gradient descent, the decision on every shortcut-flip group follows one additive rule. Hence worst-group accuracy never decreases with coverage, while individual groups can provably degrade, a mechanism for the Whac-a-Mole effect; full coverage is not always needed. In the logistic regime the validator's false-accept rate must stay below a closed-form tolerance that grows with coverage. With noiseless training data, one false accept can make the max-margin solution ignore the core. In this model, sensitivity-based discovery removes shortcuts in decreasing salience. We also give a model-agnostic certificate, valid under content-independent validation, whose validator-error term comes from an audit. Simulations match the theory, and deep networks on synthetic and real images show the predicted Whac-a-Mole signs, monotone worst-group accuracy and validator fragility. The resulting procedure, Peel (Propose-Edit-Evaluate-Learn), can propose candidate edits from the model's own mistakes. Given an oracle library of the benchmarks' intervention operators, it selects the right ones and beats group-label methods on Waterbirds and UrbanCars. With fully generated edits, run end to end from three discovery seeds, it improves on empirical risk minimization (ERM) on five of six benchmarks but trails exact edits.

This flexibility, however, does not necessarily translate into consistent forecasts: similar patch states may play different roles when used in their original temporal order. Beyond these controls, CLAIR achieves the lowest horizon-averaged MSE on five of six long-term benchmarks and the lowest aggregate overall weighted average (OWA) on M4 among evaluated methods, with competitive few-shot performance.   • Abstract: Video diffusion models can render convincing frames while failing to produce the requested action. Increasing classifier-free guidance (CFG) can strengthen motion, but also saturates and shifts colors. Scalar CFG assigns the same coefficient to the averaged and frame-varying components of its prediction difference, limiting independent control of temporal variation. THAW, Temporal Harmonic Amplification during Warm-up, retains stock CFG and adds a separately weighted residual during an initial denoising window. Its dose specifies the added norm relative to the current CFG difference, giving the extra guidance a consistent relative budget across sampling steps. THAW is training-free and reuses CFG's two model predictions with an unchanged network evaluation count. It improves cost-matched motion over scalar and scheduled CFG on five video models, with positive point estimates against existing guidance methods on three of them.
