Title: Who Is Prompt-Injecting Coding Agents? An Empirical Study of Hidden AI-Directed Text on GitHub

  • Authors: Gabi Nakibly, Zvika Babo
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28763
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28763
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28763]] Who Is Prompt-Injecting Coding Agents? An Empirical Study of Hidden AI-Directed Text on GitHub(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28763)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: AI coding agents now triage issues, review pull requests and pick up tasks inside GitHub repositories, and to do so they read text that anyone with a GitHub account can write. This makes GitHub a prime surface for indirect prompt injection, in which instructions embedded in untrusted content steer a model that later processes it. Prior work has demonstrated such attacks against agents on GitHub in controlled settings, but how often they occur in practice, who deploys them, and for what purpose remain unknown. We present the first large-scale empirical study of AI-directed hidden text on GitHub. We analyzed roughly 145 million issues, pull requests, comments and README files, detected text that GitHub does not render to a human reader but that an agent receives in full, and filtered it with a two-stage LLM classification, a template provenance check and an exhaustive manual review. We identify 2,281 validated findings in issues, pull requests and comments (16 per million items), and AI-directed hidden text in the READMEs of 107 repositories. We find a heterogeneous ecosystem of four uses: attacks on other parties' agents, defensive text that restricts agents, content override that steers what they write, and first-party orchestration, in which projects coordinate their own agents through hidden markers. Attacks are rare (about one in 3.3 million items) but aimed at maintainers' own triage, review and CI agents. Orchestration accounts for two thirds of the findings in issues, pull requests and comments, grew fivefold in four months, and forms a control channel that anyone who can comment can imitate. Replayed offline against five models we tested, the attacks succeed against one small, fast model in 19% of cases, and against none once HTML comments are stripped. We release our detection tool and every finding.

Title: Contrastive Gradient Guidance for Test-Time Multi-Preference Alignment of Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Po-Yi Lu, Jui-Chao Lu, Paul Kuo-Ming Huang, Shih-Hsin Wang, Chun-Liang Li, Hsuan-Tien Lin
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28764
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28764
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28764]] Contrastive Gradient Guidance for Test-Time Multi-Preference Alignment of Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28764)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Aligning pre-trained diffusion models with multiple context-dependent preferences requires adapting generation at test time rather than relying on offline fine-tuning, which requires hyperparameter tuning and a new training run for each preference trade-off. Existing approaches compress diverse preferences into a single scalar reward objective for fine-tuning or test-time guidance rather than directly reusing existing generative experts released for different purposes and obtained through different fine-tuning methods. In this work, we propose Contrastive Gradient Guidance (CGG), a test-time compositional generative framework that reformulates multi-preference alignment as a composition of distributions. CGG represents each preference as a contrastive preference pair of favored experts and unfavored anti-experts, whose score difference forms a contrastive gradient guidance signal that combines denoising directions (gradients) from off-the-shelf diffusion models during sampling. Experiments show that CGG reuses pre-trained and fine-tuned models as favored experts or anti-experts to control output style and safety, and that it can be combined with existing sampling-based reward guidance. With a single fixed scale per pair, CGG composes five preferences at once and gives a more balanced trade-off across text-image preference, concept control, photographic fidelity, and safety than offline fine-tuning, weight-space merging, and reward steering.

Title: AdapToC: A Unified Framework for Multi-Task Visual Token Communication

  • Authors: Zhicheng Liu, Chenliang Wu, Zhouxiang Zhao, Zhaohui Yang, Zhaoyang Zhang

  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Pretrained discrete visual tokenizers supply compact latents for synthesis, recognition, and embodied perception, yet wireless token streaming remains fragile. Under fading and noisy pilots, hard cyclic redundancy check (CRC) erasures turn corrupted blocks into missing tokens while frozen decoders amplify edge errors, which can collapse unequal error protection (UEP) into a binary choice between strong coding and omission under severe fading. Soft bit posteriors break this collapse. We present AdapToC, a single-pass transceiver that restores multi-tier rate-distortion tradeoffs without altering tokenizer weights or differentiating through pixel decoders. We align codebook geometry to the channel via offline frequency-weighted Pseudo-Gray relabeling and Halton dispersion to contract bit and burst errors with zero overhead. Because soft recovery pulls intermediate rates onto the three-tier convex hull, our unit-gated AdaLN sender schedules protection tiers across channel budgets, while our pilot-calibrated receiver feeds factorized codebook posteriors into a ConvNeXt-attention restorer with CRC bypass. Our single-pass restorer runs 8.3x faster than iterative resampling, raises accuracy while accelerating decoder-free recognition 20.6x, and improves fidelity plug-and-play across TiTok, VAR, and zero-shot NuPlan.

  • Abstract: Language-model agents can read and edit the weights of neural networks without gradients, and this is increasingly presented as a new way of repairing models.   • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Federated learning often relies on partial client participation because full participation is costly in cross-device wireless systems with limited uplink resources, heterogeneous channel conditions, energy constraints, and strict upload deadlines. However, partial participation introduces sampling variance, causing the sampled update to deviate from the full-participation update. Existing optimal client sampling methods reduce this variance but usually rely on simplified client-count budgets, while wireless-aware scheduling methods account for communication constraints but do not directly optimize sampling variance. This paper proposes resource-aware optimal client sampling (RAOCS), a unified variance-minimizing client scheduling framework under uplink resource-block, round deadline, update-information, and energy constraints. Experimental results demonstrate that RAOCS achieves accuracy comparable to that of OCS while reducing the average upload time per iteration by more than a factor of 252.

Title: Reporting Differential Privacy Guarantees via Operationally Equivalent $\varepsilon$: What Does $\varepsilon=19.61$ Mean for the 2020 U.S. Census?

  • Authors: Masayuki Terada, Shinsuke Ito, Takumasa Ishioka, Hidetoshi Matsui
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28790
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28790
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28790]] Reporting Differential Privacy Guarantees via Operationally Equivalent $\varepsilon$: What Does $\varepsilon=19.61$ Mean for the 2020 U.S. Census?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28790)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect, attack
  • Abstract: The large value $\varepsilon=19.61$, reported as an approximate differential privacy (ADP) parameter for the 2020 U.S. Census, has prompted doubts about the adequacy of Census privacy protection. It was obtained by converting component zero-concentrated differential privacy (zCDP) guarantees to ADP at $\delta=10^{-10}$. A single ADP pair can substantially loosen the original upper bound on the power (true positive rate) of a distinguishing test. At significance level $\alpha$, we express the original bound as the smallest pure differential privacy (DP) parameter giving the same power bound. We call this the $\alpha$-operationally equivalent $\varepsilon$. The full curve represents the original bound exactly on $(0,1)$, even when the bound is nonconcave. For maximum-power curves and bounds derived from trade-off functions, we characterize the extrema of operationally equivalent $\varepsilon$ over an interval of significance levels. When finite, they give the parameters of the pure DP bounds that enclose the original power bound on that interval. The mapping preserves the ordering of power bounds at each significance level. For Laplace and Gaussian mechanisms with equal noise variance, conversion to single ADP pairs or Gaussian DP envelopes can reverse this ordering. For the P.L. 94-171 redistricting data, we reproduce the reported $\varepsilon=19.61$ as the sum of rounded component ADP values. The published zCDP guarantee yields operationally equivalent $\varepsilon$ values of approximately 4.25, 2.94, and 3.18 at significance levels of 1%, 5%, and 10%, respectively. Converting the same guarantee to a single ADP pair at $\delta=10^{-10}$ gives power bounds near 1 at all three levels. The pure DP scale thus clarifies constraints on distinguishing attacks that are difficult to discern from the reported $\varepsilon=19.61$.

Title: RadAG: Reward-Adversarial Attention Guidance for Efficient Inference-Time Alignment of Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Hyeongchan Jeon, Kwanyoung Kim
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28809
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28809
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28809]] RadAG: Reward-Adversarial Attention Guidance for Efficient Inference-Time Alignment of Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28809)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion and flow-based models have achieved remarkable success in generation, yet their samples often remain suboptimal in fidelity and text alignment. Two lines of inference-time methods address this: extrapolation guidance, such as classifier-free guidance (CFG) and its variants, and reward guidance, which aligns samples with reward models of human preference. Extrapolation guidance needs only forward passes, but it builds its reference by heuristic rules unrelated to the reward and therefore cannot steer sampling toward a given reward. Gradient-based reward guidance steers sampling directly toward a higher reward, but backpropagates through the entire generator at every step. This trade-off raises the question of whether extrapolation guidance can steer sampling toward a higher reward without a full backward pass. To answer this, we introduce Reward-Adversarial Attention Guidance (RadAG), which constructs the reference by perturbing the attention logits of a single block against their reward gradient and extrapolates away from it. We prove that this update increases the reward of the clean estimate to first order, while its backward pass stops at the perturbed block instead of traversing the entire generator. On FLUX.2-klein-4B and 9B with PickScore and aesthetic rewards, RadAG achieves the highest target reward in every setting with 30-65% less time than gradient-based and candidate-based baselines.

Title: When Being Wrong Is Right: Reverse-Capability Audio CAPTCHAs through Psychoacoustic Design

  • Authors: Mingjie Bao, Yu Dai, Guangke Chen, Guozhu Meng
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.HC, cs.SD

  • Abstract: Large audio-language models (LALMs) have recently been shown to bypass audio CAPTCHAs designed around limitations of automatic speech recognition. It selects challenges in which stable human perceptual limitations cause the human-reference answer to differ from the physical ground truth, while machine analysis tends toward that truth. Title: More Is Never Less: Affordable Robustness to Additive Attacks via Monotone Multiple-Instance Learning

  • Authors: Alexander Herzog, Shae McFadden, Lorenzo Cavallaro
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28825
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28825
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28825]] More Is Never Less: Affordable Robustness to Additive Attacks via Monotone Multiple-Instance Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28825)
  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: Static malware detectors that represent each sample as a bag of extracted features, such as permissions, API calls, or imported functions, are cheap to deploy and remain state-of-the-art under temporal drift and in selective classification. Yet, attackers can evade them by adding benign-looking content that outweighs malicious evidence. Canonically, detectors encode the presence of a few thousand features, selected during training, as sparse multi-hot vectors over a fixed vocabulary. Features that emerge after training remain invisible. We show that linear models and multi-layer perceptrons (MLPs) over this input are sum-pooled set models in disguise. Sum-pooling lets added benign features cancel malicious evidence. Instead, we cast malware detection with set-shaped static features as a multiple-instance learning (MIL) problem. To this end, we propose MalSets, which uses max-pooled Deep Sets models with a monotone head to ensure that any malicious evidence remains undiluted; thus, more is never less. Under MIL, a general text encoder maps any feature string to an embedding, thus eliminating the long-standing out-of-vocabulary problem at test time. Max-pooling retains the malicious evidence regardless of how many benign features an attacker adds. Since max-pooling can only grow when features are added, constraining the head alone makes the malware score monotone under additions, even against a white-box attacker adapted to this architecture. While monotone detectors are never evaded by state-of-the-art black-box or white-box attacks, they often significantly pay for this robustness in clean performance since benign features cannot count as evidence. However, by combining a non-monotone set encoder with max-pooling and a monotone head, MalSets maintains the robustness of prior monotone classifiers while achieving state-of-the-art performance.

Title: Hidden Sensitivity in LLM Pre-training: A Dynamic System View

  • Authors: Bo Liu, Yuan Xie, Junan Chen, Fujun Han
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28830

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: In Large Language Model (LLM) pre-training, loss curves can conceal substantial instability, i.e., even small perturbations may lead to dramatically different predictions while leaving loss nearly unchanged. To investigate this phenomenon and reveal the underlying mechanisms, we first formulate LLM pre-training as a non-autonomous dynamical system that jointly evolves model weights and Adam optimizer states. Based on this formulation, we introduce a normalized sensitivity score in optimizer-relative coordinates to identify sensitive training checkpoints. At one such checkpoint, a weight perturbation of only 10^(-5) leads to 35.7% disagreement in top-1 predictions and 16.2% disagreement in benchmark answers after 200 training steps, with only 0.8% of greedy generations remaining identical despite similar validation losses. Furthermore, we find that perturbations to Adam's first and second moments induce comparably large effects, with similar sensitivity patterns observed in OLMo-1B. Finally, guided by our diagnostic, targeted stabilization reduces top-1 disagreement to 0.83% and increases generation exact match to 88.3%, at the cost of a 0.11-nat increase in validation loss. Our findings reveal a hidden form of optimizer-state-dependent sensitivity in LLM pre-training that conventional loss-based evaluations may overlook, while demonstrating its potential for diagnosis and mitigation.

This detail allocation score ranks candidates from the same source. Comparing their response--label alignment with that of the real source image also guides repair decisions and, in multiclass tasks, the choice of regions to edit. Selected candidates and accepted repairs are used for training with the original labels and the unchanged downstream segmenter. Experiments on camouflaged object detection, degraded salient object detection, and adverse-condition semantic segmentation show that our selection outperforms equally sized random and label-agreement subsets. Combining selection and repair improves over real-only training by up to 0.019 in $S_\alpha$ on COD10K and 2.86 mIoU points on ACDC.

Title: Dynamic Model Merging with Multi-Layer Aggregated Routing Evidence

  • Authors: Yuxi Li, Jiacong Hu, Jie Song, Mingli Song, Yuanyu Wan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28855
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28855
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28855]] Dynamic Model Merging with Multi-Layer Aggregated Routing Evidence(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28855)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Dynamic model merging aims to retain the efficiency of a unified model while better preserving task-specific expertise through input-dependent routing at both the task and head levels. However, even the routing components of the state-of-the-art approach remain limited: the task routing is highly sensitive to the choice of routing layer, whereas the head routing relies on simple max-logit competition. To address these limitations, we propose multi-layer aggregated routing evidence (MATE) as a unified mechanism for improving both task and head routing. Moreover, we observe that this signal also encodes useful information about the suitability of the corresponding classification heads. This motivates us to further combine the MATE signal with the max-logit score to guide classification head selection.   • Authors: A Adith
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28856
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28856

  • Keywords: interpretability, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: EEG foundation models are pre-trained almost exclusively with masked reconstruction through a free-form decoder, sometimes with hand-crafted spectral targets added, as in MANAS-2. PhyMind keeps a standard masked transformer encoder, but its decoder must explain the masked signal through 68 cortical regions, each driven by damped stochastic oscillators and an aperiodic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, projected to the recording's own montage by a fixed boundary-element lead field evaluated per derivation. Title: Interaction Retention and Output-Likelihood Training in Parallel Sampling

  • Authors: Da Li, Bo Jin, Xiaopeng Wei, Hui Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28858
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.28858
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.28858]] Interaction Retention and Output-Likelihood Training in Parallel Sampling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28858)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Parallel samplers such as masked diffusion language models generate several coordinates per step from conditional marginals and randomize which coordinates are generated together, so even exact conditionals return a distorted distribution. We give an exact local account of this distortion. Around any full-support product law, the Gram operator of the sampler's response is diagonal in the interaction decomposition, and its eigenvalues are retention probabilities: the probability that an interaction has a unique latest-revealed coordinate. This retention spectrum determines the exact frontier between output accuracy and conditional calibration, its regularization path, and the population response of finite-mixture likelihood training. For pairwise interactions it yields a sharp dichotomy. Under homogeneous retention, output-likelihood training is equivalent to a single context gain; heterogeneous retention opens correction directions beyond any scalar, with an advantage bounded by an explicit heterogeneity gap. On binarized digits and MNIST, output-likelihood training within a heterogeneous schedule library lowers calibrated test negative log-likelihood in all twelve paired comparisons, and the gain survives pair-specific recalibration and a stronger path optimizer. For a frozen 169.6-million-parameter masked diffusion language model, serially normalized context gains are 1.070 and 1.056 at 8 and 16 rounds, against the predicted values 1.134 and 1.058.

  • Authors: Zhiling Zhou, Jiachun Pan, Fengzhuo Zhang, Dirk Bergemann, Zhuoran Yang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29002
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29002
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29002]] Demystifying Classifier-Free Guidance for Auto-Regressive Image Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29002)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) has been widely adopted in autoregressive (AR) models for high-quality image generation. Despite its strong empirical performance, its mechanism in AR models remains unclear. This paper demystifies the mechanism of CFG through both empirical and theoretical studies. By examining the top-ranked semantics of different components in CFG, we show that texture information is primarily encoded in the difference between the conditional and unconditional logits, whereas both logits share previous-token repetition as their leading semantics. This strong repetition semantics obscures the desired texture information, causing greedy decoding from the conditional logits alone to produce nearly pure-color images. Through a training-dynamics analysis of shallow transformers, we prove that this shared repetition semantics does not arise from limitations or failures of pretraining, but instead originates from the texture sparsity of images. We further show that CFG improves generation quality by rectifying this repetition bias during inference. Motivated by the shared semantics between conditional and unconditional logits, we propose Attention Weight Reuse (AttnReuse), which reuses intermediate attention computations from the conditional-logit forward pass to accelerate the unconditional-logit computation. AttnReuse reduces about $25\%$ of attention computation with little performance degradation across different models.

Title: Mirrored Min-of-K Training

  • Authors: Jiaru Zhang, Zeyun Deng, Hyunsoo Yun, Yupeng Zhou, Bingyao Du, Juntong Peng, Zichong Yang, Can Cui, Chuheng Wei, Ruqi Zhang, Ziran Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29008
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29008
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29008]] Mirrored Min-of-K Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29008)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Min-of-K (MoK) training is widely used to train models that produce diverse outputs from random noise. For each target, it draws K outputs from the model and backpropagates only through the one closest to the target. However, MoK generally does not make the generated distribution match the data distribution. In this paper, we analyze the MoK bias across all values of K and show that tuning K shifts between failure modes, from mode dropping at small K to distribution flattening at large K. We further prove that every nontrivial loss based only on target-candidate distances fails to recover some data distribution, since these distances do not reveal how the candidates are placed relative to each other. Based on these analyses, we propose mirrored MoK (MMoK), a drop-in modification of MoK training that draws one extra sample from the model, applies the closest-candidate selection rule against it, and subtracts the resulting distance. Since this distance is measured between samples of the model, it provides additional information about how the candidates are placed relative to each other. We prove that MMoK can in principle recover data distributions that MoK cannot. We empirically validate MMoK on five scenarios, namely synthetic mixtures, pedestrian and vehicle trajectory prediction, tropical cyclone forecasting, robotic policy learning, and ambiguous medical image segmentation. The results confirm the effectiveness and generality of MMoK in both distribution learning and task performance.

Title: Watermark Claims Under Test: Lessons from Auditing a Production LLM

  • Authors: Felix Desyatirikov
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29018
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29018
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29018]] Watermark Claims Under Test: Lessons from Auditing a Production LLM(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29018)
  • Keywords: watermark
  • Abstract: Can we tell whether an LLM is watermarked without knowing its secret key? Published black-box tests suggest that carefully designed prompts can reveal the statistical footprint of a watermark. This paper examines what happens when those tests move from controlled reference implementations to a deployed model whose watermarking parameters are undisclosed. We present an audit of the Red-Green watermark presence test on Claude. Across ten initial configurations, the published test never rejects on a model the provider states is watermarked, despite detecting a local reference at comparable sample sizes. Constructed contexts can trigger native digit-to-word preferences, while comparisons within a single response can measure how the model plans its answer. Tokenization changes which contexts the probe actually controls. These findings show why validation on a surrogate model does not establish validity on the model under investigation. A redesigned probe finds a substantial context-dependent shift on Claude Fable that is absent on two sibling controls. However, the pre-registered decision remains inconclusive because selective refusals violate the protocol, and the effect's watermark origin is unresolved. We show how to design controls, distinguish statistical rejection from evidence of watermarking and interpret a null without treating it as proof of absence. We provide researchers and practitioners working on LLM evaluation, watermarking, and provenance with an audit checklist grounded in concrete successes and failures. Code, prompts, and parsed responses are available for reproducing the analyses.

Speculative decoding can reduce this cost, yet its behavior across the rendering requirements of T2I prompts remains insufficiently understood. We introduce T2ISpec, a benchmark of 1,400 prompts balanced across seven rendering topics and two difficulty levels. It evaluates autoregressive and diffusion/flow accelerators by latency, work performed by the target model, and image quality. Our study reveals three key findings: (1) aggregate results hide large differences between topics, (2) alignment and preference scores can disagree with prompt constraint satisfaction, and (3) large reductions in target work can yield limited latency gains. Motivated by these findings, we propose TopicRoute, a plugin that combines prompt routing with speculative and cache controls without retraining the generator. Across six model--method pairs, it achieves a geometric mean speedup of 1.0308x over the native methods, and the speedup carries over to external prompts with the same settings, while quality gains and losses vary across configurations.

Title: LITMUS - Literature Informed Testbed for Mapping Usecases and Synthesizing Risk-Cards

  • Authors: Seshu Tirupathi, Elizabeth M. Daly, Radu Marinescu, Javier Carnerero Cano, Yannis Belkhiter

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29034]] LITMUS - Literature Informed Testbed for Mapping Usecases and Synthesizing Risk-Cards(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29034)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: The proliferation of large language models (LLMs) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) in real-world applications has heightened the need for systematic, safety-oriented deployment frameworks. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach in generating robust, evidence-based risk assessments for LLM deployments.

Title: Recurrent Point Transformer via Test-Time Training

  • Authors: Wentao Zhou, Xuweiyi Chen, Charles Saunders, Aruni RoyChowdhury, Zezhou Cheng
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29059
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29059
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29059]] Recurrent Point Transformer via Test-Time Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29059)
  • Keywords: transformer, segmentation
  • Abstract: Real-world point clouds are not naturally bounded: 3D scans grow as sensors move through rooms, LiDAR surveys extend across forest plots, and 4D captures can span thousands of frames. Yet most point-cloud encoders are designed for fixed-size inputs, forcing inference-time cropping or tiling that loses long-range context and requires ad-hoc output stitching. We formulate arbitrary-extent point-cloud segmentation as recurrent sequence-of-chunks processing and introduce PoinTTT, a recurrent point-cloud encoder built on Point Transformer V3 (PTv3). PoinTTT augments PTv3 with residual Large Chunk Test-Time Training (LaCT) layers in both the encoder and decoder. A pilot study on ScanNet and nuScenes shows that recurrent TTT improves segmentation over PTv3 baselines. These results suggest that test-time training is a practical primitive for scalable spatial and temporal point-cloud understanding.

Title: Single-Transformer Latent Diffusion

  • Authors: Hao-Yu Hsu, Shenlong Wang, Alexander G. Schwing
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29064
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29064
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29064]] Single-Transformer Latent Diffusion(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29064)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Latent generative models typically rely on three separate networks: an encoder that tokenizes pixels, a generator that models the latent distribution, and a decoder that maps latents back to pixels. UNITE recently merged the first two into a single generative encoder trained in one stage, but its decoder remains a separate network. We ask whether this last network can be folded in too, so that one diffusion transformer encodes, generates, and decodes. Simply letting the shared backbone decode does not help. What makes it work is to cast all three modes as position-aligned sequence maps over one fully shared backbone, distinguished only by a mode token and the timestep. The resulting model, UNIFIED, is simple: one network trained from scratch in a single stage with only reconstruction and flow-matching losses, without adversarial losses or pretrained encoders (e.g., DINO). On ImageNet-1K 256x256 at matched parameters, UNIFIED improves gFID over UNITE at three sizes (~60M, ~115M, and ~215M) and reaches 2.08 with 215M parameters, the lowest among compared models under 250M parameters. On text-to-image generation, UNIFIED-B (218M) reaches a GenEval score of 0.87 at 512x512, exceeding single-stage pixel diffusion models with around one billion parameters. Full unification of latent diffusion is thus not merely feasible but beneficial.

Title: RES: Accelerating Diffusion Language Models with Rolling Entropy Sampling

  • Authors: Patrick Pynadath, Nicolas Perez-Nieves, Bobak Shahriari, Jean Tarbouriech, Ruqi Zhang, Ivan Lobov
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29066
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29066
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29066]] RES: Accelerating Diffusion Language Models with Rolling Entropy Sampling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29066)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: A diffusion language sampler makes two choices for every position in its denoising window: where the next token comes from, and when the position is committed to the KV cache. Using the entropy-bound sampler in DiffusionGemma, we study two extremes of the second choice. Committing each token as soon as it is accepted removes the chance to revise it, which hurts when the entropy bound is relaxed to trade accuracy for speed. When accepted tokens are frozen, macro accuracy falls from 73% to 20% as the bound is relaxed, while the unfrozen sampler stays near 70%. Committing a whole block at once wastes computation, since tokens converge in a largely left-to-right order and more than half of the window holds already-final tokens by twenty denoising steps. These findings motivate the Rolling Entropy Sampler (RES), which commits a contiguous accepted prefix once it reaches a minimum length and keeps positions farther ahead open to revision. Unlike earlier rolling samplers, RES runs on uniform diffusion, where accepted tokens can still change, and it needs no training. Across seven benchmarks, RES increases tokens per forward pass from 19.3 to 25.5, while macro accuracy goes from 80.7% to 78.8%, and it extends the speed-quality Pareto frontier beyond what tuning the entropy bound alone reaches.

Title: TesseraFlow: Scalable Video Diffusion for Dynamic Medical Imaging Inverse Problems

  • Authors: Atakan Topcu, Mohammadhossein Momeni, Alfredo De Goyeneche, Michael Lustig
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29099
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29099
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29099]] TesseraFlow: Scalable Video Diffusion for Dynamic Medical Imaging Inverse Problems(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29099)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Generative priors are state-of-the-art for modeling medical images in image reconstruction tasks, but reconstruction with video priors takes tens of seconds to minutes per frame. Latent priors inherit the errors of their autoencoder, the memory of pixel-space whole-frame priors grows with the frame size, and pixel-space patch priors are slow for two reasons. First, their deep networks process every frame at full resolution at every sampling step, even though early steps resolve only low frequencies. Second, their samplers spend many network evaluations per sampling step on overlapping windows, multiple re-noising rounds, or backpropagation through the prior. We introduce TesseraFlow, a complex-valued pixel-space flow prior that reduces the first cost. A global encoder reads a low-resolution view of every frame once per sampling step. A single shallow decoder operates on this view during early steps and on full-resolution space-time tiles during later steps, so no network processes a whole frame at full resolution. Our sampler, ReTile, addresses the second reason. We use randomized grid shifting at every sampling step along with re-noising, which acts as a stochastic gradient instead of computing overlapping tiles, so a single network pass per sampling step suffices. On its own, ReTile speeds up an existing global-context patch prior by 6-9x, and our prior adds a further 3-5x speedup. TesseraFlow reconstructs about one 256x256 frame per second, 20-367x faster than patch and latent video priors, and can match or exceed the best generative baseline on cardiac, brain, and abdominal MRI and lung CT, at a fraction of its GPU time.

Title: When Can a Rewiring Control Test LLM Weight Readers? C2 and an Eligibility Protocol

  • Authors: Vaishak Girish Kumar, Sanika Vilas Najan
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29120
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29120

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models can predict the outputs of small neural networks from raw weights, but success could reflect recognition of weight-space regularities (fingerprints) rather than computation, and function-preserving controls cannot tell these apart. C2 separates fingerprinting from computation only under three conditions, which we state as an eligibility protocol: a strategy restricted to C2-invariant summaries must already predict the target; C2 must change a property specific to the rewiring; and the reader's identity must be attested. In two public CNN zoos the conditions traded off: weight statistics predicted CIFAR-10 accuracy in-distribution, but C2's effect on accuracy was not specific to the swap, while per-image targets were specific but not predictable from invariant summaries. On a constructed population where a planted fingerprint rule works, all conditions hold and C2 separates a rule-following reader from an executor. A self-hosted Qwen3.8-27B followed the rewired function rather than the shortcut, and showed no evidence of using the weights with thinking disabled. A registered replication on fresh networks confirmed both findings (tracking minus stuck 0.875, 95% interval [0.750, 1.000]), and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-14B also followed the rewiring (tracking minus stuck 0.729 [0.552, 0.885]). The transforms, audits and raw records are available from the authors.

Title: Supply the Global, Supervise the Local: Combining Representation Conditioning and Alignment in Diffusion Transformers

  • Authors: Chaojie Ji, Sergio Quiroga-Sandoval, Ruxin Wang, Zheju Zheng, Yankai Cao
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29138
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29138
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29138]] Supply the Global, Supervise the Local: Combining Representation Conditioning and Alignment in Diffusion Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29138)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: A pretrained image representation helps train a diffusion transformer in several roles, including as an alignment target, which the model's hidden states learn to match, as in representation alignment (REPA), and as a condition, an extra input describing the image to be generated. Combined, they would give the model an input and a target, but whether they conflict is unknown. To test this, we let the condition carry only part of REPA's target: we split the representation of each image patch into an image-level (global) part, shared by all patches of the image, and a within-image (local) part, the patch's difference from the image-level part, and use one token carrying the image-level part as the condition. Then, the two roles overlap on the image-level part, where they could conflict, while only REPA supervises the within-image part, where we can see whether the token helps REPA. We find that the token and REPA together teach both the image-level part and the within-image part, the main source of REPA's FID improvement, far more than the sum of what each teaches alone, so they cooperate rather than conflict. These findings give one design rule for combining REPA with an image-level condition, supply the global, supervise the local, which we implement as Global-Condition REPA (GC-REPA): REPA with the token as an extra input; the token carries no information beyond REPA's target and is drawn at sampling from a separately trained token prior. GC-REPA greatly accelerates convergence: on ImageNet 256*256, SiT-XL/2 + GC-REPA reaches REPA's 4M- and SiT-XL/2's 7M-iteration FIDs within 200K and 100K iterations (20 and 70 fewer).

  • Authors: Savya Khosla, Chris Dongjoo Kim, Alex Schwing, Derek Hoiem, Christopher Clark, Sangho Lee
  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) tokenize images using fixed spatial grids, so visual token count grows with pixel area rather than scene content. We introduce RVLM, a VLM trained to use regions rather than patches as its visual units. Analyzing region construction for general-purpose VLMs, we find that regions must cover the full image, stay spatially connected during end-to-end training, and preserve fine structure such as small text, while semantic grouping adds little. On unseen pathology and satellite images and on video benchmarks without any video training, RVLM performs on par with the patch baseline while using 2-3$\times$ fewer visual tokens. These results establish regions as a scalable alternative to patch-based visual representations while preserving the spatial precision required for grounded prediction.

Title: ARC: Adaptive Rollout Control for Efficient Synchronous LLM Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Yihui Zhang, Tianyu Wo, Jinghao Wang, Xiao Zhou, Huitian Xue, Xiaoyang Sun, Zeying Wang, Chunming Hu, Renyu Yang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29156
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29156
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29156]] ARC: Adaptive Rollout Control for Efficient Synchronous LLM Reinforcement Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29156)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Synchronous reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models proceeds through a repeated rollout--barrier--update loop. Each update must wait for a fixed number of complete, group-relative training samples, yet individual response lengths and reward outcomes vary widely and only emerge as generation progresses. Static admission and unconditional completion therefore leave accelerators idle in some steps, and waste computation generating samples that will never contribute to the current update. We present ARC, a closed-loop rollout architecture that governs synchronous RL rollout at group granularity. ARC uses \textit{Calibrated Streaming Backfill} to judge from partial observations whether a group is still worth completing, backfilling a fresh candidate immediately when it is not; \textit{Deferred Group Completion} to park an uncertain early stop and resume it later instead of discarding it outright, bounded by a resume-cycle limit that guarantees termination; and \textit{Adaptive Admission} and \textit{Pipelining} to order admitted samples by early-measured duration and to admit further candidates only while expected supply still falls short of the target number of accepted groups. Under online evaluation, ARC reduces rollout time by $37.4\%$, a $1.60\times$ speedup, and improves effective GRPO throughput by $50.1\%$ over existing baselines, while directly recovering 98.9\% of the training groups that would ultimately have been kept.

To resolve these dual failures, we introduce FocusFlow, a segmentation-free framework that structures the integration trajectory into two disjoint phases. During early coarse structure formation, FocusFlow applies Feature-Divergence Localization (FDL) to spatially gate the velocity field using intrinsic cross-attention. Once coarse structure has formed, FocusFlow applies Reference Generation Guidance (RGG), a trajectory-correction term that drives convergence to the target shape by pulling the trajectory toward an explicit clean-latent target. Evaluated on the joint Google Scanned Objects and PartObjaverse-Tiny benchmarks, FocusFlow obtains the best preservation scores and the largest edit magnitude among compared methods, suggesting that it can reduce the traditional preservation-modification trade-off. By providing stable, localized geometric control without manual spatial annotations, this phase-scheduled approach lowers technical barriers to the modification of synthetic 3D media.

Title: Decoupling Perception from Generation for Agentic 3D CT Report Generation

  • Authors: Chenyu Wang, Mingyu Chen, Wenchao Li, Kayhan Batmanghelich
  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Chest CT reports repeatedly assess the same anatomical regions for findings and their attributes, such as whether a lung nodule is present and where it is located. Yet current CT report generation models largely follow generic vision-language modeling, which may miss subtle abnormalities when training data are limited. Title: What Makes Recurrence Generalize Across Lengths?

  • Authors: Yoonsung Jung, Sunghun Jung, Louise Nayeong Koh, Yongjun Choi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29171
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29171
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29171]] What Makes Recurrence Generalize Across Lengths?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29171)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Multi-step reasoning requires models to preserve and update intermediate information as computation unfolds. State tracking captures this fundamental requirement, but models that succeed within a fixed range of state transitions may not generalize when substantially more updates are required. Recurrent Transformers offer a natural mechanism for addressing this challenge by repeatedly applying shared Transformer blocks, allowing computation to scale with problem length. We study when such recurrent computation generalizes beyond the training range in a controlled state-tracking task, varying operation representation, positional encoding, and recurrent capacity. We find that recurrence alone is insufficient: multi-token template representations and absolute positional encoding can severely limit extrapolation, whereas atomic operation representations enable substantially stronger length generalization. The effect of positional structure also varies with recurrent depth, with periodic positional encoding achieving the strongest mean performance at greater recurrent depth. Most importantly, scaling the recurrent operator extends the extrapolation horizon, reaching 35.1% exact-match accuracy on sequences of 20-40 operations after training only on 2-10, while substantially larger fixed-depth Transformers remain at 0%. Additional recurrent computation at test time can extend the range over which the model generalizes, but does not eliminate failure on much longer sequences. These results suggest that recurrent computation can support length-generalizable state tracking, but that its effectiveness depends critically on whether the learned computation remains reusable across sequence lengths.

Title: Ziv Meets Sumcheck: Zero-Knowledge Proofs of Floating-Point LLM Inference

  • Authors: Hassan Khodaiemehr, Khadijeh Bagheri, Chen Feng
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CR, cs.IT
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29188
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29188
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29188]] Ziv Meets Sumcheck: Zero-Knowledge Proofs of Floating-Point LLM Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29188)
  • Keywords: privacy, attack, transformer
  • Abstract: Zero-knowledge proofs can show that a neural network produced a given output without revealing the network, but current systems for language models prove integer approximations of the model rather than the floating-point arithmetic that is actually deployed. Proving floating-point matrix multiplication has been regarded as hard, because rounding after every addition makes the result depend on the order of the additions. We avoid this obstacle by proving the correctly rounded product, in which every dot product is computed exactly and rounded once. This result is independent of the hardware and at least as accurate as standard kernels. We give an interactive proof for this relation whose cost is close to that of integer matrix multiplication, with roughly a thousand times fewer committed values than per-operation floating-point circuits need constraints, extend it to every layer of a transformer, and give a privacy construction for the complete argument whose zero knowledge we prove under explicit hypotheses. We implement the system and prove the full forward pass of GPT-2 small, including all twelve blocks and the output over the whole vocabulary, in 19 minutes on two processor cores; verification takes 21 seconds. The verifier rejects all 236 attacks we tested, and the model's outputs agree with high-precision inference at least as closely as standard 16-bit inference does.

Through HKS, THETA uses structural information from heat diffusion to guide both representation learning and few-shot adaptation with unlabeled target-domain data. Extensive experiments across diverse graph domains demonstrate that THETA consistently outperforms existing baselines on downstream tasks, highlighting the value of heat diffusion as a structural prior for transferable graph representations.

Title: Why Adaptive Optimizers Struggle with Large Convolution Kernels

  • Authors: Tianyu Ding, Jiachen Jiang, Jinxin Zhou, Yong Ma, Zhihui Zhu, Luming Liang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29232
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29232
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29232]] Why Adaptive Optimizers Struggle with Large Convolution Kernels(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29232)
  • Keywords: transformer, segmentation
  • Abstract: Large convolution kernels enlarge the receptive fields of ConvNets at a modest computational cost, but they are notoriously difficult to train. Existing large-kernel networks therefore rely on costly training techniques, such as parallel small-kernel branches with batch normalization. In this paper, we trace this difficulty to adaptive optimizers such as Adam, which normalize the update of every weight separately. Since input features rarely average to zero, all weights of a kernel share a common gradient, and these optimizers move the mean of a $k\times k$ kernel $k$ times as far as a single parameter. We find this mean amplification in widely used ConvNets and vision transformers, and show that it can force the maximal learning rate to fall as $1/k^2$. We further prove that the basis of the discrete cosine transform (DCT) isolates the mean and is the only orthonormal basis that diagonalizes every stationary gradient statistic with mirrored boundaries. We therefore train large kernels through their DCT coefficients and convert them back after training. The resulting method, DCT-Rep, requires only a few lines of code and adds no parameters, no inference cost and negligible training overhead. Extensive experiments on image classification, object detection, semantic segmentation and image restoration show that DCT-Rep consistently improves large-kernel ConvNets and vision transformers, by $+0.79\%$ top-1 accuracy for a large-kernel ConvNeXt-T and $+0.43\%$ for ViT-S/16 on ImageNet-1K, $+1.4$ box AP on COCO, $+1.2$ mIoU on ADE20K and up to $+0.4$ dB on image deblurring, where the gain grows with the kernel size.

Title: Revealing Semantic Gaps in Zero-Knowledge Virtual Machines via Obligation-Targeted Semantic Fuzzing

  • Authors: Hongbo Wen, Jingyu Ke, Hanzhi Liu, Yanju Chen, Yu Feng
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29260
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29260
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29260]] Revealing Semantic Gaps in Zero-Knowledge Virtual Machines via Obligation-Targeted Semantic Fuzzing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29260)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Zero-knowledge virtual machines (zkVMs) prove correct execution of programs and underpin systems securing billions of dollars in digital assets. Their security depends on alignment between the instruction set architecture (ISA) that programs target, such as RISC-V, and the constraint system, polynomial equations that check the prover's record of every intermediate value (the witness). Mismatches, or semantic gaps, come in two kinds: underconstraint bugs let a malicious prover forge a proof of an execution the ISA forbids (a soundness violation), and divergence bugs make the zkVM itself deviate from the ISA or crash, so an honest prover cannot prove a legitimate execution (a completeness violation). Both cluster at corner cases such as division by zero that conventional fuzzing almost never reaches, and underconstraint bugs leave program output unchanged. Our key insight is that these corner cases correspond to constraint obligations derived from the ISA specification and the proof system's algebraic structure. We build Beak, a reinforcement-learning fuzzer in which obligations replace three random decisions: they supply inputs that trigger each obligation, reward executions that trigger new combinations of obligations, and direct witness tampering to the step where an obligation is triggered. Beak flags divergence bugs by comparing registers against a reference RISC-V emulator, and underconstraint bugs by checking whether the constraints accept a tampered witness. On six leading production RISC-V zkVMs, Beak detects 25 of 37 known bugs (67.6%) versus 5 (13.5%) for the prior state of the art, and discovers 14 previously unknown bugs, all responsibly disclosed and patched.

It aims to maximize the utilization of information from a single sample from the perspective of structured modeling. Specifically, we first designed a Multi-Frequency Decoupling (MFD) module, which divides features into different frequency domain components, allowing each branch to focus on information expression at different semantic levels, thereby alleviating overfitting and enhancing feature representation capabilities. Secondly, we introduce a Progressive Feature Integrator (PFI) module to promote complementary fusion among multi-level features, thereby avoiding the dominant effect of a certain level of feature in the fusion process. Furthermore, we propose an Selective Context Filtering (SCF) Module that adaptively selects key contextual information by modeling the contrast relationship between the foreground and background, thereby achieving precise localization of the lesion area. Extensive experiments across seven diverse datasets validate that OB-SAM achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Title: QASparse: Query-Adaptive Sparse Attention with Tailored Budgets and Tokens

  • Authors: Zhiyuan Chen, Zihao Yang, Quanjiang Li, Haijiang Li, Jiancheng Pan, Qichao Zhao, Zihan Dong, Qiushi Shi, Jinshi Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29280
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29280
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29280]] QASparse: Query-Adaptive Sparse Attention with Tailored Budgets and Tokens(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29280)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Long-sequence inference in large language models is constrained by the high cost of prefilling, where dense attention scales quadratically with context length. Exploiting similarity across attention heads offers a way to reduce redundant computation. Prior approaches in this direction have relied on localized query observations to determine head-level computation reuse or budget allocation. However, inter-head similarity does not imply uniform query requirements, leaving adaptation to varying budget needs and selection preferences across query regions a key challenge. We introduce QASparse, a Query-Adaptive Sparse attention mechanism with hardware-efficient execution. QASparse preserves the efficiency of shared cross-head importance estimation while adapting computational budgets and token selections to query requirements, and it executes these dynamic sparse decisions through dedicated kernels. Its query adaptivity stems from two complementary innovations: Local Budget Regression models local variations in computational demand along query positions to estimate budgets for different query regions and attention heads; Boundary Voting lets queries within each head vote over candidates near the selection cutoff using actual query/key scores, refining the retained set while preserving the initial selection size. To reduce the execution overhead of dynamic budgets, we further design length-specialized selection kernels that efficiently support query-adaptive sparse patterns across context lengths. Together, these designs efficiently combine shared estimation with local decisions while adapting both how much attention is computed and which interactions are retained. Experimental results demonstrate improved benchmark scores and faster prefill attention computation compared with existing sparse attention baselines.

Title: RadioWorld: Transferring World Models to Radio-Frequency Radiance Fields

  • Authors: Hu Zhou, Jiazhou Zhou, Shuowen Zhang, Ying-Chang Liang, Liang Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.NI, eess.SP
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29282

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Electromagnetic (EM) wave propagation in the physical world constitutes a Radio-Frequency (RF) radiance field, providing rich information for 6G communication and sensing. However, existing neural RF field methods suffer from three fundamental limitations: (1) they predominantly rely on per-scene optimization and cannot generalize to unseen scenes; (2) they require costly reference RF measurements in each target scene; and (3) they degrade severely when extrapolating beyond the vicinity of reference measurements. To overcome these limitations, we present RadioWorld, a general framework that adapts a world model to synthesize RF radiance fields directly from physical scene geometry and transceiver locations, without requiring any reference RF measurements at inference. Specifically, RadioWorld couples two specialized modules within a dual-stream Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) architecture: a Scene-Understanding Transformer that encodes 3D scene geometry and transceiver locations to extract environment-aware global EM propagation features, and a Field-Generation Transformer that attends to these features to synthesize the RF radiance field with the local query conditions. We also establish UrbanRF-Bench, a large-scale ray-traced benchmark spanning 657 urban scenes and 32,850 RF radiance fields across seven global cities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RadioWorld significantly outperforms specialized baselines and accurately synthesizes RF fields at arbitrary locations in unseen cities. Code, datasets, and checkpoints will be released upon acceptance.

Title: Robust Concept Erasure in Text-to-Image Models via Simulating Brain Functional Localization

  • Abstract: Concept erasure in text-to-image models aims to prevent a target concept from appearing in generated images while preserving general image generation capability. To address this gap, we introduce IVO (Initial latent Variable Optimization), a new concept reactivation attack that changes the starting point of image generation by optimizing the initial latent. Across U-Net diffusion and flow-matching transformer architectures, CeFlune achieves a favorable balance between concept erasure and general image generation capability in comparisons with up to 15 baseline methods. Title: From Vanishing Gradients to Recursive Self-Improvement: Evaluating the Most Influential Papers in Large Language Model Evolution (1997-2026)

  • Authors: Saadh Jawwadh
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29303
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29303

  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: The rapid proliferation of Large Language Model (LLM) research makes it increasingly difficult to distinguish incremental refinements from true paradigm shifts. This review evaluates 22 of the most influential publications spanning nearly three decades (1997-2026), organized through a causal, chronologically anchored taxonomy centered on the author's birth year (1999). Beginning "before 1999" with how recurrent neural networks (RNNs) forgot earlier context (much like forgetting Chapter 1 by the time a reader reaches Chapter 9), we trace how Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks introduced a "bouncer" mechanism of forget, input, and output gates to selectively preserve long-term memory. We then examine how Word2Vec turned language into vector mathematics (king - male + female ~ queen), why sequential RNNs hit a parallelization wall, and how the Transformer's all-to-all attention weights launched the modern LLM era alongside a quadratic bottleneck. Next, we analyze why scaling parameters alone leaves capacity under-trained unless data scales equally (Chinchilla), how Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) left alignment superficial, and how that gap compounded into adversarial jailbreaks and single-direction refusal subspaces (J-space), where instructing a model not to think of a restricted concept surfaces it much like the human "red ball" paradox. Finally, we synthesize the current state of AI (2024-2026), encompassing recursive model collapse, autonomous reasoning-model jailbreak agents, machine-speed agentic intrusions, and closed-loop recursive self-improvement (AIDE^2).

Title: Reward Tilting via Semantic Noising

  • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Steering a generative model toward a desired property typically requires separate fine-tuning for each reward or additional computation during sampling. We propose semantic noising, which conditions generation on a noisy semantic representation of the data. The resulting posteriors correspond to exponential tilts by linear-quadratic rewards in semantic space, allowing a single model to support objectives and strengths chosen after training. A conditional generator can be trained to approximate these posteriors directly, while models that generate semantic representations by diffusion provide analytic tilted denoisers for the same family of rewards. We illustrate semantic noising in three settings. An autoregressive molecular generator trained with semantic noising controls properties individually and jointly, with a better reward--KL trade-off than property-conditioned models. On ImageNet, a joint diffusion model of images and their CLIP embeddings achieves FID comparable to REG, and tilting it toward a higher aesthetic score matches the best of 16 samples at the cost of a single sample. Finally, pretrained factorized models such as Kandinsky 2.2 support the same tilts without retraining.

Title: Probe Before You Learn: Allocating Prompts from Initial Gradients under Label Noise

  • Authors: Jiazhe Zhang, Zibin Geng, Qiang Ma, Xuefeng Jiang, Yijun Lin, Jingyi Wang, Yu Yan, Xingzhou Zhang, Min Liu, Yuwei Wang, Sheng Sun
  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29336
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29336]] Probe Before You Learn: Allocating Prompts from Initial Gradients under Label Noise(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29336)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Prompt learning provides a parameter-efficient way to adapt pretrained vision--language models through text and visual prompts, but noisy labels prevalent in real-world data inevitably bias prompt updates and thereby undermine adaptation effectiveness. Prior work on robust prompt learning focuses on suppressing unreliable updates under noisy supervision. We uncover a pivotal yet overlooked aspect--prompt allocation: shifting the parameter budget between text and visual prompts can substantially enhance robustness while keeping the training objective and total budget fixed. However, suitable prompt allocations vary across tasks and are unknown a priori, rendering brute-force candidate training computationally prohibitive. We propose GPrompt, a training-free method that derives allocation between text and visual prompts using only loss gradients at initialization before prompt optimization. Using a small sample of images and generated pseudo-labels as shared supervision, GPrompt performs an initialization probe to extract paired text and visual loss gradients, and characterizes the geometry of their trace-normalized Gram matrices through both concentration within each modality and overlap between modalities. GPrompt then derives a closed-form allocation, and projects it onto feasible prompt counts under the exact parameter budget, enabling task-specific selection without training candidate configurations. Experiments across eleven datasets show that GPrompt outperforms the strongest baseline by up to 7.75% while selecting configurations close to the best allocation identified through exhaustive training. The average runtime of gradient probe across datasets is 99.9% lower than that of exhaustive candidate training.

Title: NPUFHE: An Efficient CKKS Execution System for Ascend NPUs

  • Authors: Ke Changke, Jian Wang, Xuan Xu, Yijing Zong, Jiqiang Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29353
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29353
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29353]] NPUFHE: An Efficient CKKS Execution System for Ascend NPUs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29353)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) enables computation directly on encrypted data, but its substantial computational cost hinders adoption in privacy-preserving artificial intelligence (AI) applications. Neural processing units (NPUs) already deployed in AI infrastructure provide a practical platform for FHE acceleration. However, after mapping the number theoretic transform (NTT) to matrix multiplications on Ascend NPUs, modular arithmetic and representation conversion on the Vector backend still incur substantial overhead at stage boundaries. Evaluation-key material of key switching also increases storage and data-transfer costs, and whole-limb task assignment causes tail load imbalance. We present NPUFHE, an end-to-end CKKS execution system optimized for Ascend NPUs. To reduce modular recovery and twiddle costs in NTT, we propose Boundary-Optimized NTT, which eliminates intra-axis modular recovery, compiles intra-axis twiddle factors into matrix constants, and introduces two-level folded modular recovery at axis endpoints. To alleviate evaluation-key storage and transfer pressure, we design Compact Shoup encoding, which halves the key representation through local coefficient recovery. Finally, we propose Cost-Aware Parallel Tiling to mitigate tail load imbalance caused by whole-limb task assignment. It uses a cost model to adaptively partition tail tasks and smooth stepwise latency increases. We integrate the above designs into OpenFHE. On one Ascend 910B4, full-packing CKKS bootstrapping with real evaluation keys takes 0.95 s, encrypted CIFAR-10 ResNet-20 inference with bootstrapping requires 9.87 s per image, and encrypted MNIST inference achieves an amortized latency of 19.81 ms per image. The peak NTT throughput of NPUFHE reaches up to $184.22\times$, $2.04\times$, and $4.46\times$ that of OpenFHE on CPU, TensorFHE, and CROSS, respectively.

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29369]] ClothCast: Generative Cloth World Modeling under Partial Observability(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29369)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: World models support robotic manipulation by predicting how the scene evolves under a planned action. For garments this prediction is notoriously difficult: deformations are complex, and self-occlusion leaves much of the cloth unobserved. Planned gripper trajectories provide a useful motion prior because cloth held by a gripper moves with it, while the remaining deformation must be predicted. We introduce ClothCast, a world model that applies this prior to the cloth's points and learns the residual motion. ClothCast takes one RGB-D observation, the camera pose, the robot's own model and the planned gripper trajectories, with no cloth mesh or material parameters. It lifts the observation to a layered point set that includes hypothesized occluded layers, carries the points with a blended rigid prior derived from the gripper poses, and predicts the residual motion with a video-diffusion point-flow model. Over entire 4- to 11-second episodes at 10 Hz, the moved points are rendered into depth, from which a geometry-conditioned completion model synthesizes RGB; after release, a hand-designed settling step replaces the learned motion. We further release ClothCastBench, 10,000 simulated dual-arm cloth-manipulation episodes with the ground-truth cloth mesh, including occluded layers, at every frame. On 492 held-out episodes, ClothCast achieves 2.5x lower sequence-averaged visible-surface Chamfer distance than Flex-{\pi}, the strongest baseline in geometry (14.0 mm vs. 35.6 mm), and 3.7 dB higher cloth-region PSNR than Cosmos-Predict2.5, the strongest baseline in cloth PSNR. On a 50-episode ablation subset, the rigid prior alone, applied to ClothCast's learned initial geometry with the same post-processing, already outperforms both geometry-capable baselines, and the learned residual lowers its error by about a third.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29393
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29393]] Can Speech LLMs Recognize When Audio Evidence Is Insufficient? A Diagnostic Study and a Pre-Generation Probing Mitigation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29393)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Speech large language models are usually evaluated on questions that have answers. Reliance on synthesized speech has been named as a limitation of audio benchmarks but not quantified; we quantify it, and show it large enough to reverse a finding. Since no prompt we tested repairs the residual failure, we transfer pre-generation attention probing to the multimodal setting. A probe over frozen prompt representations reaches 0.773 AUROC at predicting which unanswerable questions the model will answer anyway, where semantic entropy over ten sampled generations is at chance: the model fabricates the same answer every time, so sample disagreement cannot see a failure it is confident about.

  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29468

  • Keywords: robust, extraction, watermark
  • Abstract: Robust watermarking for panoramic imagery is impeded by the topological mismatch between spherical signals and planar representations. Conventional methods fail under $SO(3)$ viewing rotations due to non-linear geometric warping and struggle with viewport extraction, which typically necessitates computationally expensive inverse remapping. To address these challenges, we propose RSWP, a watermarking framework grounded in icosahedral group theory. Leveraging the property that the icosahedral group ($\mathcal{I}_{60}$) serves as the largest discrete subgroup of $SO(3)$, we decompose spherical rotations into tractable local planar rotations and combinatorial facet permutations. By employing an equivariant feature extractor followed by a permutation-invariant sorting module, this paradigm rigorously synchronizes rotation-distorted features. Moreover, the dual-extractor design inherently supports direct, blind watermark retrieval from partial 2D renderings without geometric calibration. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RSWP achieves state-of-the-art robustness against continuous $SO(3)$ rotations and viewport transformations while preserving high visual fidelity.

Title: OnCache: Learning Dynamic Caching Schedules for Efficient Diffusion Inference

  • Authors: Zhikang Xie, Xichen Ye, Yifan Wu, Yuxuan Liu, Qianlong Wang, Haoshen Yu, Peizhu Gong, Weizhong Zhang, Cheng Jin
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29480

  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Diffusion models have revolutionized generative tasks but incur high inference latency due to iterative denoising. Cache-based strategies accelerate inference by reusing intermediate features, yet they largely rely on static, sample-agnostic schedules. Our empirical analysis reveals two factors that these schedules overlook. (i) denoising dynamics and computational requirements vary across prompts, motivating adaptive computation allocation. (ii) sensitivity to caching errors varies across timesteps, so static schedules may reuse features at error-sensitive steps or waste computation at less sensitive ones. We therefore propose OnCache, a dynamic caching framework that makes prompt-adaptive decisions online about how much computation to allocate and when to refresh cached features. We further extend OnCache to video generation on Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B, achieving state-of-the-art efficiency--quality trade-offs across both modalities.

Title: CAR: Reducing Decoder Output Shift in Visual Token Pruning via Coverage Selection and Attention Reallocation

  • Authors: Xixi Jiang, Pingcheng Dong, Chen Yang, Xin Yang, Kwang-Ting Cheng
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29489
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29489
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29489]] CAR: Reducing Decoder Output Shift in Visual Token Pruning via Coverage Selection and Attention Reallocation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29489)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Visual token pruning is an effective way to accelerate multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing methods degrade substantially under aggressive pruning, especially on visual grounding (VG). We analyze the sources of this degradation from the perspective of the output shift that pruning causes in decoder layers, and decompose this shift in closed form into two terms, an image summary error and an attention allocation error. The summary error stems from the loss of visual information, and the allocation error from the drop in the share of attention assigned to visual tokens. To reduce both errors, we propose CAR (Coverage selection and Attention Reallocation), a token pruning framework that restores attention share while maintaining information. Specifically, to minimize the summary error, whose upper bound tightens as the retained tokens better cover all visual tokens, our coverage selection retains tokens that are both salient and non-redundant, with saliency from encoder heads chosen by a label-free probe. To minimize the allocation error, our attention reallocation reassigns the attention of the discarded tokens to the retained ones through a closed-form logit bias. On Qwen2.5-VL-7B with $90\%$ of the visual tokens pruned, CAR retains $93.0\%$ of the unpruned accuracy on visual question answering (VQA) and $77.9\%$ on VG, against $26.5\%$ for the best existing method on VG; the logit bias alone raises the VG accuracy of existing methods by up to $24$ points. Experiments on multiple MLLMs across VQA, VG and video understanding show that CAR generalizes well and consistently outperforms existing methods.

Title: Adaptation or Poisoning? Security Risks of Conversational Test-Time Learning in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Honghao Feng, Shanhan Qu, Kai Tong, Shuquan Man, Huiping Zhuang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29532
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29532
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29532]] Adaptation or Poisoning? Security Risks of Conversational Test-Time Learning in Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29532)
  • Keywords: security, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Test-time learning (TTL) enables large language models to adapt to unlabeled inputs during deployment. The model updates a lightweight adapter by minimizing the next-token prediction loss on the input it receives. When these updates persist across conversation turns, every user query serves both as the request answered in the current turn and as adaptation data for later turns. By choosing the queries used for adaptation, an attacker can influence parameter updates and weaken safety alignment through ordinary interactions with a TTL chat service. To demonstrate this risk, we introduce Progressive Test-Time Adaptation Poisoning (P-TAP), a query-only multi-turn attack that jailbreaks the model by poisoning its session adapter through the service's own updates. A sequence of escalating adversarial queries shapes the adapter turn by turn; the final harmful request is then answered under the accumulated state without itself being used for adaptation. Across four open-weight instruction-tuned models, enabling updates raises attack success rates on average by 13.00 percentage points under greedy decoding and by 22.25 points under best-of-10 sampling, relative to matched sessions receiving the same queries without updates. Answering adversarial queries but adapting on unrelated text or on benign text about the same topic removes most of this increase. Among existing safeguards, screening incoming queries flags every attack stream before the final request, while a perplexity-based detector of unsafe adaptation flags none of the successful attacks.

  • Abstract: Quantization is the standard way to shrink large language models, and it is usually judged by perplexity and task accuracy. We ask a different question: after quantization, which words can the model still say at all? A token is silenced when no hidden state can make it the top choice of the output layer, so a greedy decoder can never produce it. We give a simple, scalable way to estimate how many tokens a quantized output head silences, combining three checks: a direction that lets a token win (so it is safe), an upper bound on how close a token's row is to being trapped inside the others, and an exact match that proves a token can never win. Each count is therefore a certified range: exact matches give its lower end and flagged tokens can only raise its upper end. In nearly all settings we report down to 3 bits the two ends coincide, so the counts are exact. Across three open models we find that quantization silences tokens: on large-vocabulary models it already appears at 8-bit precision, up to a few hundred previously reachable tokens on a 1B-parameter model, and grows as precision drops, while a small-vocabulary model resists until 3 bits. The loss is far from uniform across languages: on a multilingual vocabulary the silenced tokens are overwhelmingly non-Latin (Korean, Chinese, Thai, Arabic and Hebrew), even though Latin tokens dominate the vocabulary. Silencing is thus a measurable, language-sensitive side effect that standard averages miss, and it should be reported alongside them.

Title: ParseGap: Testing What Coding Agents Authorize and What Shells Execute

  • Authors: Jieyu Li
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29546

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: LLM coding agents authorize shell commands by parsing a command string against a policy and passing the raw string to a system shell, so the gate adjudicates one reading while the shell executes another. We present ParseGap, a differential testing framework that runs each command through an unmodified vendor checker and GNU Bash 5.2 in an isolated container, flagging a violation whenever an approved command produces policy-violating runtime effects. Across eight production coding agents, ParseGap identifies 17 root causes across three divergence boundaries: at the parse, where an upstream tree-sitter-bash defect hides a second command from Mistral Vibe and Gemini CLI; at the utility, where every checker permits read-only binaries whose arguments write files or launch programs (9-47 of 55 cases); and at confinement, where an allow rule in OpenAI Codex CLI disables its read-only OS sandbox.
