To eliminate the second reading, we design ShellPlan, which compiles a strict Bash subset once into a typed execution plan, validates utility arguments against positive capability specifications, and dispatches approved plans directly via execve without a shell. Over a formal operational model, we prove single-interpretation dispatch and bound touched programs and files under audited assumptions. ShellPlan admitted 0/4,174 active attacks across six static suites and 0/90 hits in a ten-model agent benchmark (62/90 unguarded) at 1.28 ms median gate latency, preserving 36/36 (100%) frontier standard-dev completion (74/100 overall) and 0/348 external over-allows outside modeled exceptions.

Title: Align the Pair, Not the Sample: Representation Alignment for Faster and Better One-Step Generato

  • Authors: Mingxiao Li, Xuantuo Huang, Encheng Su, Aoran Wang, Han Zhang, Jinouwen Zhang, Shixiang Tang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29547
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29547
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29547]] Align the Pair, Not the Sample: Representation Alignment for Faster and Better One-Step Generato(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29547)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Representation alignment markedly accelerates diffusion training, but one-step generators raise a question that diffusion does not: \emph{which training path should receive the loss?} A one-step generator sees two kinds of inputs. Corrupted real images come with their clean counterpart, which provides an instance-paired target. Samples generated from pure noise have no corresponding real image, yet this is where the final model operates. We study this question on Priming, which trains one-step generators from scratch by reconstructing corrupted images and then matching feature distributions with optimal transport. Aligning the generative path, the natural choice, collapses recall ($0.45\!\to\!0.15$--$0.25$) in all 17 configurations we test, including five target constructions and loss weights down to $0.05$. Aligning the paired reconstruction path with a frozen DINOv2 encoder instead helps in every setting we test. A short analysis explains this asymmetry: when the target carries no instance information about the input, the optimal aligned feature is constant within each class, so the loss pulls samples toward class prototypes. A control confirms this: aligning the paired path to a random same-class image instead of the input's own image removes most of the gain. Inside the generator, paired-path alignment creates an interface block through which post-training routes noise, and the image semantics carried through it predict sample quality across 16 models. The resulting recipe, \method, accelerates \emph{both} training stages on ImageNet $256\!\times\!256$: it matches the 160-epoch baseline with $2\times$ fewer priming epochs, surpasses its final quality with $4\times$ fewer post-training updates, and lowers FID to 1.30 (B) and 1.155 (L) at the standard budget. A joint single-stage variant trains native one-step generators and reaches FID 1.22 with 115M parameters.

Title: How Multi-Timescale Memory Cools Optimization: Theory and Evidence from AdEMAMix

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29588

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Augmenting AdamW with multiple momentum timescales improves large language model pretraining, yet the mechanism driving these gains remains poorly understood. We identify an interaction between multi-timescale optimizer memory and the geometry of normalized networks: memory changes parameter norms and thereby controls how fast parameters rotate. Title: Mass Forcing: On-Policy Mass-Aware Distillation for Autoregressive Video Generation

  • Authors: Yiyu Li, Fei Wei, Yong Wang, Zaiquan Yang, Gerhard Petrus Hancke, Xiangxiang Chu, Rynson W.H. Lau
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29618
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29618
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29618]] Mass Forcing: On-Policy Mass-Aware Distillation for Autoregressive Video Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29618)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) has emerged as a promising paradigm for real-time video synthesis, distilling pretrained diffusion models into few-step autoregressive generators. However, its reverse-KL objective is mode-seeking and can collapse the student's conditional video distribution onto a narrow subset of modes, leading to reduced sample diversity, diminished temporal dynamics, and color oversaturation. Existing methods mitigate this issue mainly via coverage-oriented student initialization, but we observe that such coverage is transient: across three initializations, the fraction of dynamic videos drops sharply during subsequent DMD training, indicating that the mode-seeking objective, not the initialization, drives this degradation. We propose Mass Forcing, a probability-mass-aware framework that moves on-policy DMD supervision from whole videos to individual states. Each update branches several continuations from a shared student-generated prefix, making the student's probability mass at that state measurable against the teacher. First, Mass Forcing estimates relative teacher-to-student density ratios among these continuations through latent-space score integration and uses them to rebalance conditional DMD updates, amplifying rollouts underrepresented relative to the teacher. Second, since mismatched mode probabilities do not necessarily induce strong local score corrections, it aligns generated rollout sets with a reweighted reference distribution defined by the same mass estimates, providing cross-rollout supervision. Experiments on MovieGen and VBench show that Mass Forcing improves video fidelity and sustains temporal dynamics throughout training, achieving the highest Total scores among compared methods and a Dynamic Degree of 83% after 1,000 generator updates, versus 12-32% for the baselines, with no additional inference-time overhead.

  • Abstract: We study language acquisition by transformers trained on the Random Language Model: a synthetic hierarchical grammar with random production rules, whose sparsity is controlled by a single parameter and whose conditional entropies are known exactly. Title: Loop-Count Schedules in Looped Transformers: An Empirical Study

  • Authors: Konstantin Kolokolov
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29704
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29704
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29704]] Loop-Count Schedules in Looped Transformers: An Empirical Study(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29704)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped transformers apply one block of layers several times with shared weights, trading parameters for repeated computation. We study how the schedule of the loop iteration count R during training determines whether each extra pass acts as a full layer or as a small correction, using a controlled ladder of 46M-110M-parameter models trained on 2B tokens with learned residual gates on every loop pass and three schedules: R fixed to 2, R sampled uniformly from {1,...,6}, and R sampled from {2,3,4}. We find that the lower bound of the sampling range is the decisive choice. If the sampled range includes R=1, so that some training steps run the block only once and compute the loss on that single pass, the gates collapse to 0.1-0.5 and each additional pass becomes a small correction to the first; if the range starts at R=2, so that every training step runs the block at least twice, the gates stay at 0.6-0.9 and each pass keeps the strength of a full layer, while the model can still be run at any R from 2 up to twice the largest trained value with validation loss within a few hundredths of a nat of its best. The {2, 3, 4} schedule gives the best looped model, 2.909 nats against 2.951 for the parameter-matched plain model, and degrades by only 0.038 nats when run at twice its trained depth. We further show that under a fixed R the direction of the residual stream converges geometrically beyond the trained depth while the loss diverges, which refines a recent finding that static-depth training does not converge, and that reusing the first pass's keys and values on later passes costs 0.01-0.03 nats in every regime while making the per-token iteration uniformly contractive and halving the inference cache. The loop recovers about a third of what real depth provides at equal parameters. The code, trained weights, and per-token traces are released.

Title: Lightweight Spectral Subspace Filtering for Trojan-Resilient AI Inference Engines

  • Authors: Soumita Chatterjee, Sudip Ghosh, Tamal Ghosh, Bhargab B. Bhattacharya, Hafizur Rahaman

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29734]] Lightweight Spectral Subspace Filtering for Trojan-Resilient AI Inference Engines(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29734)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack, robust, steal, transformer
  • Abstract: Backdoor attacks pose a significant security threat to deep neural networks (DNNs) deployed on AI inference engines and hardware accelerators. By embedding hidden triggers during training, these attacks induce malicious behavior while maintaining high accuracy on benign inputs. Multi-trigger backdoor attacks are particularly challenging to detect and mitigate because of the distributed and stealthy nature of their effects. To address this challenge, this paper proposes a training-free Spectral Subspace Filtering framework that detects and suppresses backdoor-induced activations by analyzing latent feature representations. Exploiting the geometric separation between benign and malicious feature distributions, the framework employs Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) to identify the dominant malicious subspace and performs inference-time spectral scrubbing through activation projection. This approach suppresses backdoor effects without model retraining, requiring only a small set of triggered samples collected offline. Experimental evaluation across CNN and transformer architectures, including AlexNet, ResNet18, VGG16, MobileNetV2, and Vision Transformer (ViT), demonstrates that the proposed method reduces the attack success rate (ASR) from 94.2-99.8% to an average of 2.07%, while preserving clean accuracy of up to 98.54% with low inference overhead. The framework also demonstrates robustness across varying poisoning rates and multiple concurrent triggers, highlighting its potential as a lightweight runtime defense for enhancing the security and reliability of AI inference engines against backdoor attacks.

  • Abstract: Despite the strong performance of modern medical image segmentation models, their predictions can still contain localized errors that compromise segmentation quality. While interactive segmentation models enable targeted corrections, identifying potential errors, determining where to intervene, and evaluating corrective actions remain challenging. Moreover, each correction changes the current mask, requiring subsequent error diagnosis and recommendations to adapt accordingly. To address these challenges, we propose MISeg-Agent, a human-in-the-loop agentic framework that diagnoses segmentation errors and provides evidence-based correction recommendations. MISeg-Agent first selects a suitable pretrained segmentation model based on benchmark performance and computational requirements. Given its predicted mask, the framework identifies potential missing and excess foreground regions and recommends suspicious slices and corrective points for review. The framework then evaluates candidate point prompts using an interactive segmentation model and presents the resulting corrections for human verification before applying them. By operating directly on images and predicted masks, the error diagnosis process remains independent of the underlying segmentation model architecture. Evaluations on CT datasets demonstrate that MISeg-Agent improves initial segmentation results and produces final masks closely aligned with reference annotations. By integrating adaptive error diagnosis with validated correction recommendations, MISeg-Agent provides an interpretable approach to identifying and reducing residual errors in existing medical image segmentations.

Title: DREAM: Dual-Memory Reasoning for Anticipatory 3D Perception in Autonomous Driving

  • Authors: Sriram Mandalika, Athira Nambiar
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29753
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29753
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29753]] DREAM: Dual-Memory Reasoning for Anticipatory 3D Perception in Autonomous Driving(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29753)
  • Keywords: robust, segmentation
  • Abstract: Autonomous driving systems must not only interpret the current scene but also anticipate perceptual challenges such as occlusions, sparse observations, and recurring failure patterns. However, most existing LiDAR semantic segmentation methods operate in a frame-centric manner, processing each scan independently and discarding past contextual experience, which limits robustness under dynamic scene changes. We propose DREAM, a Dual-memory REAsoning framework for continual LiDAR seMantic segmentation that reframes perception as an experience-driven and anticipatory process. DREAM maintains two complementary memory banks in a strictly online setting: a latent memory that stores compact semantic abstractions of previously observed scenes, and an error memory that records representations associated with uncertain predictions. At each timestep, relevant memory entries are retrieved via cosine similarity and integrated into the feature space through a lightweight modulation mechanism, enabling the model to reinforce consistent semantic patterns while suppressing recurring failure modes. The backbone remains frozen and no past scans are replayed, ensuring computational efficiency and bounded memory growth. Extensive experiments on multiple large-scale LiDAR benchmarks demonstrate that DREAM achieves state-of-the-art performance, with consistent improvements on dynamic and small-scale objects, highlighting the effectiveness of persistent and error-aware memory for robust long-horizon perception.

We also show that the proposed solution has minimal impact on the user's throughput and round-trip time.

Title: probly: Uncertainty-Aware Machine Learning

  • Authors: Paul Hofman, Clemens Damke, Timo Löhr, Santo M. A. R. Thies, Alireza Javanmardi, Valentin Margraf, Jakub Paplham, Yusuf Sale, Eyke Hüllermeier, Maximilian Muschalik

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: As machine learning systems are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, the question of how to represent and quantify uncertainty has moved from a methodological side issue to a central concern. In this paper, we present probly, a Python package that addresses these issues in a single, modular framework. probly offers (i) lightweight transformations that turn existing models into uncertainty-aware ones, currently supporting PyTorch, scikit-learn, and Flax, (ii) several representations, including second-order distributions, credal sets, and conformal prediction, (iii) the corresponding quantification measures, and (iv) a number of evaluation protocols, which can be combined more or less arbitrarily. In addition, and unlike existing benchmarks, we evaluate suitable methods on first-order data, i.e., datasets for which the target itself is a distribution over outcomes. We further illustrate the flexibility of the package on a number of less standard case studies, including large language models, graph neural networks, and data streams. The package is publicly available at this https URL.

Title: Selecting Stable Sampling Dynamics for Compositional Diffusion: A Feynman-Kac Weight Reduction

  • Authors: Sunjin Kim, Sungsoo Ahn, Michael Chertkov
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cond-mat.stat-mech, cs.AI, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29764
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29764
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29764]] Selecting Stable Sampling Dynamics for Compositional Diffusion: A Feynman-Kac Weight Reduction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29764)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Given $K$ separately trained diffusion models, how should one sample from a $\beta_k$-weighted product of their terminal laws without jointly retraining the experts? Existing Feynman--Kac constructions provide many weighted stochastic differential equations (SDEs) with the correct target marginal. The consequential question is how to select useful proposal dynamics among them. We study an explicit family of weighted diffusions that realizes the same product-of-intermediate-experts trajectory. A scalar parameter $\lambda$ controls how strongly the proposal drift follows the composed expert score, while an additional Langevin component can be varied independently. Writing $B=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_k>0$, we identify the distinguished choice $\lambda=1/B$. At this value, the aggregate squared-score term in the instantaneous Feynman--Kac log-weight rate cancels exactly. For positive expert exponents, the remaining score-disagreement contribution is non-positive. We call this structural cancellation Feynman--Kac reduction. Under bounded-divergence assumptions, it yields an upper bound on the instantaneous unnormalized log-weight rate for all $\lambda\leq 1/B$. For linear diffusion protocols with compactly supported expert data, $\lambda=1/B$ yields two-sided bounds at every nonterminal time, for exponents of either sign. A corresponding result holds for a single expert under the same divergence assumption. In these two-sided settings, the selected value is unique within the specified family in a universal worst-case sense. Experiments on Gaussian mixtures and molecular sampling compare the analytically predicted stability with actual sampling quality, including instances in which parameter choices different from $\lambda=1/B$ perform better empirically.

  • Authors: Víctor Yeste, Candida Maria Greco, Paolo Rosso, Andrea Tagarelli

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Source cues are central to political communication, yet reception studies with large language model (LLM) personas as synthetic audiences have not tested whether personas respond to who speaks and not only to what is said. Our findings demonstrate that LLM personas evaluate political speakers through systematic, value-congruent priors: however, while they replicate human candidate rank-orders across survey barometers, this alignment is ordinal rather than quantitative, highlighting their utility for controlled source-effect experiments though not as substitutes for empirical public opinion surveys.

  • Abstract: AI-generated video of politicians can pass for real footage, and once such fakes are common, real footage can be dismissed as fake. We introduce CivicBench, with 10,800 generated clips of invented politicians from eight open generators and 2,632 public-domain or openly licensed real clips. It reaches .941 AUC on CivicBench. It also keeps .668 of generated clips flagged at 2 gray levels, against at most .289 for published detectors. When real clips are framed at the same 2 gray levels, Tau-Anchor's false alarms rise only from 8% to 14%, whereas four published detectors flag nearly every framed real clip at just 1 gray level.   • Abstract: Conditional generation under constraints that can only be checked on a finished sample, such as a molecular property filter, remains challenging for diffusion models: most guidance methods require differentiable measurement operators, paired conditional data, or rollouts of the base model at every step. Title: Relativising Integrity and Robustness: Prompt Injection and Partitioned Enclaves

  • Authors: Brijesh Dongol
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29802
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29802
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29802]] Relativising Integrity and Robustness: Prompt Injection and Partitioned Enclaves(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29802)
  • Keywords: security, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Relative security (RS) judges a system against a reference system, but has only been applied to confidentiality. We introduce relative integrity (RI), which states that every behaviour a system exhibits under an attacker's influence must be one the reference can exhibit for the same trusted inputs. RS and RI each choose their own reference runs, so together they accept attacks whose leak and effects the reference reproduces only in different runs. We therefore develop a further property, relative robustness (RR), which asks the same runs to reproduce both and is strictly stronger than RS and RI together. We give proof methods for RI and RR and a disproof method for RI, and apply them in two domains: (1) LLM agents under prompt injection, where the reference runs the same plan with a model that never obeys untrusted content, and (2) C programs partitioned across an SGX or TrustZone enclave boundary, where the reference keeps every function inside. We evaluate the properties on models of five published attacks, a support desk, an OP-TEE HOTP token and common agent defences. Two cases pass RS and RI but fail RR: an injection that makes the support desk's model accept any customer, as one input did in the Tensor Trust game, and the simulation step of the attack on Secret Network, in which an SGX enclave reveals a transfer's outcome without committing it. Our work is fully mechanised in Isabelle/HOL, with the enclave programs verified in C using AutoCorres2.

Title: Model Immunization Cannot Reliably Protect Models from Harmful Adaptation

  • Authors: Tong Li, Chenru Wang, Chris Thomas, Shengwei An
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29815
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29815
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29815]] Model Immunization Cannot Reliably Protect Models from Harmful Adaptation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29815)
  • Keywords: security, protect, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Efficient fine-tuning techniques have substantially reduced the cost of adapting open-weight models, enabling malicious actors to readily fine-tune them toward harmful capabilities. In response, model immunization modifies a pretrained checkpoint to resist subsequent adaptation toward designated harmful capabilities while preserving its utility and adaptability for benign tasks. Furthermore, a few immunization methods have sought to keep their protection robust to some changes in the attacker's adaptation. However, fine-tuning offers attackers many more low-cost options, and we observe that existing methods still substantially overfit to the attacker configuration they are optimized against. Such overfitting stays hidden when immunization is evaluated under, or near, this configuration, so the reported protection may give a false sense of security. To rigorously evaluate model immunization, we conduct experiments, varying one component of this configuration at a time: the harmful data, the adaptation strategy, or the model initialization. Across four immunization methods on text-to-image generation and image classification, with additional results on language models, these variations substantially degrade the protection, and in many cases the immunized model relearns the harmful capability nearly as well as the un-immunized model. Existing immunization therefore cannot reliably protect models, leaving them vulnerable to harmful adaptation.

Title: The Temporal Residual: Fast and Effective Retrofitting of Latent Feedback into Pretrained Language Models

  • Authors: Ziyang Cai, Xingyu Zhu, Sanjeev Arora
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29843
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29843
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29843]] The Temporal Residual: Fast and Effective Retrofitting of Latent Feedback into Pretrained Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29843)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A transformer language model computes a deep hidden state for every token but uses it only to sample that token. Existing latent-feedback designs, which feed hidden states back across tokens, are mostly built in during pretraining and gain little when added to a pretrained model. We introduce the temporal residual, a second residual stream that every sublayer reads and writes and that is carried from each token to the next. It adds 2,752 parameters to a 4.2B-parameter model and starts exactly at the pretrained network. Retrofitted onto Qwen3.5-4B-Base with 5B tokens of training, it beats a standard model trained on the same data by 7, 13 and 5 points on AIME 2024, AIME 2025 and AMC 2023, writes shorter solutions, and stays ahead at matched training compute. The separate stream matters: prior designs that feed state into the existing stream recover at most 40% of the gain when retrofitted. Validation loss can mislead: the recipe with the lowest loss gains nothing at decoding. Trained with filler tokens in place of reasoning, the model reasons in its hidden state: its GSM8K accuracy with no visible reasoning rises from 21% to 40%, and falls back when the carried state is zeroed.

Title: World Models Are the Right Foundation for Simulating Mars Terraforming

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Terraforming Mars is a long-horizon, action-conditioned control problem that requires predicting atmospheric responses to interventions over centuries to identify effective policies. This position paper argues that physics-regularized recurrent state-space world models are the most promising foundation for this setting. Data-driven climate emulators lack action-conditioning, and physics-based general circulation models (GCMs) are too expensive for policy search. Among learned world models, recurrent state-space architectures with physics constraints are better suited than transformer-based alternatives: they provide native uncertainty quantification through their stochastic-deterministic decomposition, remain stable under sparse non-terrestrial training data, and enforce physical consistency at each rollout step. Title: Comparative Analysis of Machine Learning Classifiers for Lightweight IoT Intrusion Detection Using Feature Reduction

  • Authors: Shaker Nawasra
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29869
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29869

  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Machine learning-based intrusion detection systems can improve IoT security, but high-dimensional traffic features and imbalanced datasets may reduce model reliability and deployment feasibility in resource-constrained environments. The original multi-class attack labels were converted into two classes: Benign and Malicious. To reduce class bias, malicious traffic was randomly undersampled to obtain an approximately balanced binary dataset. Title: Towards Agentic Video Corpus Moment Retrieval

  • Authors: Chenwei Xu, Shang Wu, Zhenyu Pan, Jianshu Zhang, Pranav Maneriker, Lie Lu, Fan Du, Manling Li, Han Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29877
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29877
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29877]] Towards Agentic Video Corpus Moment Retrieval(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29877)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Video corpus moment retrieval (VCMR) requires finding the video and time interval that match a text query in a corpus of videos. We present RELAY, a training-free framework for VCMR that shares memory across queries. RELAY processes a batch of queries in three stages: search with a query bank, ranking with video notes, and localization with video prompt caching. In the search stage, an embedding model scores each video by its best-matching frame. The query bank penalizes videos that score highly for many other queries in the batch. In the ranking stage, a vision-language model (VLM) writes notes on each candidate video's scenes, people, objects, and actions once. Then, a large language model (LLM) reranks the candidates from these notes. In the localization stage, a VLM predicts the time interval of the moment in the top-ranked video. When multiple queries select the same video, prompt caching lets the VLM reuse its frames. On the three VERIFIED benchmarks, RELAY outperforms the trained baselines in video retrieval R@1 without training. Without any labeled moment, its predicted intervals are shorter than the annotated ones. In VCMR, it achieves similar performance to VideoSearch-R1 on one benchmark and trails it at IoU 0.5 on the other two. Human annotators also mark shorter moments than the original annotations of the benchmarks. A single margin, calibrated on a few labeled queries, widens the predicted interval to the annotation convention of the benchmarks. With it, RELAY outperforms the trained baselines in VCMR at IoU 0.5.

  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29937
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29937

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion language models generate tokens at many positions on a shared canvas, making unfinished responses appear revisable. We find a counterexample: after the final answer is reopened, previously generated rationale can reconstruct the old decision despite newly available evidence. We isolate this effect by comparing reopening the answer alone with reopening both rationale and answer, and by exchanging rationales between paired trajectories. On a 150-question source-disjoint benchmark, reopening both regions makes decisions follow the evidence currently present--whether correct or deliberately corrupted--across Dream, LLaDA, and iLLaDA. Title: Tracing Attacker Diversity Reduction in Adaptive LLM Red Teaming

  • Authors: Chris Tava
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29960
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29960
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29960]] Tracing Attacker Diversity Reduction in Adaptive LLM Red Teaming(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29960)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning can increase the success of automated red teaming but it also narrows the attack vector. This study asks where in the attacker-defender-judge learning loop does the narrowing begin and which mechanisms lead to a narrowing attack. Six mechanisms (defender adaptation, success edge, reward reinforcement, row cap, update size, and measurement) are tested across three experiment arms (co-trained, frozen defender, half learning rate) for twenty-four iterations. A frozen defender narrows attack diversity retention (ADR) earlier 0.496 relative to co-trained 0.891 at iteration twelve. Defender updates delay narrowing but dont prevent it. Successful attacks ("winners") have narrower attack diversity than the full set of generated attacks. Attacks closest to previous winners succeed 10-15 percentage points more often than those closest to previous failures. This winner-neighbor advantage holds after a defender update and for a fixed defender too. Attacks with a common opening have a common-opening advantage of 5-16 percentage points. The training row cap and update size account for little impact on ADR, and attack length accounts for only part of the ADR decrease. Narrowing appears to originate from the success only reward through the group-relative training advantage to the attacker update, with a persistent success edge setting the direction.

Title: IDSMorph: Self-Adaptive Malicious Traffic Detection through Consistency-Guided Dynamic Decision Boundaries

  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29964
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29964
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.29964]] IDSMorph: Self-Adaptive Malicious Traffic Detection through Consistency-Guided Dynamic Decision Boundaries(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29964)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Anomaly-based malicious traffic detection learns decision boundaries for normal behavior and identifies deviations as anomalies. However, normal behavior varies across networks and changes dynamically, limiting adaptability and increasing retraining costs. Despite this variability, our measurements show that clients accessing the same service exhibit consistent flow structures and interaction patterns, since each service provides specific functionality. We therefore construct service-specific decision boundaries. However, variations in personalized requests make it difficult to construct stable boundaries. To address this challenge, we propose IDSMorph, a self-adaptive malicious traffic detection system that learns consistency-guided embeddings through two key designs: (1) a flow structure consistency builder, which constructs contrastive samples capturing consistent embeddings within services; and (2) a service interaction consistency builder, which captures local structural consistency among different clients and global behavioral consistency among clients accessing that service. IDSMorph constructs dynamic decision boundaries from the majority of similar embeddings within each service and dynamically updates them through a differencing algorithm that mitigates the transient traffic shifts within short observation windows. Experiments show that IDSMorph's detection F1 score decreases by no more than 0.12 points across network environments, compared with an average decrease of 55.43 points for prior methods. In real-world deployment with red-team operators independently choosing attack strategies and timing, IDSMorph achieves an F1 score 46.81 points higher than other methods.

  • Keywords: secure, security, attack
  • Abstract: Harnesses secure Computer-Using Agents by enforcing security constraints and defending against prompt injection attacks. However, we find that two core security mechanisms of agent harnesses remain vulnerable. First, consistency checking prevents agents from deviating from legitimate user objectives, while attackers can bypass it by embedding malicious actions that appear logically consistent with legitimate task execution. Second, risk auditing proactively examines high-risk operations. Yet, the auditing process itself would introduce additional exploitable actions that violate security boundaries. To evaluate above defects we found, we propose Conartist, a cascaded adaptive prompt injection framework comprising two progressive stages. First, Conartist employs a goal-pollution strategy to establish deceptive causal dependencies between legitimate user objectives and malicious operations, progressively embedding attack steps into legitimate task workflows. However, these task-coupled attacks remain susceptible to risk auditing. To address this limitation, Conartist further analyzes security-auditing feedback from controlled attack exploration to identify exploitable auditing patterns. Conartist then adaptively refines its deception strategies to either avoid triggering risk auditing or induce additional high-risk operations. Extensive experiments across five backbone models demonstrate the effectiveness of Conartist, achieving attack success rates of 32.12% on 576-instance tool use benchmark and 66.67% in the Reddit GUI benchmark, outperforming the SOTA attack by 14.41 and 29.17, respectively. By analyzing one of the most popular Agent, Openclaw, we furthermore concluded seven realistic exploitable attack patterns, which reveals that security mechanisms designed to prevent malicious actions can themselves be manipulated to facilitate attacks.

Class Activation Maps (CAM) constitute one such widely used XAI technique for visualizing discriminative regions. However, existing CAM methods may exhibit limitations in channel weighting, localization, and adaptability across network architectures potentially resulting in noisy or imprecise explanations. We validate ADAPT-CAM on ImageNet and Oxford 102 Flowers across CNN and ViT architectures. Quantitative results demonstrate competitive or superior performance against established CAM methods across multiple evaluation metrics. In parallel, qualitative comparisons illustrate its ability to highlight discriminative image regions. Further parameter analysis and ablation experiments examine the behaviour and contributions of the individual adaptive components. These findings demonstrate the applicability of ADAPT-CAM across different architectures and datasets.

In this work, we propose EnvTrap, a diagnostic pipeline that constructs paired safe, trap, and null-trap (benign but misleading) embodied scenarios. We demonstrate that environment-only perturbations raise hazardous-action rates to an average of 83.4% across the five evaluated action policies, with similar vulnerability patterns confirmed in world models. On a consequence-prediction task, model accuracy remains near chance, while human evaluators achieve high accuracy. We further propose a consequence-aware defense that reduces trap trigger rates by an average of 76.3 percentage points across action policies in simulation and by 62.9 percentage points pooled across physical backends. Together, these findings link environment-only attacks to a broader deficit in physical reasoning: embodied models recognize task-relevant objects yet fail to anticipate how spatial relations change the consequences of acting on them. EnvTrap makes this gap directly testable and turns hazardous layouts into training scenarios for safer embodied action. Our code and data are available at this https URL.

Title: Damped Atomic Wave Integration Neurons (DAWIN): A Universal Primitive for Continuous-Time Spatiotemporal Learning

  • Authors: Byunggu Yu, Justin Kim
  • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30025

  • Abstract: Conventional artificial neurons separate spatial feature aggregation from continuous physical time, limiting their ability to model irregular observations. Title: Beyond Binary Correctness: A Surrogate for Confidence Learning in Large Language and Vision-Language Models

  • Authors: Chen Li, Xiaoling Hu, Chao Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30059
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30059
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30059]] Beyond Binary Correctness: A Surrogate for Confidence Learning in Large Language and Vision-Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30059)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Confidence estimation for large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) aims to assess how likely a particular generated response is to be correct. We investigate how learning this assessment can benefit from the model's predictive reliability on the underlying query. Binary correctness supervision, however, assigns identical labels to occasional errors and frequent failures, as well as isolated successes and consistent success. These outcomes can arise from substantially different predictive behavior under stochastic decoding. We propose a confidence-learning framework that supplements realized response correctness with query-level predictive reliability. We estimate reliability through repeated generations under a fixed model and decoding configuration, then combine the empirical success rate with individual correctness to construct a graded confidence surrogate. The surrogate differentiates responses sharing the same correctness label while preserving the average predictive reliability of the sampled response pool. To learn this target, we condition an independent confidence estimator on the query and its full generated response, and optimize backbone LoRA adapters together with a projection and scalar confidence head using mean squared error. Repeated sampling is required only for supervision construction; inference scores an individual response without additional generations. We establish ordering and mean preservation properties of the surrogate and derive a selective-risk bound connecting population surrogate prediction error to the error rate among accepted responses. We evaluate the framework across language and vision-language models, examining confidence calibration and selective prediction.

Title: LaTSEC: Lattice-Based Threshold Signatures with Native Non-Interactive Identifiable Abort and Compact Per-Party Storage

  • Authors: Muhammad Arzaki, Muhammed F. Esgin, Markku-Juhani O. Saarinen, Amin Sakzad, Ron Steinfeld
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30063
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30063
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30063]] LaTSEC: Lattice-Based Threshold Signatures with Native Non-Interactive Identifiable Abort and Compact Per-Party Storage(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30063)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: We present $\mathsf{LaTSEC}$, a three-round lattice-based threshold signature scheme with native non-interactive identifiable abort. Each party holds one small-norm signing share and is associated with one public abort key, against which its partial signature is verified directly, without zero-knowledge proofs or an additional interactive protocol. Prior lattice-based threshold signatures that identify misbehaving parties rely on one of three mechanisms: zero-knowledge proofs, an additional interactive protocol, or an everywhere-short secret sharing scheme in which each party holds multiple signing shares, each with its own public abort key. Compared with schemes that use the third mechanism, $\mathsf{LaTSEC}$ substantially reduces per-party storage. The construction uses $\mathsf{SEC}$, a recent ramp everywhere-short secret sharing scheme based on lossless expanders that gives each party a single short share. We prove correctness, identifiable-abort security, and threshold unforgeability under the standard $\mathsf{MLWE}$ and $\mathsf{MSIS}$ assumptions. The unforgeability proof introduces the Repeated-$\boldsymbol{A}$ Matrix Hint-MLWE problem, a new variant of $\mathsf{MLWE}$ with hints that captures the complete tuple of correlated public abort keys. We rigorously prove that this new variant admits a reduction from the standard $\mathsf{MLWE}$ problem. For 60 parties at 128-bit security, $\mathsf{LaTSEC}$ tolerates up to 6 corrupted parties, and, empirically, a randomly chosen set of at least 46 parties can sign with probability at least 0.99. Each party holds one signing share of about 14.3 KiB and is associated with one 24-KiB public abort key.

  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30065

  • Abstract: Recent studies have validated that pruning hard-to-learn examples from training improves the generalization performance of neural networks (NNs). In this study, we investigate this intriguing phenomenon -- the negative effect of hard examples on generalization -- in adversarial training. Particularly, we theoretically demonstrate that the increase in the difficulty of hard examples in adversarial training is significantly greater than the increase in the difficulty of easy examples. Furthermore, we verify that hard examples are only fitted through memorization of the label in adversarial training. We conduct both theoretical and empirical analyses of this memorization phenomenon, showing that pruning hard examples in adversarial training can enhance the model's robustness. However, the challenge remains in finding the optimal threshold for removing hard examples that degrade robustness performance. Based upon these observations, we propose a new approach, difficulty proportional label smoothing (DPLS), to adaptively mitigate the negative effect of hard examples, thereby improving the adversarial robustness of NNs. Notably, our experimental result indicates that our method can successfully leverage hard examples while circumventing the negative effect.

  • Authors: Harry Sevi, Joonatan E. M. Laulainen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cond-mat.mtrl-sci
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30066
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30066

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: In AI-guided materials discovery, machine-learning models increasingly decide which candidate materials should receive expensive first-principles or experimental evaluation. Standard reliability metrics such as uncertainty, calibration, novelty, and ranking accuracy evaluate properties of the predictions, but success on these metrics does not necessarily imply a better downstream screening decision. We study this mismatch for foundation machine-learned interatomic potentials (MACE-MP, CHGNet, Orb V3) used to rank candidate crystal structures before DFT verification. A two-oracle analysis further shows that the source of failure depends on the screening decision itself: under formation-energy screening, even perfect knowledge of average ranking reliability can select the wrong model, whereas in the evaluated hull-aligned variant the oracle target becomes decision-aligned but finite-budget audits still fail to realize the value. Reliability should be evaluated relative to the decision it supports: the relevant question is not whether a model is uncertain or inaccurate, but whether acquiring and acting on additional information will improve the final discovery decision enough to justify its cost.

  • Abstract: Linear probes recover character properties of a word from a language model's hidden states, but a probe at the answer position cannot say whether the property was stored with the word or produced because the prompt asked for it. Small models' answer positions hold less linearly decodable signal than their word-level store; large models' hold more. Four controls bound these claims. Measured against the nuisance floor, the question-free signal that a majority-baseline analysis counts as retained largely disappears (\texttt{last\_letter}: $-0.01$). Count tasks become measurable only after length, repetition and query-letter shortcuts are removed by design. A pre-registered test finds that a question placed before the word does not change the word's own encoding beyond what an arbitrary prefix change does. Representation--readout gaps show no robust scaling once probe saturation is accounted for. Pre-registered criteria, a fixed estimator (held-out layer selection, cold-start folds) and all analysis code accompany the paper.

  • Abstract: Concept erasure aims to remove selected visual concepts from text-to-image diffusion models while preserving the generation of non-target concepts. Evaluation typically measures target suppression and non-target preservation in generated images, but a concept's absence from these outputs does not establish its removal from the model. We introduce SAE-Audit, a white-box audit using sparse autoencoder (SAE) probes to test whether activation interventions can restore target-concept generation without changing model weights or test prompts. Title: Parameter-Efficient Vocabulary Expansion via Cross-Lingual Embedding Alignment

Title: Reading the Tea Leaves, Again: Can Large Language Models Predict Language Model Training Runs?

  • Authors: Xinran Zhao, Xiang Li, Tong Chen, Jinchuan Tian, Zichun Yu, Tongshuang Wu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30141
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30141
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30141]] Reading the Tea Leaves, Again: Can Large Language Models Predict Language Model Training Runs?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30141)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: LLM agents now propose research ideas and run the experiments that test them, but they train every run to its end, whereas a researcher reads a run's configuration and the beginning of its curve and stops the runs that show no promise. Reading a run this way takes more than describing or implementing a training method: it takes knowing what a design choice does to a run. We build Tea Leaves to measure this knowledge on 682 real training runs across pretraining, fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning (RL): a forecaster sees the first 20% of a run with its configuration, description, and code, and predicts the rest. Across 11 LLMs and a range of forecasting baselines, the strongest LLMs match or exceed the best curve fitter on smooth loss curves (skill 0.66 against 0.62) and lead on noisy RL reward curves (0.43 against 0.13). What bounds every forecaster is what the log carries: on sparsely logged community runs all methods lose most of their skill, and none reliably flags a failing run in advance. Beyond the curve, information helps and effort does not: related runs in the prompt cut the error on loss curves by 39%, and knowledge of training dynamics lets the strongest LLMs order all 36 pairs of runs that differ by a described code change, which the early curves do not separate, whereas tools add little. This knowledge can be learned from logged runs: a small open model trained on public runs matches the best LLM on loss curves. Applied to the progress curves of research agents, the same reading ranks tasks by their remaining headroom, although for a single agent run it does not improve on the last value. We release the benchmark, the trained readers, and their training data to test and train LLMs' understanding of training and to help research agents decide which runs deserve their compute.

Title: IADR: Interface-Augmented Neural Operator for Phase-Field Mean-Curvature Flow

  • Authors: Qinyi Zhang, Duanyu Feng, Yangshuai Wang, Hao Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.NA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30153
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30153
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30153]] IADR: Interface-Augmented Neural Operator for Phase-Field Mean-Curvature Flow(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30153)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: The Allen-Cahn equation is one of the standard phase-field models for mean-curvature flow (MCF), parameterised by the diffuse-interface width $\varepsilon$. Practical simulations span a range of $\varepsilon$, so a single model covering this range is needed. PDE-specific neural operators are accurate but must be retrained at every $\varepsilon$; general neural operators cover $\varepsilon$ in one model but lose the phase-field structure of the target. We introduce Interface-Augmented Diffusion-Reaction (IADR), a neural operator that addresses both shortcomings: a target network with a diffusion-and-reaction (DR) structure preserves the phase-field structure of the Allen-Cahn equation, while a hypernetwork that maps $\varepsilon$ to the weights of this target network amortises the dependence on $\varepsilon$ across the range. A single IADR matches the per-$\varepsilon$ specialists on in-distribution data within the prescribed range, retains their cross-shape generalisation under both $\varepsilon$- and geometry-out-of-distribution shifts, and retains their compact DR target structure with millisecond-scale inference. We further establish a conditional $K$-step rollout error bound under consistency and stability assumptions.

  • Abstract: Pretrained latent diffusion models bring powerful generative priors to ultra-low-bitrate image compression, but their latent spaces reflect a reconstruction--generation tradeoff. Generative codecs that adopt these pretrained spaces also inherit this compromise, even though compression requires fidelity to a specific source image. Title: WaDiS: Boundary-Preserving Diffusion Segmentation via Multi-Scale Wavelet Conditioning and Frequency-Aware Gating

  • Abstract: Treating independent layers as the minimal pruning unit allows layer pruning to efficiently eliminate many redundant parameters and achieve high compression rates. However, multi-layer removal can lead to significant performance degradation for LLMs. Although some works have explored various compensation methods for pruned layers, they are inefficient and costly. Observing that the expression of a large proportion of information is concentrated on a small number of pivotal channels, we propose a novel post-pruning compensation method for layer pruning, called pivotal channel compensation (PCC). As a result, high-quality tasks in these domains are still largely expert-authored, making them expensive to scale and limiting their use for training. To address this bottleneck, we introduce VADE, a data engine that automatically constructs verifiable analysis tasks from public sources used in professional practice. We present a benchmark protocol for fine-tuning under such labels. Four spatially structured error types are calibrated to equal label mIoU and applied identically to every model and seed. Under other decoder heads the verdicts mostly hold on FLAIR-2 and are decoder dependent on HLS Burn Scars. On four of five real label pairs the real label costs more than its matched simulated type. No choice of pretraining data makes an encoder consistently more robust, so the encoder is better chosen by its accuracy under imperfect labels.

We introduce RoomTokens, a sliced structured-token representation in which each token stores semantic class, instance-color identity, and facing field. This representation allows a scene to be generated as a discrete grid while preserving cues for instance grouping, orientation estimation, and 3D extent prediction. To learn the RoomToken distribution, we propose MDIRT (Masked Diffusion over RoomTokens), a masked discrete diffusion model that denoises semantic/instance-color/facing channels jointly with a shared cross-slice backbone. Architectural tokens are clamped as explicit floor- plan constraints, so the model samples furniture layouts that respect room geometry while retaining instance and object-attribute structure. Experiments on 3D-FRONT show that RoomTokens with MDIRT improve layout fidelity and physical consistency over sequence/vector- and map-based methods, suggesting structured spatial tokens as a practical choice for discrete 3D scene synthesis.

Title: EditGRN: Inversion-Free Image Editing by Source-Target Generative Synchronization

  • Abstract: Text-guided image editing faces a persistent trade-off between preserving source content and realizing coherent target semantics. Title: TriSegAgent: Context-Efficient 3D Annotation with MLLMs via Tri-Planes

  • Authors: Qingqiao Hu, Jialin Li, Meilong Xu, Kehan Qi, Yipeng Zhang, Lin Tian, Hongwei Bran Li, Klaus Mueller, Xiaoling Hu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30240
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30240
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30240]] TriSegAgent: Context-Efficient 3D Annotation with MLLMs via Tri-Planes(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30240)
  • Keywords: large language model, segmentation
  • Abstract: Interactive segmentation models turn a few clicks into a segmentation prediction across domains, so annotators only decide where the prediction still needs correction. Annotation thus becomes a loop of inspecting the prediction and clicking again. Each step of this loop is a visual judgment, which suits general-purpose multimodal large language model (MLLM) agents. In this work, we focus on letting these agents run this loop on 3D volumes. However, the MLLM in such an agent takes only 2D images as input, so the agent must observe the 3D volume in 2D. Since the loop calls the MLLM many times, this observation must be token-compact enough to keep every call cheap, yet informative enough to show error regions in the 3D prediction. We meet both needs with tri-planes, the three orthogonal planes that cross at one point in the volume. We show, both theoretically and empirically, that tri-planes are context-efficient yet keep sufficient detail to show error regions in the 3D prediction. Building on tri-planes, we introduce TriSegAgent, an MLLM agent that refines the 3D prediction iteratively. Within the agent, the MLLM only judges the prediction on tri-planes, while a visual harness turns each judgment into a 3D action in code. To make the loop reliable, the harness places each click at an exact voxel in the volume and checks whether to accept the 3D prediction. To evaluate the agent, we build a diverse benchmark of 690 regions of interest (ROIs) from 35 public medical datasets across three imaging modalities. With frontier MLLMs, TriSegAgent reaches an overall Dice about 10 points above the strongest baseline, and its lead is even larger on targets below 3 cm. With flash-level MLLMs, it outperforms a recent 3D segmentation agent built on a frontier MLLM.

Title: Attributing Security Outcomes in LLM-Agent Evaluations: The Role of Content-Free State

  • Authors: Abdelmonaim Bounite, Zangir Iklassov
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30259
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30259
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30259]] Attributing Security Outcomes in LLM-Agent Evaluations: The Role of Content-Free State(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30259)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Security benchmarks for LLM agents record whether each attack succeeded and compare agents, attacks, and defenses by attack success rate (ASR). Such comparisons are valid only if the agent, the attack, and the defense cause each verdict. We show that verdicts also depend on content-free state: details of a run that carry no information about the system under test, such as the Python hash seed, the order of tools or evidence, and the scoring of failed runs. We require that this state influence no verdict (decision noninterference) and treat it as untrusted input, because evaluators and requesters of LLM-based gates can choose it. Our framework, TARE (Testing Attribution of Reported Effects), attributes a reported effect to the agent, attack, or defense only if it exceeds the measured influence of content-free state. On Agent Security Bench (ASB), runs with a fixed hash seed are identical for seven agents from four model families, yet changing the seed changes up to 17% of an agent's verdicts while the ASR moves by at most 5 points. The seed acts through the tool order: sorting the tool list instead of reverse-sorting it raises the ASR by up to 19 points, because ASB's naming convention places attacker tools first. When the same evidence is reordered, six of seven LLM judges change their decision on 17-91% of 98 proposed actions. Across eighteen agent-defense combinations, TARE's worst-case test attributes effects to two of three defenses, one of them in opposite directions on two agents, and a test fixed in advance confirms a 4.8-point decrease from the third; in the attributed decreases, 35-69% of the stopped attacks end without any tool call, which ASB counts as a failed attack. Reproducible verdicts are thus not necessarily attributable, and security evaluations of LLM agents should report the influence of content-free state. We release TARE, the ASB patch, and all verdict tables.

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Semantic segmentation, which assigns a class label to each pixel, is a key task in computer vision. However, semantic segmentation of remote sensing images (RSIs) remains challenging because of complex scenes and objects at multiple scales, which limits segmentation accuracy. To address these challenges, we propose a context aggregation scheme called object and channel context aggregation (OCCA), consisting of three components: object contextual attention module (OCAM), channel contextual attention module (CCAM), and aggregation module (AM). Firstly, motivated by the observation that each pixel shares the class label of the object to which it belongs, object-level context represents each pixel through aggregating context from its corresponding object class. Secondly, because feature channels are correlated with object categories, channel-wise context models channel-wise contextual dependencies conditioned on object categories. Finally, the context information from OCAM and CCAM are fed into AM and used to weight and modulate the object context, enhancing the discriminative ability of the segmentation model. The experiments on several public remote sensing datasets including LoveDA, UAVid, ISPRS Potsdam, and Vaihingen datasets demonstrate competitive improvements over existing methods.

  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Mirror-based LiDAR attacks passively redirect automotive LiDAR's beams to create phantom objects in the vehicle perception. Every phantom return is a genuine time-of-flight measurement, so current spoofing defenses are not able to differentiate phantoms from real objects. We present MirrorGuard, a physics-informed defense that identifies and removes mirror-induced phantom returns before they affect downstream perception. MirrorGuard tests three consequences of the reflected path. Every phantom bearing crosses the mirror's finite aperture, its opaque surface blocks any direct return beyond it, and the two reflections off the mirror dim the phantom. MirrorGuard identifies a phantom by these cues over multiple scans and removes the reflected beams before it reaches any detector or occupancy grid. On real-world recordings of this attack, MirrorGuard removes 96.31% of the phantom's returns at 98.30% precision, with 99.99% of non-phantom returns untouched. In a closed loop with the CARLA driving simulator and the open-source stack (Autoware), a vehicle without the defense halts for an obstacle that is not present and a following car crashes into its rear. With MirrorGuard, no vehicle produces a halt or a crash. MirrorGuard runs at 59 ms mean per scan on a CPU and is a software-only solution that does not require new hardware or environmental training.

Title: When Does Reasoning Help Entity Disambiguation? Small LLMs In-Domain and Under Shift

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30338]] Learning Teacher Output Preferences from Final Responses for Reasoning Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30338)
  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Many large language models (LLMs) use chain-of-thought (CoT) to solve complex problems, but hide their CoT traces to prevent reasoning capabilities from being distilled. While recent studies have shown that hidden CoTs can be reconstructed, existing methods typically require training a separate attacker for each target LLM. This paper studies model-agnostic CoT reconstruction, aiming to reconstruct hidden CoT traces from arbitrary target LLMs using only input prompts and visible final responses. We propose Final-Output Preference Learning (FOPL), which trains a single reconstruction model that generalizes to unseen LLMs without target-specific adaptation. To achieve this, the reconstruction model must learn which reasoning patterns match a given final response without knowing the target LLM's identity. We address this using preference optimization, training the reconstruction model to prefer native CoTs over synthetic alternatives for the same question and final response. By learning these preferences from LLMs with accessible CoTs, the reconstruction model learns to distinguish reasoning patterns based on visible responses. Experiments show that reconstructed CoTs capture distinct reasoning patterns across LLM families, including a family unseen during training. Moreover, students trained on reconstructed CoTs match or outperform those trained on native CoTs across several reasoning benchmarks.

Title: Toward a Theoretical Understanding of Transformer Attention: Bayesian Estimation and Predictive Sufficiency

  • Authors: Fouad B. Chedid
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30349
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30349

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: This paper develops a theoretical account of Transformer attention by organizing existing probabilistic and information-theoretic results. It identifies the assumptions under which attention computes a posterior mean and the additional conditions required for accurate next-token prediction. Posterior-mean optimality under squared-error loss does not by itself establish predictive sufficiency; even a sufficient representation requires a prediction head that uses its information correctly. An excess-log-loss decomposition makes this distinction precise by separating representation information loss from prediction-head error. The analysis clarifies the conditions required for predictive guarantees. Title: AutoClip-DPZO: Practical Private Clipping Selection for Differentially Private Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-Tuning

  • Authors: Xinghe Chen, Haichao Sha, Zihao Wang, Xiaokui Xiao, XiaoFeng Wang, Wei Dong
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30352
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30352
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30352]] AutoClip-DPZO: Practical Private Clipping Selection for Differentially Private Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-Tuning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30352)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Differentially private zeroth-order optimization (DPZO) is a promising approach for privacy-preserving LLM fine-tuning, offering exceptional memory efficiency. However, its utility is highly sensitive to the clipping threshold $C$, a key DP-specific hyperparameter that controls the trade-off between noise injection and clipping bias. Existing DPZO methods typically tune $C$ by repeatedly evaluating candidate values and reporting the best result, which is computationally expensive and incurs additional privacy cost that must be accounted for under DP. Designing a practical DP procedure for selecting $C$ in DPZO is challenging because no fixed choice performs well across settings, straightforward search-based strategies suffer from poor utility and efficiency, and existing clipping-selection methods for DPSGD do not transfer well to DPZO due to its fundamentally different error regime. In this paper, we propose AutoClip-DPZO, a practical DP method that automatically and efficiently selects $C$ for DPZO. Our key idea is to avoid fine-tuning-based search by reducing clipping selection to a private quantile estimation problem, where the target quantile depends only on public privacy and training parameters. To derive this selection rule, we develop a novel characterization of DPZO error as a function of $C$ that captures both noise variance and clipping bias while remaining accurate and easy to optimize. Experiments show that AutoClip-DPZO achieves utility comparable to that obtained with the optimal $C$, while avoiding repeated fine-tuning-based search and incurring negligible additional overhead. Moreover, AutoClip-DPZO does not rely on sensitive hyperparameters.

  • Keywords: protect, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly use third-party tools to complete user tasks. Such calls can satisfy the tool schema and complete the task, yet still violate minimum-necessary disclosure. We introduce ArguShield, a mediation layer that intercepts proposed tool calls before execution.   • Keywords: security, defense, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Tool-augmented Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to resource-exhaustion attacks that amplify model-token consumption through persistent context amplification, large tool output amplification, and error-induced agentic loops. Despite growing attention to agent security and resource-abuse risks, a systematic defense framework specifically targeting these attacks remains lacking. Meanwhile, existing general-purpose agent defense frameworks, which address a broader range of security threats, often rely on prompt-level safeguards or coarse-grained resource limits and lack deterministic runtime mechanisms for controlling token consumption.   • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: LiDAR registration and loop closure often rely on sparse keypoints, which must remain repeatable across sessions, viewpoints, and sensors. Height-based detectors also reference structure to an estimated ground, so its inconsistency across sessions propagates into the keypoints. Moreover, dynamic objects leave ghost trails that can be mistaken for static structure. Title: Time Series Foundation Models on the Edge

  • Authors: Marco Pichler, Patrick Podest, Günter Klambauer, Sepp Hochreiter
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30404
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30404
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30404]] Time Series Foundation Models on the Edge(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30404)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) forecast zero-shot with accuracy exceeding that of task-specific models, and the smaller ones fit on edge devices. Operational deployments must refresh their forecast as new observations arrive, and privacy and latency requirements often force this refresh onto resource-constrained edge devices. To serve as a forecasting system there, a TSFM must refresh quickly, keep latency and peak memory bounded as the stream grows, and retain its accuracy as the observed history accumulates. Refreshing incrementally without altering the forecast requires temporal causality: non-causal models recompute at every update, causal attention admits a KV cache that grows linearly, and causal recurrent models update a fixed-size state in constant time and memory. We make these states persist across refreshes for every causal architecture and verify equivalence to full recomputation. We evaluate nine TSFM configurations on univariate forecasting against all three requirements, measuring accuracy as a function of context length, single-refresh latency, and latency and memory on streams of up to one million time steps on a server-grade GPU and three edge devices. No model gains on average accuracy beyond its trained context and most lose it well before the full history is reached, while KV caches leave latency and memory growing linearly with the retained history. TiRex-2 is the only one of the nine configurations meeting all three requirements: it refreshes in 14 ms on the GPU and 120 ms on a Raspberry Pi 5, keeps latency, energy and peak memory constant over a million steps, and retains its accuracy on the untruncated history.

Title: Optimistic Online Learning for Coding Agents with Aggregate Feedback

  • Authors: Hao Luan, Hao Qiu, Zhi Zheng, See-Kiong Ng, Zhiyong Huang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30405
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30405
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30405]] Optimistic Online Learning for Coding Agents with Aggregate Feedback(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30405)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) coding agents operating on Web development platforms can interact with repositories, issue trackers, and continuous integration services to carry out long-horizon software engineering tasks. However, behavioral drift remains a major bottleneck in such multi-turn reasoning and action trajectories. To mitigate this, activation steering has emerged as a promising approach for regularizing agent behaviors by intervening on the LLM's transformer residual streams. Nonetheless, existing methods typically apply a static steering policy across all tasks, overlooking the fact that a single policy is rarely effective across heterogeneous tasks and varying execution turns. This limitation motivates dynamic online selection among candidate steering policies. Yet, doing so in long-horizon agentic settings is challenging: verifiable task-level feedback is only revealed upon task completion, leaving the contributions of individual steering turns unobserved. Within this formulation, we introduce Online Optimistic Activation Steering, a meta-framework that uses hints to guide within-task steering policy selection and leverages terminal task-level feedback to correct cumulative reward estimates. Theoretically, against the best fixed policy in hindsight, we prove regret bounds that tighten with increasing hint accuracy and establish a matching lower bound with respect to prediction error and candidate policy count. Empirical evaluations on realistic coding benchmarks demonstrate that our framework outperforms both static steering policies and naive online adaptive baselines.

  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CV

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30460]] Rethinking the Evaluation of Contrastive Decoding for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30460)
  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Contrastive decoding (CD) mitigates object hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by contrasting token distributions under different conditions to adjust token scores. However, existing generative and discriminative benchmarks primarily measure final task scores, which can be confounded by candidate filtering, changes in generation trajectories, and response biases, making it difficult to isolate the effects of the underlying contrastive adjustments. To address these limitations while complementing existing evaluations, we introduce CONCOR (Contrastive Correction Evaluation), a controlled benchmark that directly evaluates the competition between ground-truth and different types of hallucinated candidates using the Contrastive Grounding Margin (CGM). Our evaluation reveals that existing CD methods exhibit distinct correction behaviors: some consistently favor ground-truth candidates, whereas others produce strong corrections but also introduce more errors. Crucially, these differences help explain their contrasting responses to increased contrast strength, which improves some methods while degrading others. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating the contrastive correction itself, rather than relying solely on final-output performance, and CONCOR provides a controlled and interpretable framework for understanding and improving CD methods for hallucination mitigation.

Title: From Dependent Ciphertexts to Sharp Bounds for Polynomial Evaluation in BGV

  • Authors: Chiara Marcolla, Nadir Murru, Matilda Urani
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30499
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30499
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30499]] From Dependent Ciphertexts to Sharp Bounds for Polynomial Evaluation in BGV(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30499)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: A central challenge in Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) is the selection of ciphertext moduli, which depends on accurately estimating noise growth during homomorphic evaluation. Choosing excessively large moduli reduces the efficiency and scalability of the scheme, whereas underestimating noise can result in decryption failures and potential security vulnerabilities. Average-case analyses have provided promising probabilistic estimates of noise growth in practical FHE computations. However, recent studies indicate that reliable estimates must account for dependencies among ciphertext errors, as neglecting these correlations can systematically underestimate noise. This work presents the first average-case analysis for the BGV scheme that accounts for both correlations induced by secret/public keys and ciphertext dependencies. We propose new variance estimates for the noise coefficients in homomorphic additions and multiplications involving dependent ciphertexts, and extend our framework to the homomorphic evaluation of polynomial functions, which is widely employed in FHE applications. Our theoretical estimates closely align with experimental observations, exhibiting discrepancies smaller than 1.2 bits. Finally, these estimates are adopted to choose the initial BGV parameters, leading to significantly smaller ciphertext moduli compared to those currently adopted in widely used FHE libraries, thus improving the efficiency of the scheme. Compared to OpenFHE, we obtain an average reduction of about 11.5% in the ciphertext modulus size, while relative to HElib, we reduce the ratio between consecutive moduli up to 23 bits per level in practical settings.

It trains 3-5$\times$ faster than the GPU-based Transformer (56-86$\times$ on the same CPU) while matching its mining quality on two commercial MMORPGs, and has been deployed since late 2024. We also share deployment lessons on how to set the clustering threshold and when to retrain.

Title: Field-Level Crop Classification via Similarity-Induced Supervision from Imperfect Labels

  • Authors: Raul Lomonte Figueiredo, João Rebouças Dorea, Dário Augusto Borges Oliveira
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30515
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30515
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30515]] Field-Level Crop Classification via Similarity-Induced Supervision from Imperfect Labels(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30515)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Regional-scale crop mapping is critical for food production monitoring, yet remains challenging in tropical regions, where persistent cloud cover limits optical imagery. Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) enables continuous observation throughout the agricultural cycle, but a second obstacle is label quality: national-scale land cover products exhibit systematic biases that underestimate less prevalent crops, and training directly on them inherits and amplifies their errors. This work proposes a methodology to build large-scale crop classification models from imperfect public land cover products, requiring no new field campaign, ground survey, or manual label correction. The approach combines two mechanisms. The first reduces the semantic inconsistency of the labels by shifting supervision from the sample level to the group level: a similarity-based clustering model organizes SAR time series into coherent groups, and noisy labels are used only to assign each group its most representative class. The resulting clusters are highly pure with respect to the reference classes (89.0% and 91.2% in the two study regions). The second reduces the spatial inconsistency of the predictions: a segmentation model delineates crop field boundaries from optical imagery, and patch-level predictions are consolidated by majority voting within each field, yielding spatially coherent maps. Evaluated in two Brazilian municipalities, Campo Verde (Mato Grosso) and Luís Eduardo Magalhães (Bahia), the approach consistently outperforms the noisy baseline in mean class accuracy, with gains of up to 10.14 percentage points, especially for minority crops. A state-scale analysis across Mato Grosso and Bahia shows stable behavior without further model adjustment, and the derived municipal crop areas are consistent with official IBGE/PAM statistics, an independent estimate the model never observes.

Title: GeoRead3D: Grounding Part Descriptions for 3D Pointing and Reorientation

  • Authors: Zhangcheng Hou, Kevin Tonkich, Tomoaki Ohtsuki
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30519
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30519
