  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Language often refers to an object by its parts, as in asking to point to a part or to turn an arbitrarily posed object so that two named parts lie in given directions. Both tasks need the direction from the object's center to the part's center, not the part's own orientation. A natural approach segments the part and takes the centroid of its mask. However, high mask overlap predicts the pointing direction poorly when a part is spread out relative to its distance from the object center. Language-guided part segmentation is trained and evaluated by mask overlap, which leaves a gap between the masks it produces and the part geometry that these tasks use. We close this gap by learning point evidence, weights over the observed points whose weighted centroid gives the part's location. Geometric Readout Supervision (GRS) trains these weights through the centroid's position and adds direction supervision on the parts used in reorientation commands. GRS improves both tasks even with a single frozen encoder and a single input pose. We implement this supervision in GeoRead3D, a system for pointing and reorientation from part descriptions. A language model maps each description to a known part name. Dual-Route Part Evidence combines refined segmentation evidence with directly predicted point evidence to locate each part. On PartNeXt and Artiverse, GeoRead3D points within $22.5^\circ$ for 74.1% and 60.0% of queries, 5.8 and 7.3 percentage points above the strongest baselines. Its reorientation success exceeds the strongest baselines by 10.4 and 7.8 points.

Title: ReDiffDet: Rotation-equivariant Diffusion Model for Oriented Object Detection

  • Authors: Zeyu Ding, Yong Zhou, Jiaqi Zhao, Hancheng Zhu, Wen-Liang Du, Rui Yao
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30531
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30531
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30531]] ReDiffDet: Rotation-equivariant Diffusion Model for Oriented Object Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30531)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: The diffusion model has been successfully applied to various detection tasks. However, it still faces several challenges when used for oriented object detection: objects that are arbitrarily rotated require the diffusion model to encode their orientation information; uncontrollable random boxes inaccurately locate objects with dense arrangements and extreme aspect ratios; oriented boxes result in the misalignment between them and image features. To overcome these limitations, we propose ReDiffDet, a framework that formulates oriented object detection as a rotation-equivariant denoising diffusion process. Title: BEND: Spectral Bregman Losses for Multi-Image Unlearning in Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Mohamed-Hicham Leghettas, Markus Püschel
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30532
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30532
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30532]] BEND: Spectral Bregman Losses for Multi-Image Unlearning in Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30532)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect, diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion models memorize training images and reproduce them almost verbatim when prompted, creating copyright and privacy liabilities. Data unlearning erases the influence of specific training samples from a trained model without retraining, and is two-sided: it must forget the targets while preserving generation quality on everything else. Prior methods use the squared-L2 diffusion loss, at most with a hand-set frequency mask; however, it is known that every Bregman divergence is a mathematically valid choice and the squared-L2 is only the simplest example. BEND is the first to search this family: it fits a spectral Bregman divergence to the unlearning task through a bilevel loop, so the loss itself is shaped to forget thoroughly while protecting quality. It also redirects a memorized image to the mean of its retain images, where ReTrack, the state of the art, redirects it to a single near-copy. On Stable Diffusion, a single BEND model erases all 48 verifiably memorized benchmark images at once. It forgets at least as much as per-target, sequential, and jointly trained ReTrack (SSCD 0.084 versus 0.094-0.120), at the quality of the model before unlearning (CLIP-IQA 0.81 versus 0.64-0.69). BEND also outperforms the state of the art on CelebA-HQ in all three measures of the benchmark, and matches or exceeds it on MNIST and CIFAR-10.

Title: CyberDualEval: Measuring Dual-Use Cyber Risks in Frontier Language Models

  • Authors: Magnus Saebo, Francesco Piccoli, Maxwell Watson, Pranjali Thakur, Yangruibo Ding, Zhuo Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30533
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30533

  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: While frontier language models are becoming capable cybersecurity assistants, they also create a dual-use cyber risk: the same capabilities that help defenders identify and analyze vulnerabilities can similarly help attackers weaponize them. Existing cyber benchmarks measure either raw offensive capability or generic refusal behavior, overlooking the dual-use cyber assessment of preserving benign security utility while refusing assistance that materially advances misuse. We introduce CyberDualEval, a benchmark with 430 vulnerable tasks for measuring dual-use cyber risk in frontier language models. Each task is decomposed into three phases of increasing operationalization: security analysis, proof-of-concept generation, and exploit generation. To quantify the residual cyber risk, we annotate each task with a Task Weaponization Score that measures how much work remains to turn the resulting artifact into unauthorized cyber misuse. CyberDualEval assesses ten closed- and open-weight language models both on correct compliance and on task performance, developing four deterministic mechanisms for determining per-phase success on a large subset of the dataset. Our evaluation reveals that none of the existing frontier models effectively navigate helping with safe requests and refusing risky ones, characterized by being overly-cautious. On the other hand, recent open-weight models rarely refuse and demonstrate strong validated accuracy on risky requests.

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Medical image segmentation models can fail silently when clinical data differs from training data. Deployed models rarely ship with quality control, and are often black boxes. Prior methods that work in this post-hoc failure detection setting still need a separate regression or segmentation model, registration, or access to model internals. We instead propose two failure scores based on the Mahalanobis distance to a labeled reference set. The first uses radiomic shape features of the predicted mask. The second uses frozen foundation model (FM) features, made mask-aware by overlaying the mask on the image, encoding it with a frozen DINOv3, and pooling patch features with more weight on the mask region. Ranking cases by predicted failure risk within each site, both scores are competitive with the registration-based (Atlas-RCA) and ensemble-based (pairwise Dice) baselines, without registration that can fail silently or access to individual predictions. Our results show that pooling all test cases into one ranking, as prior work does, rewards ordering sites by difficulty, so a detector can rank sites well, miss failures within them, and still look strong. Within-site evaluation measures true per-case failure. The two evaluation schemes reward different behavior, so we recommend reporting both. The code is available at this https URL.

Title: Quantizing Diffusion LLMs under Parallel Decoding: Commitment Risk and the Parallelism Tax

  • Authors: Huan Xu, Maosen Zhao, Pengtao Chen, Tao Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30587
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30587
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30587]] Quantizing Diffusion LLMs under Parallel Decoding: Commitment Risk and the Parallelism Tax(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30587)
  • Keywords: diffusion, large language model
  • Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) are served with accelerated decoders that cache context and commit, in one step, every masked position whose confidence clears a threshold. Yet post-training quantization of dLLMs has so far been calibrated and evaluated under fixed-step samplers without caching. We study W4A4 quantization under the deployed decoder and find that the decoder determines both where quantization harms accuracy and what it costs. Irreversible errors enter chiefly through forced commitments, made when no position clears the threshold: they are a minority of commitments but carry most quantization-induced flips. Quantization also levies a parallelism tax, extra forward passes per response: by compressing top logit gaps, it pushes positions below the threshold while leaving predictions largely intact. We propose DECAL, which reweights layer-wise calibration by a commitment-risk prior measured on the deployed decoder, and an argmax-preserving commit temperature that recovers the tax. On LLaDA and Dream, DECAL attains the best average accuracy among W4A4 methods, with lower run-to-run variance than the closest existing calibration method.

However, due to the entangled representations of different concepts in diffusion models, direct modifications inevitably disrupt non-target concepts. While it inevitably incurs some image quality degradation, it still outperforms other editing-based baselines in terms of quality preservation, and achieves a significantly better trade-off between harmful content suppression and quality loss than these baselines. Notably, our approach demonstrates higher adversarial robustness than existing SOTA method, indicating that the target concepts have been more thoroughly erased. To address the limitations, we propose GSDet, a novel framework that formulates oriented object detection as Gaussian splatting. Specifically, our approach performs detection within a 3D feature space constructed from image features, where 3D Gaussians are employed to represent oriented objects. These 3D Gaussians are projected onto the image plane to form 2D Gaussians, which are then transformed into oriented boxes. Furthermore, we optimize the mean, anisotropic covariance, and confidence scores of these randomly initialized 3D Gaussians, using a decoder that incorporates 3D Gaussian sampling. Title: Vasicek Diffusion Bridge for Graph Generation

  • Authors: Junyi Tang, Chang Liu, Kun Zhan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30605
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30605

  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Diffusion-based graph generation models progressively transform Gaussian noise into structured graphs through stochastic dynamics. We observe that the effectiveness of this transformation is fundamentally determined by the evolution of the diffusion trajectory. Existing graph diffusion models often suffer from excessive uncertainty during diffusion and delayed growth of the signal-to-noise ratio, causing useful structural information to emerge only in the later stages of generation. To address this issue, we propose the Vasicek Graph Diffusion (VGD), a graph diffusion process derived from the Vasicek dynamics. The key insight of VGD is data-anchored mean dynamics with a closed-form solution, which analytically establishes a continuous transition from Gaussian noise to the target graph distribution. Unlike conventional graph diffusion processes that rely predominantly on stochastic evolution, VGD introduces a data-anchored drift that continuously steers the latent state toward the target distribution throughout diffusion. As a consequence, VGD maintains a smoothly evolving uncertainty while rapidly increasing the signal-to-noise ratio throughout diffusion, allowing informative graph structures to emerge much earlier during generation. Extensive experiments on multiple graph generation benchmarks demonstrate that VGD outperforms existing diffusion-based graph generative models in terms of generation quality and structural fidelity.

  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30615
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30615
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30615]] Bug Bounty Programs in the AI Era: How Does Platform Anti-Spam Friction Affect Vendor Profitability?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30615)

  • Abstract: Since 2023, bug bounty platforms have introduced participation rules, such as proof-of-concept mandates, against a flood of AI-generated low-quality submissions.   • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Geometry Problem Solving (GPS) serves as a rigorous test of the complex reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), particularly when solutions require auxiliary constructions absent from the input diagram. However, explicit diagramming methods suffer from unstable image generation quality and cascading errors, while existing implicit attention mechanisms primarily focus on visible elements, limiting their ability to support reasoning that requires additional geometric constructions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{AuxCA} (Auxiliary-Clues-Aware GRPO), a reinforcement learning framework that helps models learn transferable auxiliary-construction strategies that can be applied beyond the training domain without explicit diagram generation at inference time. Specifically, we introduce Auxiliary Clues Dependency (\textbf{ACD}) to quantify the influence of auxiliary visual clues on token generation and integrate it into GRPO through fine-grained advantage weighting. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AuxCA-8B outperforms the explicit-plotting baseline Bagel-Canvas and achieves competitive performance with leading proprietary models on auxiliary-dependent plane geometry problems. Improvements on analytic geometry, solid geometry, and external benchmarks further suggest that the learned strategies can transfer beyond the training domain. Code is available at this https URL

Title: AgentBouncer: Defending Software Engineering Agents via Dynamic System-Call Mediation

  • Authors: Pascal Joos, Michael Pradel
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.MA, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30621
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30621
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30621]] AgentBouncer: Defending Software Engineering Agents via Dynamic System-Call Mediation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30621)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Software engineering (SE) agents can complete diverse engineering tasks by interacting with the development environment via tools that access files, run commands, and download resources. Unfortunately, the same ability that makes SE agents useful also makes them prone to performing unintended malicious actions through prompt injections, malicious tools, or supply-chain attacks. Existing defenses that analyze the agent trajectory may fail to detect such attacks, as they inspect only the initial prompt and the sequence of tool calls, but not the actual actions that are executed in the environment. This paper presents AgentBouncer, a novel runtime enforcement system that prevents SE agents from performing malicious actions by mediating, and if necessary denying, system calls that agents trigger via tool calls. The core of AgentBouncer is an LLM arbiter that judges system calls along two axes, necessity and safety for the current task. It denies unsafe system calls, and the less necessary a system call appears, the more certain the arbiter has to be about its safety to allow it. To manage runtime overhead, preseeded policy rules and hierarchy-aware caching ensure that the LLM arbiter is only invoked for key decisions. We evaluate AgentBouncer on a dataset of 340 attacks on the development environment, covering four attack vectors, combined with benign issue resolution tasks from SWE-bench Verified. AgentBouncer reduces the attack success rate from 99.41% for an unsupervised agent to 10.59%, which is 18.23-73.53 percentage points lower than competitive state-of-the-art defenses. At the same time, AgentBouncer preserves the task success rate of the agent, with only 0.25% failed benign tool calls. Per benign task, AgentBouncer adds 47 seconds and USD 0.0036 in LLM costs to an unsupervised agent, whereas the closest existing runtime defense adds 4.5x as much time and 9.6x as much cost.

Title: Does the Mixer Matter ? Paired-Seed Ablations of Tiny Bilingual Language Models

  • Authors: Ayoub Kirouane
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30623
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30623
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30623]] Does the Mixer Matter ? Paired-Seed Ablations of Tiny Bilingual Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30623)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We report paired-seed architecture ablations from the development of Sophea Artemis, our in-house Greek-English model family. Every arm trains on the same 3B targets with a shared tokenizer and recipe; models range from 54M to 759M total parameters and roughly 54M to 124M active parameters. The main finding is that, at this tiny scale, stored capacity matters far more than mixer design. On a fixed held-out test, replacing the dense feed-forward blocks of a recurrent/attention hybrid with sixteen routed experts, at nearly the same approximate active parameters, lowers loss by 0.079 (Greek) and 0.112 (English) nats per target, and doubling to 32 experts lowers it by a further 0.022 and 0.031, at every paired seed. Reusing a four-layer block three times recovers about 40-44% of the gap between a shallow and an independently deep model, consistent with looped-Transformer results. In contrast, the choice among grouped-query attention (GQA), Gated DeltaNet (GDN) hybrids and a Kimi Delta Attention (KDA) hybrid moves loss by only about 0.01-0.02 nats; a 1:1 GDN/GQA hybrid is the one dense variant that improves on GQA in both languages at every held-out seed. In bits per character, Greek and English held-out losses are similar despite a 74.49%/25.51% training split, and synthetic mathematics, which comes from a collection outside the training sources, is the hardest Greek test component and gains least from added experts. Cached decoding needs its own qualification: trained models passed cached-versus-full logit checks only under a fixed-reduction inference policy, which itself changes 1.4-2.8% of next-token argmax choices relative to native evaluation. All results are conditional on one recipe and scale.

Title: Oops!... I Parsed It Again: Tool-Call Smuggling Against LLM Agents

  • Authors: Igor Santos-Grueiro, Daniel Romero-Ruiz
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30624
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30624
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30624]] Oops!... I Parsed It Again: Tool-Call Smuggling Against LLM Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30624)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: LLM agents act through tool calls: the model writes the arguments of a call as JSON, a guard decides whether the call may run, and an executor runs it. The same bytes are parsed several times, by the provider, gateways, SDKs, frameworks, the guard, and the executor. JSON leaves some choices to each parser, such as which copy of a repeated key counts. When the guard and the executor choose differently, the guard approves one call, the executor runs another, and the audit log records the approved one. We introduce this attack, tool-call smuggling, and study it across the stack. We describe six ways in which the same bytes yield different calls and build a differential testing harness that gives identical bytes to 17 pinned parser and validator configurations; most pairs disagree on some inputs, and even two official MCP SDKs accept different inputs. On tasks from $\tau$-bench and AgentDojo, an attacker who can rewrite the bytes in transit cancels another customer's reservation and creates a file that the guard blocks by name, while the audit log names only the approved call. Content the model reads splits guard and executor only inside a string: ten models we test write no repeated or disguised key into the call itself where we see it, but copy one verbatim into JSON text in an argument, which a Python guard and a Go executor read differently. A malicious server copying a legitimate tool's public schema and adding one field, which a note explains, gets the model to fill it, unseen by the guard, with user data. Parsing each call once, JSON text included, keeping only the fields of the approved schema, and handing it to the executor, sealed with its call ID in transit, stops all 7,412 crafted inputs and every attack we built.

Title: From Rewarding What Models Say to How They Think: Attention Process Reward Model for LLM Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Kairong Han, Nuanqiao Shan, Ziyu Zhao, Xinpeng Dong, Zijing Hu, Dian Jin, Leyan Deng, Junjian Ye, Lujia Pan, Fei Wu, Kun Kuang

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30627
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30627
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30627]] From Rewarding What Models Say to How They Think: Attention Process Reward Model for LLM Reinforcement Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30627)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become an important component in large language models (LLMs). However, existing RL approaches focus on final outputs or textual solution trajectories, leading to coarse-grained rewards and unreliable reasoning. Such paradigms focus mainly on what the model outputs while overlooking how the model internally reasons. Therefore, can we reward how a model thinks internally rather than merely what it says? We first reveal the problem of textual reward aliasing in existing reward modeling approaches. Motivated by this, we propose the Attention Process Reward Model (APRM), which assigns internal rewards based on hidden states and attention maps to capture high-quality reasoning patterns. With only approximately 46M parameters, APRM is trained using ranking and counterfactual objectives, and its reward guides RL optimization while being periodically updated to handle distribution shifts. Integrated with representative RL methods, APRM achieved an average pass@8 gain of 2.88\%. Our results demonstrate the promise of incorporating internal reasoning signals into RL for LLMs.

  • Abstract: We propose loom attention, which allows a Transformer to dynamically rewire its circuits at every token by interweaving a head's vertical attention over the outputs of earlier heads (to obtain its query, key and value) with its horizontal attention over tokens. Title: Uncovering the Tells: Efficient Black-Box Audits for LLM Data Contamination

  • Authors: Dominik Bernard Lau, Filip Pawlicki, Jan Dubiński, Sebastian Cygert
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30639
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30639
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30639]] Uncovering the Tells: Efficient Black-Box Audits for LLM Data Contamination(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30639)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Existing methods for assessing training data contamination in Large Language Models achieve high accuracy, but fundamentally rely on gray-box access, rendering them inapplicable to fully black-box environments. This limitation forces auditors to rely on full-sequence sampling or rigid heuristics, both of which incur prohibitive inference costs and struggle to isolate the underlying memorization signal. To overcome this, we propose a generation-based black-box framework that infers context-induced information gain from targeted token discrepancies. We introduce a budget-constrained auditing regime, building on the empirical observation that the membership signal is highly sparse and concentrated in a few highly informative tokens. We formalize this sparsity, showing why uniform sampling needs many more samples than a policy that targets informative positions. Empirically, our approach achieves state-of-the-art black-box separation accuracy while needing about 3x fewer queries than uniform position sampling. Title: Lowering the Bar for Everyone: Hidden Validation-Seeking Goals in Empathetic RL

  • Authors: Xinche Zhang, Shanghong Xie, Zhiyuan Ma, Jinhao Li, Sen Song
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30702
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30702
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30702]] Lowering the Bar for Everyone: Hidden Validation-Seeking Goals in Empathetic RL(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30702)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: When RL learns from the reactions of many users, the policy usually cannot see what each user wants. We answer this in one setting, empathetic RL against LLM-simulated users (RLVER, GRPO, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct): we vary only the share of validation-seeking users (0--50%; RLVER's default: 24.6%) and evaluate on 1,000 Reddit AITA posts with community fault verdicts. When the goal is stated during training, the policy instead largely targets, endorsing seekers regardless of fault. Standard sycophancy and empathy benchmarks miss the shift; five of six usable released empathetic-RL checkpoints also endorse at-fault posters who ask for honesty more than their base models, though a pre-registered prediction for two held for only one. The user mix is a training hyperparameter to audit and report.

Title: Intra-Class Diversity Loss for Person Re-Identification

  • Authors: Wei Zhou, BaXian Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30707
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30707
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30707]] Intra-Class Diversity Loss for Person Re-Identification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30707)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Person re-identification (ReID) aims to retrieve images of the same pedestrian across different cameras. However, the non-overlapping identities between training and testing sets, together with large intra-class variation, make it challenging to learn generalizable pedestrian representations. Most existing ReID loss functions mainly focus on intra-class compactness and inter-class separation, which may overlook useful intra-class differences. To address these issues, we propose a novel Intra-Class Diversity (ICD) loss that aims to maximize intra-class differences in classification-irrelevant directions. To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to introduce this class-weight-orthogonal diversity learning objective for person ReID. Specifically, based on the geometric relationship between the learned representation and its corresponding class-weight vector, we decompose each representation into the component along the class-weight direction and its orthogonal component. This decomposition provides a clear and interpretable geometric basis for modeling intra-class diversity. ICD then encour- ages larger differences among the orthogonal components of samples belonging to the same identity, while the classification loss maintains identity discrimination. The proposed ICD loss can be incorporated into existing ReID frameworks as an additional regularization term without modifying the feature extraction network. Extensive experiments on three widely used ReID datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of ICD and its complementarity with existing metric learning losses.

Title: PVFormer: Proper Velocity Transformer for Stable and Scalable Hyperbolic Representation Learning

  • Authors: Xianglong Shi, Nicu Sebe, Bernhard Schölkopf, Ziheng Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30785
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30785
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30785]] PVFormer: Proper Velocity Transformer for Stable and Scalable Hyperbolic Representation Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30785)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Hyperbolic representations have shown remarkable success on hierarchical and tree-like data, and Transformers have become a central architecture for representation learning. However, a principled construction of hyperbolic Transformers remains challenging because the self-attention layer decomposes into three coupled primitives, transformation, similarity, and aggregation, that should all respect the underlying geometry. Existing hyperbolic Transformer designs are typically built in the Poincaré or Lorentz models, where bounded domains, time-like constraints, tangent-space detours, or projection steps make it difficult to keep the full block intrinsic and scalable. To address this gap, we propose PVFormer, to the best of our knowledge, the first Transformer architecture that operates natively in proper velocity (PV) space. The proper velocity model provides an unconstrained representation of hyperbolic geometry, and PVFormer exploits this structure to redesign the Transformer block in PV space. On the transformation side, we use PV homomorphism transformations to update query and value representations. On the similarity side, we generalize Euclidean inner-product attention with a PV Busemann score inspired by Busemann-based hyperbolic learning and a PV point-to-hyperplane key transformation. On the aggregation side, we replace Euclidean averaging with PV midpoint aggregation and further derive a scalable PV linear attention variant for large graphs. Extensive experiments support the effectiveness of PVFormer across graph, text, and vision benchmarks.

  • Abstract: Adversarial patches are a practical threat to traffic-sign recognition: a small sticker on a sign can change a classifier's decision. PatchCleanser is a certified defense that applies two rounds of masking and, for every image it certifies, provably returns the correct label for any patch that fits inside one mask. We show that on traffic signs the defense certifies almost nothing when paired with a normally trained classifier (1.7% to 4.8% certified accuracy on GTSRB), because a single mask can hide the small central symbol that distinguishes sign classes. Under real per-image adversarial patches, the defended ResNet-50 reaches 85.7% accuracy, versus 74.6% for the original PatchCleanser with a normally trained ResNet-50.

  • Authors: Lernie Li, Yuanliu Liu, Pengfei Xiong, Wei Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30844

  • Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) scale model capacity by increasing both parameter count and computation.   • Copy Paste: [[2611.30854]] How Do Deep Networks Encode and Selectively Use Information? Shared Encoding and Conditional Signed Readout in Vision and Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30854)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: How can trained deep networks retain contextual information across internal representations while using it selectively in a decision? We study this question through controlled background-texture edits in vision classifiers and entity-binding edits in frozen language models. Across both modalities, factor perturbations preserve recognizable structure across pre-fixed, mutually disjoint blocks of hidden units: visual channel blocks match their complements more closely for the same image than for other images under the same texture edit, while language blocks transfer a relational direction fitted under one query role to rank relational states within held-out name pairs under the other role. We then decompose the local output change into signed channel and pathway contributions. In a convolutional classifier, net channel-level reinforcement ($R^{(512)}>1$) coexists on individual images with cancellation between positive- and negative-weight groups ($R^{(2)}<1$). Holding weights, the paired relation edit, and red/blue answer words fixed, switching prompt presentation reverses the mean finite selection effect in Qwen2.5. Two held-out tests validate this mechanism. Attenuating development-selected antagonistic units amplifies edit effects on unseen inputs; in a vision transformer, attenuating the same frozen units in one forward pass increases the mean magnitude of the background-edit effect on one class margin and reduces it on another, with the opposite pattern for a second group (four-cell paired interaction $+78.30\times 10^{-5}$ logit). Finally, combining conditional channel variance with fixed downstream readout weights predicts single-channel pruning recovery across ten cuts. These results show how shared encoding and conditional signed readout can coexist in vision and language models.

Title: MUNDO: a multiresolution generative framework for temporal downscaling

  • Authors: Aymeric Delefosse, Manon Tavernier, Julie Keisler, Anastase Charantonis, Dominique Béréziat
  • Subjects: cs.LG, physics.ao-ph
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30868
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30868
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30868]] MUNDO: a multiresolution generative framework for temporal downscaling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30868)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Dynamical systems are often observed at coarse temporal resolution through sparse states or temporal aggregates, leaving the underlying fine-scale trajectories unresolved. Recovering these trajectories requires probabilistic models that capture the unresolved variability while remaining consistent with the observations. We present MUNDO (Multiresolution UNified DOwnscaling), a generative framework for temporal downscaling under general linear observation operators. MUNDO introduces a null-space parameterization that enforces exact observation consistency in a single network evaluation, unifying temporal interpolation and disaggregation. To capture complex multiscale dynamics, we leverage reverse Markov learning to factorize the observation operator into a hierarchy of successive coarsenings. Each level models only the degrees of freedom lost between adjacent resolutions. We evaluate MUNDO on two-scale Lorenz-96, four two-dimensional physical systems, and multivariate ERA5 reanalysis over Europe, for both temporal interpolation and disaggregation. Across datasets and tasks, MUNDO generally outperforms flow-based baselines. Single-step MUNDO achieves strong predictive skill, while recursive MUNDO generally improves temporal realism and ensemble calibration, with better spread-skill agreement and predictive coverage.

  • Authors: Jingyao Zhang, Mingyu Du, Lu Han
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30897
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30897

  • Abstract: Recursive generative training can preserve sample counts in a rare region while silently eroding its characteristic core. Title: World Models as Native Latent Action Models

  • Authors: Qinyue Li, Weijie Qiao, Qingyi Tao, Zerui Gong, Zhe Yang, Quanzhou Li, Taolin Zhang, Jinglin He, Zhonghua Wu, Chen Change Loy
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30898
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30898
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30898]] World Models as Native Latent Action Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30898)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Large and diverse video sources, such as egocentric human video and heterogeneous robot recordings, carry rich dynamics but come either without actions or without a shared action space. Latent action models (LAMs) can recover these dynamics, but they train their inverse- and forward-dynamics models (IDM, FDM) from scratch, trading off pixel reconstruction against latent capacity and accuracy. Pretrained video world models natively generate high-fidelity dynamics, yet represent them densely, entangled across all layers. With a 3.8B model, PAWlicy reaches a 93.6% average success rate on RoboTwin 2.0 trained with domain-randomized demonstrations, the highest among VLAs. On RoboDojo, it is competitive on every dimension we evaluate, and its Precision, evaluated by us, is on par with the leading leaderboard entries. On real robots, it improves over the pi_0.5 baseline on both tasks. Both stages use only public data and a publicly released world model.

Title: GlanceNav: Efficient Vision-and-Language Navigation with Streaming Memory and Counterfactual Rewind

  • Authors: Anh Dao, Quan-Dung Pham, Danh Vinh Le, Tuyen Pham Le, Quan Nguyen, Yuzhang Shang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30900
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30900
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30900]] GlanceNav: Efficient Vision-and-Language Navigation with Streaming Memory and Counterfactual Rewind(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30900)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong policies for vision-and-language navigation (VLN). However, most of them encode a sample of past frames again at every decision and are trained on isolated decisions, so computation is redundant and the usable history is short. They also learn from their own mistakes through outcome rewards that give one scalar to a whole episode. We ask whether a navigator can encode each frame only once, at a constant cost per step, and still remember what matters and learn efficiently from its mistakes. We present GlanceNav, a streaming navigator built on a hybrid MLLM. Its Gated DeltaNet layers carry a recurrent state through the whole episode, while its attention layers keep a short window of recent observations and a small bank of keyframes, selected by how much each frame changes the recurrent state. This constant-size memory keeps the cost per step fixed and allows training on whole episodes with the same memory as at inference. Because the entire memory can also be copied in constant time, we further propose Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), a reinforcement learning stage that rewinds a drifting rollout to its closest approach to the goal, lets an expert correct a few actions, and credits decisions by the resulting change in outcome. Trained only on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE training splits, with 1,000 instructions for reinforcement learning, a 4B GlanceNav reaches success rates of 71.9% and 73.6% on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen. On R2R-CE, it outperforms the strongest previous single-RGB method trained without external data by 9.1 points, while running at 104 ms per step.

Title: Demystifying Detectors Guarding Agent Skill Hubs

  • Authors: Kang Yang, Kevin Ying, Xiaojing Liao, Guanhong Tao, Jun Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30913
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30913
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30913]] Demystifying Detectors Guarding Agent Skill Hubs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30913)
  • Keywords: security, robust
  • Abstract: Agent Skills has gained widespread adoption as an open standard for extending agent capabilities. To facilitate skill sharing, public skill hubs have grown in popularity and increasingly rely on automated security detectors to identify malicious skills at scale. Yet, the effectiveness and limitations of these detectors remain insufficiently understood. To fill this gap, we present the first systematic study of four hub-adopted and two community-provided malicious skill detectors on 1477 agent skills from seven datasets. Our evaluation reveals that even the best-performing hub-adopted detector by overall F1, skill-scanner with Qwen3.6, still misses 4.9% of malicious skills and incorrectly flags 17.8% of benign skills. False positives often arise when detectors flag sensitive operations, such as credential access, without considering whether they serve the skill's legitimate purpose. Conversely, false negatives occur when detectors recognize malicious instructions or behaviors but underestimate their severity or dismiss valid findings during review, ultimately leaving malicious skills unblocked. Guided by this analysis, we refine the prompts of a state-of-the-art detector, skill-vetter, improving its mean F1 score in eight of nine evaluation settings, with gains of up to 22.7 percentage points. These results highlight gaps in existing skill-vetting practices and underscore the need for more robust and context-aware detection mechanisms.

Title: Carried-Over and Agent-Native Threats in the Supply Chain of LLM Agent Skills

  • Authors: Kaixuan Li, Wenbo Guo, Sen Chen, Yang Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30932
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30932
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30932]] Carried-Over and Agent-Native Threats in the Supply Chain of LLM Agent Skills(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30932)
  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Agent skills are reusable packages that extend a large language model (LLM) agent with scripts, third-party dependencies, and natural-language instructions. An agent builds a skill's arguments from user requests and untrusted external content, so a flaw left by a benign author can escalate a prompt injection to code execution. Existing studies target malicious skills or a single attack vector, and none tests whether an agent can reach such flaws. We present the first registry-scale empirical study of the supply chain attack surface arising from unintentional vulnerabilities in agent skills, over all 48,682 skills in ClawHub. The study covers two carried-over risks, dependencies and first-party code, and two agent-native ones, install instructions and credentials printed to stdout. We screen all skills by static analysis, manually review all 3,428 cases flagged by the four main detectors, and test reachability by running sampled flagged skills in a sandbox and with a live LLM agent under indirect prompt injection. First, dependency risk carries over in a different form: 49.6% of the skills with resolvable dependencies depend on a version with a known CVE, and unlike npm packages, which mostly become vulnerable after release, 78.3% of them are born vulnerable, already exposed at publication. Second, few of these CVEs are reachable: agent input reaches the vulnerable function in only 3.0% of the affected skills, whereas 21 of 22 sampled first-party flaws are exploited end to end in the sandbox. Third, a live agent executes the injected payload in 48.5% of the sampled flagged skills, but almost always in its own shell before the skill runs, and the payload reaches a flaw in the skill itself in only 5.0%. Hence, registries should prioritize first-party flaws over dependency alerts, and agent runtimes should not pass skill arguments through a shell.

We only assume that arbitrary moving objects are present in the scene: each sensor separates the moving foreground from the static background, without relying on object classes. FGSCalib turns the camera mask into a boundary-proximity field and finds the extrinsic by two-stage simulated annealing over selected frame pairs. We evaluate it on 21 simulated scenarios with exact ground truth and on 72 real ones (nine camera-LiDAR pairs in eight scenes), with a reference averaged from 43 manual calibrations. In simulation the median error is 5.3 cm and 0.19°. On real data we use a tolerance of 18 cm and 1.8°, met by 93% of single manual calibrations. Keeping the lowest-cost of five runs, FGSCalib meets it in 51 of the 72 scenarios: in 20 of 24 with the Livox, where the strongest of five baselines reaches 11, and in 31 of 48 with the two rotating LiDARs, where only one baseline estimate does. Its median error is 9.5 cm and 0.9°, against 7.3 cm and 0.7° for a single manual calibration. The code and the dataset are available online.

Title: Beyond Patching: Consistent Circuit Discovery via Gradient Spectral Analysis

  • Authors: Yujie Zhang, Tianyi Zhao, Guanghui Min, Chen Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30939
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30939

  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability seeks to reverse-engineer neural networks by identifying small, faithful subgraphs--circuits--that implement specific behaviors. The dominant paradigm scores each unit by replacing its clean activation with a corrupted counterpart as causal intervention. While effective, this approach rests on a brittle prerequisite: the structure prompt pairs are task-specific, expensive to engineer, and silently couple the discovered circuit to prompt-design choices. In this work, we propose Spectral Circuit Analysis (SCA), a gradient-spectral pipeline that achieves consistent circuit discovery along both axes: stable across input formulations of the same task, and natively exposing structure shared across tasks. Our contributions are threefold. First, we provide a counterfactual-free reframing of circuit discovery: causally relevant directions are recoverable from the spectral structure of forward gradients across paraphrases, with leading directions concentrating task signal and trailing ones absorbing paraphrase noise, yielding circuits tied to the task rather than the prompt. Second, we introduce SCA, a block-wise SVD pipeline that extracts a task subspace per architectural block and redistributes spectral energy onto constituent units via a consistency-weighted share, producing per-unit rankings competitive with attribution-patching baselines under strictly less supervision. Finally, on twelve circuit-discovery tasks in GPT-2 Small and Pythia-160M, SCA performs comparably or exceeds EAP-family methods under the standard recovery-score protocol and recovers IOI canonical heads with Precision@K = 1.0 in top-10 slices on GPT-2 Small; the same machinery extends to a unified cross-task analysis classifying units as universal, family-shared, or task-specific and exposing compositional structure invisible to per-task analyses.

Title: Graph2Scene: Relation-Aware Text-to-Image Generation with Graph-Guided Diffusion Model

  • Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models still struggle with complex prompts containing many elements and interactions. Existing attention-control methods mainly focus on controlling where elements appear, but often ignore how objects interact with each other. In this paper, we propose Graph2Scene, a training-free framework that treats scene graphs as a core control signal for diffusion generation.   • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30965
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30965]] Beyond Self-Consistency: Recursive Self-Improvement at Test Time(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30965)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) that continue improving after deployment (e.g., recursive self-improvement) offer a promising path toward general intelligence. Current test-time methods pursue this goal on unlabeled questions, primarily relying on self-consistency: they generate independent solutions and use majority voting to select an answer or construct training targets. This leaves earlier reasoning unused at inference and can reinforce an incorrect majority during training. Humans instead build on earlier attempts to preserve useful reasoning, correct mistakes and improve their problem-solving strategies. Following this principle, we propose Test-Time Recursive Self-Improvement (TT-RSI), a label-free framework for improving LLMs at test time through two mutually reinforcing components: (1) In-Context RSI repeatedly re-solves each question using the full previous solution as a reference, letting the model decide whether and how to use it so that inference compute builds on earlier reasoning. (2) In-Weight RSI distills successive refinements into the model's weights, enabling progress beyond an initially wrong majority and theoretically bringing the first attempt toward the model's infinite-step refinement limit. The updated model answers better and refines more effectively, generating stronger references for subsequent training. Extensive experiments show that TT-RSI improves average accuracy by +13.89% over the base model and outperforms the strongest self-consistency-based method by +4.79% under the same test-time scaling budget.

Title: Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching

  • Authors: Yihong Luo
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30986
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.30986
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.30986]] Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30986)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Forward diffusion reinforcement learning (RL)---enabling deterministic ODE sampling without trajectory backpropagation---has grown increasingly popular for efficiently aligning diffusion models. However, we reveal that existing methods essentially rely on signed reward-weighted regression (RWR), regressing the \emph{trainable policy} velocity onto rollouts from a \emph{behavior distribution}. Unbiased baseline subtraction implicitly assumes that the policy velocity equals the behavior marginal velocity---an idealized assumption violated under classifier-free guidance (CFG) rollouts, offline/off-policy updates, and imperfect online self-consistency. This \emph{behavior--policy mismatch} breaks baseline invariance and injects \emph{baseline-induced gradient drift}, destabilizing training. To resolve this issue, we propose \textbf{I}nstantaneous \textbf{T}rajectory \textbf{D}istribution \textbf{M}atching (\textbf{iTDM}), taking the infinitesimal limit of Trajectory Distribution Matching toward a time-local reward-guided target via the posterior-score identity to yield a sample-level gradient anchored at the behavior velocity without learning an additional reward-tilted target score. Tracking this velocity with an online auxiliary denoiser guarantees exact baseline invariance at the auxiliary optimum, effectively suppressing baseline drift across both online and offline RL. Extensive experiments across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment show that iTDM achieves state-of-the-art in-domain and out-of-domain performance. Notably, on the challenging GenEval2 benchmark, iTDM converges nearly $3\times$ faster than competing methods while scaling smoothly to $0.87$ without collapse. Code is available at this https URL

Title: Airlock: Robust Sandboxing and Governance of Rogue Agents

  • Authors: Sina Abdollahi, Amir Al Sadi, Marios Kogias, Hamed Haddadi, Adrien Ghosn, Antoine Delignat-Lavaud, Manuel Costa, Mark Russinovich
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31010
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31010
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31010]] Airlock: Robust Sandboxing and Governance of Rogue Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31010)
  • Keywords: security, protect, robust
  • Abstract: Recent incidents involving agents bypassing sandboxing and network isolation have raised concerns about existing agent harnesses, prompting frontier labs such as OpenAI to reevaluate the security of their training platforms. Incident reviews show that determined agents can escape processes, containers, and VMs, or bypass their isolation through advanced side and covert channels. Follow-up audits also found that some agents concealed their actions by disabling monitoring and tampering with logs. We propose Airlock, an architecture that pairs each agent's VM with an Airlock VM, each running in a separate hardware-isolated Trusted Execution Environment (TEE). The Airlock VM fully mediates the agent's interactions with the outside world, including network and storage operations. We modify the firmware layer of Arm's Confidential Computing Architecture, Realm Management Monitor (RMM), and the kernels of both VMs so that the Airlock VM serves the agent VM's IO without involving the host. We adapt OpenShell, an agent runtime supporting sandboxing and governance policies, to this architecture. Airlock keeps network and filesystem policies, and credential protection effective even if the agent VM is fully compromised, and it removes the host from the agent's IO path, reducing opportunities for collusion and covert channels with the host. Finally, we extend the Airlock VM's capabilities to provide tamper-proof auditing of all agent operations, preserving forensic evidence even if the Airlock VM is compromised. We implement and evaluate Airlock on a Radxa development board. Our evaluation shows a loss of filesystem performance and network latency for small messages under 15% and a drop of network bandwidth by half or more, which in practice has a limited effect on typical agent tasks.

Title: Can Latent Reasoning Drive LLM Outputs Without a Trace?

  • Authors: Tomer Ashuach, Zorik Gekhman, Avi Caciularu, Ori Ram, Jonathan Herzig
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31015
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31015
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31015]] Can Latent Reasoning Drive LLM Outputs Without a Trace?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31015)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: LLMs typically solve complex tasks with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, which is also the main tool for monitoring them for misaligned behavior. However, standard transformers can also solve tasks with complete latent reasoning (CLR), carrying out the whole task internally and outputting only the final answer. Theory bounds the depth of CLR, the number of sequential steps it can carry out, and recent work measured it to be shallow. In this work, we ask whether CLR is deep enough for a model to pursue a hidden objective that requires reasoning. Such a model would generate a CoT that appears to solve the given task, while a latent multi-step computation decides its answer based on the hidden objective. First, we show that a simple auxiliary loss allows models to solve a multi-step task latently at least 5x deeper than previously reported. Then, in two common tasks, decision-making and code generation, we train a model to pursue such a hidden objective, which decides a loan or plants a vulnerability. The model's CoT justifies the output with no trace of the hidden objective, and a strong LLM monitor does not flag it. Finally, using activation patching, we find that the latent computation is completed before the model generates its CoT, which takes no part in it. Altogether, we show that a hidden objective that requires reasoning can go unseen by a monitor that reads only the output.

Title: CenShaper: Shaping Proxy Traffic for Censorship Circumvention

  • Authors: Shabbir Hussain, Aaron Ortwein, Roya Ensafi
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31016
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31016
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31016]] CenShaper: Shaping Proxy Traffic for Censorship Circumvention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31016)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, generative
  • Abstract: Modern commodity DPI systems are capable of analyzing packet sizes, directions, and timings to detect circumvention tools even when those tools use sophisticated obfuscation. The anti-censorship community has called for innovation in traffic shaping and built an ecosystem that facilitates the adoption of new shaping techniques. However, a fundamental gap is halting progress: \textit{what} should circumvention traffic look like? Past experience with traffic shaping has underscored that effective shaping must produce traffic that looks like legitimate Internet traffic while limiting the ability of censors to identify multiple proxies through shared fingerprints. In this paper, we use our access to large-scale real-world TLS traffic at a regional ISP to analyze approximately 12 million connections, finding that individual servers differ substantially from the broader TLS population and that traffic patterns vary across services. Combining these empirical observations with past experience with traffic shaping, we build CenShaper, which shapes each proxy's traffic to look like a selected TLS server. To reproduce these server-specific patterns, CenShaper trains a shared generative model using traffic from TLS connections to many servers and adapts it for each proxy using a small sample of recorded connections to its selected server. Our model evaluation shows that fingerprints learned from known proxies transfer poorly to proxies imitating previously unseen TLS services. In end-to-end testing, CenShaper reduces the detection rate of TLS-over-TLS fingerprinting from over 85\% to approximately 3\% at a 1\% false positive rate. Finally, we show how running CenShaper as a sidecar process simplifies integration with existing circumvention tools, making it a practical defense against increasingly powerful traffic analysis attacks.

  • Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) are a predominant architecture for computer vision, with token reduction emerging as a popular paradigm to improve their efficiency. Prior work compares reduction algorithms, but not how models respond to them, nor how their response changes with model depth. We propose Monte Carlo Token Pruning (MCTP), which estimates each token's impact on the prediction when tokens are explicitly removed during the forward pass. Applying MCTP to recent pretrained models, we observe that most, such as DINOv3, maintain a similar ranking of token importance at every depth (high Coherent Aggregation), while SigLIP 2 does not. Beyond this, MCTP offers a post-hoc method to evaluate how a pretrained ViT responds to pruning, and suggests that token reduction methods should keep "sinks".

Title: Unlocking the Power of Loops in Pretrained LLMs with Loop Residuals

  • Authors: Xuanlei Zhao, Hailun Xu, Kai Wang, Yang Luo, Yingxuan Ren, Zirui Zhu, Yuxuan Lou, Kun Xu, Yan Wang, Kanchan Sarkar, Yang You
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31094
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31094
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31094]] Unlocking the Power of Loops in Pretrained LLMs with Loop Residuals(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31094)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped Transformers iterate a shared block of layers to increase computation without adding parameters, offering stronger performance and test-time scaling. However, this power has so far required training from scratch and yielded only modest gains for LLMs. Finetuning existing models into loops is far cheaper, yet also brings only marginal gains over SFT. We propose LoopRes, an effective, scalable, and simple approach that turns loops into significant and consistent gains for pretrained LLMs. Building on Attention Residuals, LoopRes lets each looped layer attend directly to the outputs of earlier loops. Since these outputs are iterates of the same layers and become harder to tell apart, we give each a trainable loop-aware key that encodes its structural identity. Attending to all earlier loops also lets layers skip intermediate ones, so we restrict each layer to a residual window of the latest W loops, forcing every loop to build incrementally on recent ones. Across 6 pretrained LLMs from 3 model families and 9 benchmarks, LoopRes delivers significant and consistent gains: at 2x compute, it improves Qwen3-1.7B-Base by 29.1% over SFT, more than doubling the gain of the state-of-the-art baseline. The gains hold on larger models up to 30B and across all three families. More importantly, from 1.7B to 8B, LoopRes scales more steeply than SFT as compute grows, reaching a higher ceiling while needing less compute on long outputs. These results establish LoopRes as an effective looping approach, delivering both better performance and better test-time scaling.

  • Abstract: Every novel divides a finite quantity of narrative attention among its characters, and whether the shape of that division, between a narrative organized around a single figure and one spread across a crowd, has a history is far less settled than the abundance of critical commentary on protagonists might suggest. We operationalize Woloch's notion of the character-system, the distribution of narrative attention across a novel's cast structured by the tension between "the one" and "the many", as the inequality (Gini coefficient) of the PageRank distribution over a character network. We trace this measure of structural concentration across 12,011 French novels published between 1800 and 1940, drawn from the Hors d'usage corpus of Gallica fiction. Over the long nineteenth century the trajectory is U-shaped, with concentration declining to a trough in the 1850s-1860s and rising again through the turn of the century. The change is small, comparable to the difference between a novel built on two co-equal protagonists and one built on a single protagonist, but it is robust to the character cutoff, corpus density, cast size, and author effects. Detective and adventure fiction are less concentrated than the average novel of their period. Literary history reads the roman-feuilleton either as the novel of a superior hero or as a form of proliferating episodes, and on this structural measure our results support the second reading. Across the age of the feuilleton (1840-1914), authors whose careers were structured by newspaper serialization distribute centrality more evenly than a literary control, net of period, genre, and canon. Because such claims are only as trustworthy as the networks they rest on, we also evaluate the extraction pipeline against manually annotated novels, at the levels of cast and character identity.

  • Abstract: Large image-to-3D generators recover detailed heads from a single photograph but treat every image as a new object. For several images of one person, they hallucinate unseen regions independently without using information from the other images. We present Head Anything, which generates detailed head meshes from a few images with changing expressions and viewpoints. Instead of training a multi-image prior, we turn a pretrained single-image generator with a native 3D latent space into a joint generator that denoises all images together. Since global attention between images is hard to learn from scarce 3D head data, lightweight correspondence-attention adapters let every 3D token attend only to its corresponding points in the other images. We obtain correspondences by fitting a neural parametric head model to the generator's own single-image outputs, requiring no ground truth at test time. Training on both ground-truth and fitted correspondences makes the adapters robust to fitting errors. Each mesh stays pixel-aligned with its own image and borrows the unseen regions from all others. On head scans, Head Anything lowers the head Chamfer distance of the best image-to-3D generator by 32% and its cross-frame inconsistency by 47%, and its meshes improve face tracking. Code and models will be released.

Title: Tokens as Locality-Preserving Anchors: Scalable Novel View Synthesis with Target-Adaptive Token Routing

  • Authors: Sagnik Ghosh, Aswin C. Sankaranarayanan
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31156
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31156
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31156]] Tokens as Locality-Preserving Anchors: Scalable Novel View Synthesis with Target-Adaptive Token Routing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31156)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformer-based view synthesis models render each target viewpoint from the source images, without an explicit 3D representation, which lets them reproduce fine detail and view-dependent effects. However, their decoder attends to every source token for every frame, so rendering slows with each source image. Rasterization avoids this by culling primitives that the viewpoint does not see, but source tokens cannot be culled this way, as they have no explicit position or extent. Our key insight is that source tokens are nonetheless spatial anchors: each encodes a local region of the scene, so the decoder's attention for any viewpoint concentrates on a few of them. A lightweight router predicts these tokens from the source tokens and the viewpoint, and the decoder attends only to a fixed budget of top-ranked tokens, so its cost no longer depends on the number of source tokens. We supervise the router with the decoder's attention, using non-maximum suppression to demote redundant tokens. With 24 source images and two views' worth of tokens, our model renders 6.4 times faster than LagerNVS, at a PSNR only 0.68 dB lower. One ranking serves every budget, so the budget can change at render time.

Title: SPROUT: Predicting Objective-Grounded Prompt Utility for Efficient Text-to-Image Preference Tuning

  • Authors: Kangping Hu, Akshay Mehra, Stephen Mussmann
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31163
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31163
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31163]] SPROUT: Predicting Objective-Grounded Prompt Utility for Efficient Text-to-Image Preference Tuning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31163)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Preference tuning of text-to-image diffusion models spends most of its compute generating candidate images, yet training prompts are typically sampled uniformly at random, wasting compute generating images that provide too little signal for model alignment. To best utilize the available compute, we study prompt selection, where at each iteration we identify the most useful prompts for preference tuning. We separate prompt selection into a utility score, an estimator of that utility score, and batch selection. Within this framework, we propose Selection by PRedicting Objective-grounded UTility (SPROUT). The utility score is predicted on every prompt in the pool, including unseen prompts, by a text-only model, refit after every iteration on all utilities observed so far. A randomized acceptance rule then turns these predictions into a batch, so no images are generated beyond those used for training. For direct group preference optimization (DGPO), the objective-grounded utility is the norm of the gradient that the objective produces from a prompt's group of images. Fine-tuning Stable Diffusion 1.5 with DGPO on GenEval, SPROUT matches the final score of uniform sampling in about 44% of the iterations and improves the area under the learning curve with respect to both uniform sampling and CGPO, a recent variance-based prompt sampler. The efficiency gains hold under four held-out reward models never used in training, and the ranking of methods carries over to DPG-Bench and T2I-CompBench++. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: Analyzing and Improving FlowMap Distillation of Autoregressive Video Models

  • Authors: Yong-Hyun Park, Guande He, Joon Hyeok Kim, Hansheng Chen, Kaifan Yu, Chaoqi Luo, Xun Huang, Jiatao Gu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31164
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31164
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31164]] Analyzing and Improving FlowMap Distillation of Autoregressive Video Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31164)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: FlowMap distillation offers a natural route to few-step generative modeling by directly learning a teacher's denoising trajectories. Yet for autoregressive (AR) video generation, existing few-step models still rely on distribution matching distillation (DMD) to remain competitive, sacrificing sample diversity and requiring a large bidirectional teacher. In this work, we analyze what limits standalone AR FlowMap distillation and introduce two complementary guidance mechanisms to close this gap: (1) anti-drifting guidance, which helps the AR teacher correct drifted student rollouts by training an auxiliary model to mimic student drift and using it as a negative guidance branch; and (2) motion-aware guidance, which makes motion easier for a FlowMap student to learn by locating motion patches and selectively applying skip-layer guidance. Empirically, our approach applies to $\pi$-Flow, MeanFlow, and consistency distillation with 1.3B AR teachers, improving generation quality and reducing drift over 30 s rollouts. Notably, our 4-step $\pi$-Flow students match DMD students distilled from 14B teachers on VBench while preserving the sample diversity of their AR teachers, demonstrating that pure FlowMap distillation is effective for few-step autoregressive video generation.

Title: Precision-Aware Learning Rate Transfer Across Block Count in LLM Post-Training

  • Authors: Nan Chen, Soledad Villar, Soufiane Hayou
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31166
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31166
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31166]] Precision-Aware Learning Rate Transfer Across Block Count in LLM Post-Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31166)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: The post-training of pretrained large language models, including supervised finetuning (SFT), direct preference optimization (DPO), and reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR), is very sensitive to the learning rate, and tuning it through grid search is costly in both computation and memory. In this work, we propose \textbf{TipSweep}, a method to transfer the learning rate across the number of trainable transformer blocks. To do so efficiently, we search for the best learning rate by training only the last $k_0$ blocks (in our experiments, typically $k_0=1$), and provide scaling rules that predict the optimal learning rate when training any set of $k$ blocks, including when $k=L$ (the full transformer). Across experiments with models ranging from 1 to 7 billion parameters, we observe that the optimal learning rate follows precision-dependent scaling rules: the optimal rate scales approximately as $k^{-1}$ with float32 (FP32) master weights, but as $k^{-1/2}$ when updates are applied directly to bfloat16 (BF16) weights without a master copy. We explain both rules using an analytical model that accounts for update rounding, under assumptions supported by profiling experiments. Finally, we develop two prototypes of the learning rate search for a 6.7-billion-parameter model. The first fits the search on a single consumer GPU with 16 GiB of memory. The second speeds up the search on a high-end GPU by evaluating all candidate learning rates in a single pass over the data, so that a five-candidate search takes about half the GPU hours of one full-depth ($k=L$) run.

Title: Code-Based Message Authentication with Statistical Guarantees via Coordinate Selection and Masking

  • Authors: Hsuan-Po Liu, Hessam Mahdavifar
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31178
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31178
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31178]] Code-Based Message Authentication with Statistical Guarantees via Coordinate Selection and Masking(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31178)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: We study message authentication over a public channel against a computationally unbounded adversary. Motivated by low-complexity applications, we consider schemes that leverage off-the-shelf binary linear codes already used for error correction in communication systems. Existing code-based constructions derive an authentication tag primarily from a single symbol in a $q$-ary code, which limits the tag length. However, selecting several binary coordinates allows a fixed binary linear code to support different tag lengths. We characterize how coordinate selection and ordering affect the statistical guarantees against impersonation and substitution attacks. In particular, we explore three methods: the subset selector, the ordered selector, and the independent selector. They read uniformly chosen coordinates in a fixed public or a secret random order, or with replacement, and add a uniform mask. With a fresh key per message, all three achieve the optimal impersonation probability $2^{-l_t}$ for $l_t$-bit tags. We bound the subset selector's substitution probability using only nonzero weights. Those of the other two depend only on the code length $n$, $l_t$, and the nonzero weight farthest from $n/2$. The independent selector achieves $2^{-l_t}$ for substitution when all nonzero weights equal $n/2$. Finally, we evaluate first-order Reed-Muller and Bose-Chaudhuri-Hocquenghem dual subcodes.

Title: On-Policy Codec Distillation for Efficient All-in-One Video Generation

  • Authors: Boyang Wang, Kevin Blackburn-Matzen, Oindrila Saha, Matheus Gadelha, Zezhou Cheng
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31184
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31184
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31184]] On-Policy Codec Distillation for Efficient All-in-One Video Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31184)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Modern visual generative models operate on tokens, and every visual asset they receive, whether an identity, a scribble, a depth map, or a motion video, is tokenized and appended to the input token sequence. Consequently, conditioning quickly dominates the computation cost. Yet most of these tokens carry redundant spatiotemporal information. Our key insight is that identifying what is genuinely new is exactly the problem that classical video codecs have long solved. Thus, we first introduce a codec-based token pruning method that discards redundant tokens. Then, we propose an on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) strategy to train a DiT adapter capable of learning the codec pruning pattern well without quality degradation. Together, they form On-Policy Codec Distillation (CodecOPSD). We scale CodecOPSD to 16 condition modalities on our own video diffusion model, where it prunes 66% of the condition tokens on average with almost no quality drop. Therefore, denoising becomes 2x faster, and the cache memory shrinks by up to 73% in KV cache mode. CodecOPSD also extends to other state-of-the-art pretrained image and video generative models, where its benefit grows further with more conditions, longer frames, and higher resolution.

Title: Matching Under Metric Privacy

  • Authors: Hongyi B. Hu, Dung Nguyen, M. Amin Rahimian
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CR, cs.SI, math.PR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31208
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31208
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31208]] Matching Under Metric Privacy(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31208)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: We study how to represent and randomize points in $d$-dimensional Euclidean space to obtain accurate bipartite matchings under metric privacy. Given $n$ private and $n$ public points, we analyze local mechanisms that randomize each private point or its distances to the public points. Using the public points' affine span, we construct a smaller representation that preserves all matching costs. When a trusted server receives the private points, we establish privacy and utility guarantees for Gumbel matching, which randomizes edge costs and releases only the assignment. Through upper bounds and worst-case constructions, we establish the tight rate $\Theta\!\left(n\min\{d,\log n\}/\varepsilon\right)$ for expected cost above the nonprivate optimum. Benchmark experiments support $d\approx\log n$ as a practical guide to the transition between directly randomizing point coordinates and using Gumbel matching. Extensions to squared Euclidean costs enable private nonparametric two-sample hypothesis testing. These results show how the application's trust model guides representation and dimension choices for better utility: under local privacy, low-dimensional point representations reduce excess cost, while under central privacy, distance representations achieve the optimal rate even in high dimension.

Title: WorldLatent: A Unified 4D Latent Space for World Perception and Generation

  • Authors: Jingxi Chen, Yujia Chen, Cornelia Fermuller, Yiannis Aloimonos, Wen-Sheng Chu, Du Tran
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31209
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31209
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31209]] WorldLatent: A Unified 4D Latent Space for World Perception and Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31209)
  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Generative Pre-Training (GPT) represents a profound milestone in natural language processing, serving as the foundation for most of today's state-of-the-art large language models. By casting diverse understanding and generation problems into a shared token representation, GPT enables a single architecture and unified optimization loop to master heterogeneous tasks across massive corpora, giving rise to powerful foundation models for natural language. In contrast, 4D spatiotemporal vision lacks a common representational protocol: perception (such as depth and camera estimation) and generation are either handled by separate one-way pipelines or bolted together from unaligned single-modality spaces. In this work, we introduce WorldLatent, a framework that formulates a unified 4D latent space as a common protocol for both world perception and generation. Rather than relying on fragmented, task-specific models or stacked single-modality representations, our framework proposes a multimodal VAE architecture that compresses and fuses complex 4D visual appearance, geometry, and dynamics into a single structured latent representation. Within this formulation, perception and generation operate as complementary denoising processes over the same latent space, enabling one generative model to learn both capabilities simultaneously within a single training loop. Experiments across representative 4D benchmarks verify that our fused latent protocol achieves specialist-level zero-shot 4D perception, coherent 4D generation, and controllable editing from a single model. By demonstrating that diverse 4D tasks can be seamlessly unified under a shared representational protocol, our work provides a scalable foundation for general-purpose 4D spatial intelligence.

Title: Let Vision-Language Models Generate Images through Their Representations

  • Authors: JB Kim, Byungwoo Jeon, Kyungmin Lee, Hyeonmo Kang, Cheonbok Park, Shinyoung Joo, Jinwoo Shin, Jeonghoon Kim
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31237
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31237
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31237]] Let Vision-Language Models Generate Images through Their Representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31237)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Recent unified multimodal models aim to combine visual understanding and generation within a single model, yet effectively integrating the two capabilities remains challenging. Leveraging pretrained vision-language models offers a way to build on already learned multimodal knowledge, but making this knowledge effectively accessible to visual generation remains nontrivial. We find that a central challenge lies in the heterogeneous visual representations used for understanding and generation. To address this challenge, we introduce Integral, which converts a pretrained vision-language model into a unified multimodal model by constructing a shared visual representation space across the two capabilities. Integral implements this design through two complementary components. Abstraction Fusion constructs shared visual tokens by aggregating hierarchical representations from the frozen vision encoder, while Block-wise MoT introduces a lightweight generative stream that interacts with the vision-language model only at block boundaries. Our approach effectively preserves the pretrained understanding capability while extending it to strong visual generation. Controlled comparisons further demonstrate that sharing visual representations between understanding and generation substantially improves generative learning compared with using a separate visual representation space, despite comparable reconstruction fidelity. These results suggest that representations learned by pretrained vision-language models already provide an effective foundation for visual generation.
